\begin{document}

\textbf{Recursive Information--Container Dynamics: A Multiscale Theory
of Adaptive Systems}

\emph{RICD 17.7 --- Spine}

This document, the spine of RICD, holds the core engine: the primitive
objects and axioms, information geometry, recursive containers, feedback
divergence field mechanics, resource dynamics, the core of network and
memory dynamics, adaptive stability and collapse, derived observables,
the fundamental results, the discussion, and the computational
specification. The companion catalog holds every further mechanism, edge
case and audit protocol, each attached to the part of the engine it
extends. Section and item numbers are shared by both documents, so every
cross-reference resolves to one of the two; a reference here to a
section that is not in the spine refers to the catalog. The
resource-cost sum M\_add(t) of §7.3 and the forcing sum F\_total(t) of
§6.4 are extension points: each catalog mechanism supplies its own term,
which is zero wherever that mechanism is not in use. Catalog quantities
can also enter as modifiers with a stated inactive value at which they
drop out: the composition variables of §6.1 (Definition 10.5b.18.14a)
the vulnerability estimate 𝒱\_\{i→j\} of the extraction flux (§8.6a,
inactive value 1), and the anchor capacity A of the point of no return
(§10.3a; §10.5b.13c, inactive value 0).

\textbf{On free parameters.}

Every concrete formula in this document carries one or more coefficients
(α, β, κ, λ, θ, and similar) that are not fixed by the theory and are
not meant to be guessed. They are free parameters with priors, to be fit
to real data through the Bayesian state-space layer once a system is
chosen. Part 15 tags every such coefficient explicitly so that an
implementation knows which quantities are structural (fixed by the
formula) and which are inferred (fit per application).

\hypertarget{part-1.-introduction}{%
\section{Part 1. Introduction}\label{part-1.-introduction}}

\hypertarget{abstract}{%
\subsection{Abstract}\label{abstract}}

Adaptive systems---from single cells to software architectures,
corporations, ecosystems, and nation-states---must continuously acquire,
process, transform, and act upon information while operating under
finite energetic and structural constraints. Existing mathematical
frameworks typically emphasize one aspect of this process, such as
dynamical systems, network topology, control theory, information theory,
or stochastic processes, but rarely provide a unified formalism capable
of describing how information, structure, resources, and feedback
jointly govern adaptive behavior across multiple organizational scales.

This paper introduces Recursive Information--Container Dynamics (RICD),
a general mathematical framework for describing adaptive systems as
recursively embedded information-processing containers. Within this
framework, every adaptive entity is modeled as a bounded container that
receives information from its environment, transforms that information
according to internal organizational constraints, exchanges resources
across its boundaries, and recursively participates within higher-order
containers. Information, energetic cost, structural organization, and
network topology are therefore treated as complementary components of a
single multiscale dynamical system rather than as independent modeling
assumptions.

A central component of the framework is Feedback Divergence Field
Mechanics (FDFM), which formalizes the internal informational dynamics
of an adaptive container. FDFM describes how discrepancies between
declared, operational, and self-modeling informational states evolve
through recursive feedback, generating measurable quantities such as
informational divergence, permeability, integrity, and accumulated
informational stress. By treating FDFM as one layer within a broader
recursive architecture, the framework separates internal informational
dynamics from higher-level structural, energetic, and network processes
while preserving their mathematical coupling.

Rather than postulating collapse as a threshold crossing or externally
imposed state transition, RICD derives adaptive stability, degradation,
and collapse from the evolution of recursive information flow under
finite resource constraints. Stable adaptation emerges when recursive
feedback maintains informational coherence across scales; instability
arises when divergence, structural constraints, energetic depletion, and
recursive memory jointly destabilize the system's attractor landscape.

The framework is intentionally domain-independent. The same mathematical
objects describe biological organisms, software systems, financial
institutions, educational organizations, distributed artificial
intelligence, and other adaptive systems by varying only boundary
definitions, resource mappings, observational operators, and recursive
depth. This universality permits theoretical analysis while
simultaneously supporting computational implementations such as forensic
auditing systems, adaptive collapse trackers, cyber-defense
architectures, organizational diagnostics, and multiscale simulation
engines.

The remainder of this manuscript develops the framework axiomatically.
Primitive mathematical objects are introduced first, followed by the
recursive operators that govern information exchange, energetic
constraints, adaptive networks, and memory. Feedback Divergence Field
Mechanics is then derived as the internal informational layer of the
theory, after which measurable observables, stability conditions, and
collapse dynamics are developed from the primitives and axioms rather
than introduced as independent assumptions.

\emph{\textbf{A note on ``derived,'' used throughout this document at
three distinct strengths.}} ``Structurally derived'' means a consequence
follows from the primitives and axioms plus definitions alone, with no
further choice involved. ``Model-derived'' means a consequence follows
mathematically once a particular default functional form has been
adopted --- the form itself is a choice, even where the qualitative
relationship it encodes is structurally motivated. ``Empirically
fitted'' means a numerical parameter is inferred from data given an
already-chosen functional form. Most of the concrete formulas in Parts
6--11 are model-derived, not structurally derived: they supply this
document's default computational instantiation of a structurally-derived
quantity, not the unique consequence forced by the axioms alone. Where
this document says a quantity ``is derived'' without further
qualification, structurally derived is meant; where a specific formula
is given for that quantity, the formula itself is model-derived unless
stated otherwise, and alternative functional forms sharing the same
qualitative relationship generally remain admissible without
contradicting anything structurally established.

\hypertarget{introduction}{%
\subsection{1. Introduction}\label{introduction}}

Adaptive systems exist because they continuously solve a common problem:
maintaining organized behavior while processing uncertain information in
changing environments. Whether the system is a biological organism
responding to external stimuli, a corporation coordinating thousands of
employees, a software platform managing distributed computation, or a
governmental institution making policy decisions, its long-term
persistence depends upon the recursive interaction between information
processing, structural organization, energetic constraints, and
environmental feedback.

Although numerous mathematical disciplines study portions of this
problem, existing approaches typically isolate one level of
organization. Dynamical systems theory characterizes trajectories in
state space but generally assumes a fixed state representation. Network
science models interactions among agents but often treats the
informational content of those interactions as secondary. Information
theory quantifies uncertainty and communication while remaining largely
agnostic regarding organizational structure. Control theory emphasizes
regulation and stability but generally presumes externally specified
objectives and architectures. Statistical mechanics explains emergent
collective behavior yet does not explicitly distinguish informational
organization from energetic exchange.

Adaptive systems, however, require all of these processes
simultaneously. Information must exist within organizational boundaries;
boundaries constrain information processing; processing consumes
resources; resource availability alters organizational structure;
structural change modifies future information flow; and the resulting
dynamics recursively propagate across multiple organizational scales. No
single mathematical framework presently formalizes these relationships
as different aspects of one recursive process.

Recursive Information--Container Dynamics (RICD) is proposed as a
general mathematical framework intended to address this gap. Rather than
beginning with predefined state variables or application-specific
differential equations, the theory begins by defining the primitive
mathematical objects shared by all adaptive systems. These objects
describe information, containers, boundaries, resources, recursive
composition, feedback, and memory independently of any specific domain.
Observable quantities such as informational divergence, permeability,
rigidity, fragmentation, resilience, and collapse are subsequently
derived from the interactions among these primitive objects rather than
postulated independently.

Within this framework, Feedback Divergence Field Mechanics (FDFM)
occupies a specific theoretical role. FDFM is not synonymous with the
complete theory. Instead, it describes the internal informational
dynamics occurring within individual adaptive containers. It provides a
mathematical description of how official representations, operational
behavior, and recursive self-models evolve through feedback, producing
measurable informational integrity and divergence that influence---but
do not fully determine---the larger multiscale dynamics of the system.

This layered architecture offers two complementary advantages.
Theoretically, it separates informational dynamics from energetic,
structural, and network processes, allowing each to be developed
independently before being mathematically coupled. Practically, it
permits individual layers---particularly FDFM---to be implemented as
standalone computational tools for applications such as organizational
diagnostics, cybersecurity monitoring, software integrity auditing, and
collapse prediction without requiring the full recursive formalism.

The objective of this paper is therefore not merely to construct another
model of adaptive collapse, but to establish a mathematical language
capable of describing adaptive systems across domains and organizational
scales. The remaining sections introduce the primitive mathematical
objects, state the foundational axioms, develop the recursive operators
governing adaptive dynamics, derive FDFM as the internal informational
layer of the framework, and demonstrate how observable quantities
associated with adaptation, resilience, and collapse emerge naturally
from these first principles.

Ontological Scope: Not Analogy, Identity

A distinction stated here plainly, because leaving it implicit has
caused real, repeated misreading: this document does not propose that
informational, organizational, and psychological systems merely resemble
physical systems closely enough to borrow physics-flavored language for
convenience. The claim is stronger and more specific than that, and
worth being explicit about precisely because it sounds, at first
hearing, like the weaker claim. Conceptual containers --- a
relationship, an institution, a belief system, a corporation, a
communication protocol, a running software process --- are not modeled
by analogy to physical law. They are direct instances of it, running on
a different substrate. A human system does not behave as if it obeys the
same organizing principles as a physical one; it obeys them,
continuously, because there is no second, separate law governing
informational organization that merely happens to rhyme with the
physical one. This is not a numbered axiom, because nothing in the
formal mathematics that follows depends on it being true --- every
theorem and derivation in this document holds identically whether this
claim is accepted or treated as a strong analogy instead. It is stated
here as the document's actual founding premise, one level above the
formalism rather than inside it, because it is the reason the
mathematics that follows is offered as physics-adjacent rather than as
organizational metaphor wearing physics notation.

\emph{Two layers: premise and hypothesis.} The claim has two layers, and
they carry different burdens. The first is a premise of physical
instantiation, held axiomatically: every container is physically
instantiated, conceptual containers included, because nothing lies
outside physical law and informational law is predicated on it.
Information is physical, stored in material or energetic states, with a
minimum thermodynamic cost for its erasure (Landauer 1961); a conceptual
container is therefore a pattern of energy and matter held together,
sometimes by material consequence (currency, law) and sometimes by
energetic balance alone, and it is subject to the same physical law as
any other system. Comprehension of the same mathematical content engages
overlapping neural substrate across individuals of very different
backgrounds (Amalric and Dehaene 2016), an instance of a shared
conceptual container running on material substrates. The second layer is
a hypothesis of shared dynamics: that physically instantiated
containers, whatever their substrate, show the same regularities in
phase transitions, boundary conditions, coupling, grid conditions, and
developmental trajectories conditioned on developmental conditions, and
that this document's equations capture those regularities. The premise
is not offered for testing; the hypothesis is, domain by domain, through
adapters and pre-registered tests. Statistical physics supplies the
precedent for the hypothesis: physically different substances near a
phase transition share critical exponents because they share a
universality class, not because they share a material composition.

The clearest available evidence for this identity, rather than mere
resemblance, is that the same formal apparatus --- a persistent
energetic signature, separable from present activity level --- applies
without modification whether the container in question is conceptual or
physical. §2.10 and §2.11 develop this in full; the point worth drawing
out explicitly here is that this apparatus did not need to be built
twice. A corporation's charter persisting as a real, reactivatable
signature after its offices close and its staff disperses; a virus's
genome persisting as pure, inert instruction, requiring wholly foreign
machinery before it can be carried out at all; a bacterial spore or
dormant seed persisting with its own latent machinery intact, sufficient
to resume on its own; a brain's synaptic architecture persisting through
the near-zero-activity interval of ischemic penumbra, the same neurons
capable of resuming their prior state --- these are not four separate
phenomena loosely unified by a shared metaphor of "dormancy." They are
the same mathematical object, B genuinely separable from W(t), appearing
at four different points along a single spectrum that runs from purely
conceptual to purely material with no discontinuity crossed in between.
A framework merely borrowing physics vocabulary for organizational
convenience would have needed different machinery for the corporation
than for the virus. This one did not, which is itself the evidence that
the underlying claim is identity rather than analogy.

\hypertarget{mathematical-architecture-map}{%
\subsection{Mathematical Architecture
Map}\label{mathematical-architecture-map}}

The theory that follows is long, and its length can obscure a
comparatively short mathematical spine. This map is a reading aid, not
new content: everything in it is stated and derived properly in its own
place later in the document, and nothing here should be cited in place
of the actual definition, proposition, or instantiation it summarizes.

\textbf{The spine.} Primitives (Part 2: information state, container,
boundary, resource, memory) → Axioms (Part 3: the foundational
commitments imposed on those primitives) → Geometry and Operators (Parts
4--5: the information manifold, informational distance, and the
recursive aggregation/projection operators) → FDFM (Part 6: the internal
representation dynamics --- official, operational, meta-representation
layers, their pairwise divergences, informational integrity and stress)
→ RICS (Parts 7--10: resource dynamics, network dynamics, memory
dynamics, and adaptive stability/collapse, coupling FDFM's informational
account to energetic, structural, and network processes) → Observable
and Tracker Quantities (Parts 11--12: the derived observables and the
formal results connecting them, then Part 15's computational contract
that turns the whole stack into an implementable tracker).

\textbf{Epistemic status, by kind rather than by location.} Because a
single Part typically mixes several of these, the useful classification
cuts across Part boundaries:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Foundational} --- primitive objects and axioms that are
  stipulated, not derived: the information state (Def. 2.1), the
  container and its structural profile (Def. 2.2), the boundary and
  memory operators (Defs. 2.3, 2.5), and Part 3's axioms. These are
  where RICD's ontology is chosen, not proved.
\item
  \textbf{Derived} --- genuine mathematical consequences of the
  foundational layer, stated as propositions/theorems with explicit
  assumptions: e.g. Theorem 12.1 (existence/uniqueness, local and
  hybrid), Proposition 12.3 (bounded integrity, conditional on G being
  normalized), Proposition 12.7 (recursive consistency, conditional on
  three stated hypotheses).
\item
  \textbf{Model-derived} --- a specific functional form chosen among
  several that would satisfy the same foundational requirement, flagged
  in the text at the point of choice: the exponential form of
  informational integrity I(t) (§6.6), the exponential-decay form of
  d\_A(t) (§10.3), the linear-in-incoherence form of per-level
  fragmentation (§11.7), and the memory kernel's default exponential
  form (§9.3) are all of this kind. Propositions built on top of a
  model-derived form (most notably Proposition 12.8) inherit that form's
  conditional status and say so explicitly.
\item
  \textbf{Fitted} --- parameters with no value fixed by the theory,
  estimated from data per application: α (§6.6), λ (§6.7, §9.3), the
  β\_S/β\_R/β\_F weights and d\_A\^{}max (§10.3), θ\_hull (§10.5a), and
  the many similarly-marked ``fitted'' constants throughout Parts 6--12.
\item
  \textbf{Adapter-supplied} --- quantities the theory deliberately
  leaves to domain judgment rather than resolving itself: the outcome
  space Ω, the commensuration maps σ\_i (§5.2), structural-scale
  measures (§7.5), and everything else catalogued in Part 15's adapter
  contract.
\end{itemize}

Worked mechanism-level elaborations in this document, such as
§4.11--§4.12's directional-geometry scope discussion, support the spine
rather than extend it. The further mechanisms, edge cases and audit
protocols are in the companion Catalog of Mechanisms, which attaches to
the spine through the extension points M\_add(t) (§7.3) and F\_total(t)
(§6.4).

\hypertarget{part-2.-primitive-mathematical-objects}{%
\section{Part 2. Primitive Mathematical
Objects}\label{part-2.-primitive-mathematical-objects}}

Recursive Information--Container Dynamics (RICD) is founded upon a small
collection of primitive mathematical objects from which all subsequent
structures are derived. These primitives are not intended to represent
measurable variables themselves; rather, they define the ontology of
adaptive systems independently of any specific application domain.
Observable quantities such as informational divergence, permeability,
rigidity, adaptive capacity, energetic reserve, and collapse are
introduced only after these foundational objects have been established.

The central design principle is that every adaptive system can be
understood as a recursively organized collection of bounded
information-processing entities interacting across multiple spatial,
temporal, and organizational scales.

\hypertarget{information-states}{%
\subsection{2.1 Information States}\label{information-states}}

The fundamental object of the theory is an information state.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 2.1 (Information State).}}

An information state is represented by a probability measure:

\emph{P} ∈ 𝒫(Ω)

where:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Ω denotes the system's state space, and
\item
  𝒫(Ω) denotes the space of probability measures on that state space.
\end{itemize}

An information state therefore represents not a single condition of the
system, but its entire probabilistic organization. This formulation
accommodates deterministic systems as degenerate probability
distributions while naturally extending to uncertain, stochastic, and
partially observable systems.

Unlike classical state-space models, RICD treats uncertainty as
fundamental rather than incidental. Every adaptive system possesses
incomplete information regarding itself, its environment, or both.
Consequently, probability measures become primitive objects rather than
secondary statistical descriptions.

\hypertarget{containers}{%
\subsection{2.2 Containers}\label{containers}}

Information cannot exist independently of organizational structure.
Before the container itself can be defined, its \emph{admissible
configuration space} must be fixed: for outcome space Ω, let 𝒳(Ω) = 𝒫(Ω)
× ℝ\textsubscript{≥0} denote the space of (information-state,
resource-level) pairs a container built on Ω can occupy. Schemas
attached to a container are defined on histories over 𝒳(Ω), never on the
container itself; fixing this ordering before Definition 2.2 is what
avoids the circularity resolved explicitly below.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 2.2 (Container).}}

A container is a pair:

\emph{C} = (Ω, Ĉ), Ĉ = (B̂\_C, M̂\_C)

consisting of an outcome space Ω and a fixed pair of \emph{generating
schemas}: \emph{B̂\_C} : H\_t → (admissible-exchange specifications on
𝒳(Ω)) is C's boundary-operator schema (Definition 2.3) and \emph{M̂\_C} :
H\_t → 𝒫(Ω) is C's memory-operator schema (Definition 2.5), both maps
defined on the history space H\_t of accumulated past states (§9.1),
never on C itself. The container's \emph{realized state} at time t is
X\_t = (P\_t, R\_t, B\_t, M\_t), where P\_t ∈ 𝒫(Ω) and R\_t ∈
ℝ\textsubscript{≥0} are the informational and resource coordinates
(§2.4), and B\_t = B̂\_C(H\_t), M\_t = M̂\_C(H\_t) (Definition 2.5) are
the schemas' realized values given the trajectory available up to t. It
is X\_t, not C, that evolves; C's schemas B̂\_C and M̂\_C are themselves
fixed, but the values B\_t, M\_t they generate are not, which is what
allows the boundary to be ``a dynamic mathematical object'' (§2.3)
without making the container's own identity a moving target. Where
earlier sections of this document write P(t), R(t), B(t), or M\_t for
``the'' container without repeating a subscript, this denotes the
corresponding component of X\_t for whichever container C is under
discussion.

\textbf{Resolving a definitional circularity.} An earlier formulation of
this document wrote B : C → ∂C and R : C → ℝ\textsubscript{≥0} ---
operators taking the container itself as their argument --- while also
listing B and R among C's own components. That is a self-referential
typing a careful reader notices immediately: C's definition would then
already need to contain the very operators being defined in terms of C.
The fix is the ordering given above, modeled directly on how Definition
2.5 already treats memory: M\_t is not a fresh operator per timestep but
the realized value of a fixed schema M̂\_C applied to history, and B is
now given the identical treatment via B̂\_C. C denotes a container's
fixed identity and pair of generating schemas, not its evolving state;
B̂\_C and M̂\_C take histories as input, never C. R is not a
separately-domained ``measure'' applied to C at all; R\_t is simply the
resource coordinate of the realized state X\_t (§2.4). Where a domain
adapter must derive that coordinate from a raw, non-numeric observation,
it supplies a resource-reading map R̂\_C : (adapter-specific raw domain)
→ ℝ\textsubscript{≥0} (Part 15), again never applied to C itself.

The container constitutes the minimal adaptive entity within the theory.

The term container is deliberately chosen rather than agent or node. A
container is not assumed to possess agency, intentionality, or
cognition. It is simply any bounded system capable of maintaining and
updating information through interaction with its environment.

\hypertarget{boundaries}{%
\subsection{2.3 Boundaries}\label{boundaries}}

Containers are distinguished by their boundaries.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 2.3 (Boundary Operator).}}

A boundary-operator schema:

\emph{B̂\_C} : H\_t → (admissible-exchange specifications on 𝒳(Ω))

generates, at each t, the permissible exchange of information,
resources, and influence between container C and its environment, as a
function of C's accumulated history (Definition 2.2) rather than of C's
identity. Its realized value at time t is written B\_t = B̂\_C(H\_t), or
simply B when the timepoint is unambiguous, paralleling Definition 2.5's
treatment of M\_t. Importantly, the boundary is not merely geometric. It
is an operator that determines admissible interactions. Two containers
occupying identical physical space may possess entirely different
informational boundaries.

Boundary operators therefore become dynamic mathematical objects rather
than fixed spatial partitions: it is B\_t, generated afresh from history
at each t, that evolves, while the generating schema B̂\_C is what
constitutes part of C's fixed identity. Their evolution will later
determine permeability, organizational restructuring, and adaptive
coupling.

\hypertarget{resources}{%
\subsection{2.4 Resources}\label{resources}}

Adaptive computation incurs finite cost.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 2.4 (Resource Coordinate).}}

Each container's realized state X\_t (Definition 2.2) carries a
nonnegative resource coordinate:

\emph{R\_t} ∈ ℝ\textsubscript{≥0}

representing the available capacity for information processing,
structural maintenance, adaptation, and environmental interaction. R is
a state coordinate, not an operator taking C as input; where a domain
adapter must derive R\_t from a raw, non-numeric observation, it
supplies a resource-reading map R̂\_C, instantiated per Part 15.

No assumption is made regarding the particular conservation law
governing the resource.

\hypertarget{memory}{%
\subsection{2.5 Memory}\label{memory}}

Adaptive systems possess history.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 2.5 (Memory Operator).}}

Each container possesses a memory operator:

\emph{M\textsubscript{t}} : \emph{H\textsubscript{t}} → 𝒫(Ω),

where:

\emph{H\textsubscript{t}} = \{\emph{P}(s) : 0 ≤ s ≤ t\}

denotes the complete historical trajectory of the information state.

Unlike Markovian systems, future evolution depends not only upon the
present state, but upon the historical trajectory encoded by Mₜ. Memory
therefore constitutes a primitive mathematical object rather than an
auxiliary delay term.

\hypertarget{environment}{%
\subsection{2.6 Environment}\label{environment}}

No adaptive system exists in isolation.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 2.6 (Environment).}}

The environment of a container Cᵢ is defined as:

\emph{E\textsubscript{i}} = 𝒞 \textbackslash{}
\emph{C\textsubscript{i}},

where 𝒞 denotes the collection of all containers.

The environment therefore consists of every interaction external to the
container's own internal organization.

\hypertarget{recursive-embedding}{%
\subsection{2.7 Recursive Embedding}\label{recursive-embedding}}

Recursive organization is the defining characteristic of complex
adaptive systems.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 2.7 (Recursive Embedding).}}

Given containers C₁, \ldots, Cₙ, there exists an aggregation operator:

\emph{A} : \{\emph{C\textsubscript{1}}, \ldots,
\emph{C\textsubscript{n}}\} → \emph{C}\textsuperscript{*}

mapping collections of lower-level containers into higher-order
containers.

The resulting container C* inherits its own information state, boundary,
resource state, and memory, while simultaneously containing the original
lower-level systems. Recursion therefore generates organizational
hierarchy without requiring distinct mathematical rules at each scale.

\hypertarget{observation}{%
\subsection{2.8 Observation}\label{observation}}

Observable variables are not primitive. They arise through observation.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 2.8 (Observation Operator).}}

An observation operator for container C:

O\_C : 𝒳\_C → 𝒴

maps C's realized states X\_t ∈ 𝒳\_C (Definition 2.2) onto a space 𝒴 of
measurable quantities, Y\_t = O\_C(X\_t). Its domain is the evolving
state, not the container's fixed identity C, consistent with Definition
2.2's distinction between the two.

Different observers may employ different observation operators while
referring to the same underlying adaptive system.

\hypertarget{principle-of-ontological-economy}{%
\subsection{2.9 Principle of Ontological
Economy}\label{principle-of-ontological-economy}}

The framework deliberately minimizes primitive assumptions. Only six
primitive mathematical objects are assumed:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  information states;
\item
  containers;
\item
  boundary operators;
\item
  resource measures;
\item
  memory operators;
\item
  recursive aggregation.
\end{itemize}

2.10 Material and Conceptual Container Architecture

To preserve the frameworks universality claim (Theorem 12.10) with an
honest account of how the boundary operator B is actually sustained in
different domains, containers are partitioned into two structural types.

A material containers boundary is precipitated endogenously, by the
direct physical, chemical, or electrical interaction of its own
component subsystems --- subatomic activity binds the atomic container;
electron sharing forms the molecular boundary; a cell's own metabolic
exchange manufactures and repairs its own membrane. In a material
container, the physical form is not separate from the systems function:
it is a living, self-adapting part of that function, updated
continuously as an inseparable byproduct of ordinary activity rather
than as a distinct, separately-invoked project.

A conceptual container has no such pre-existing physical substrate. Its
boundary is an informational partition, and the container's own
energetic signature exists as a system if and only if its distributed
messengers (φ\_k) are actively exchanging information; if the messengers
stop interacting entirely, the signature collapses, B → ∅, and the
container dissolves (Definition 10.5b.0.7).

The distinction this is built to prevent conflating: a conceptual
containers messengers frequently construct or acquire physical, material
tools --- an office building, a filing cabinet, a server rack --- to
house or facilitate their exchange. That physical tool is not the
container. The activity of a business's employees, owners, and customers
does not cause the building to grow or adapt around them the way a
cell's own activity grows its membrane; the building is a static
resource asset (an element of Ω or R), not a component of the boundary
operator itself. The actual container is the loop of exchange, and the
building can remain standing, completely intact, for decades after the
conceptual container that once occupied it has fully dissolved.

This produces a real, structural asymmetry in tool-upkeep cost, not
merely a difference of vocabulary. A material containers tool upkeep is
folded into and inseparable from its ordinary metabolic function ---
M\_maint (§7.5) is continuous and automatic, with no moment at which the
system separately decides to divert resources toward repairing its own
form as opposed to simply continuing to function. A conceptual
containers tool upkeep is a discrete, separable cost layered on top of
its ordinary relational exchange: updating a charter, renegotiating a
lease, filing a new permit requires messengers to divert deliberate
energetic output specifically toward that end, distinguishable in
principle from the ordinary business of the system exchanging
information with itself. The same underlying resource debt mechanics
(§7.9) govern both, but the two costs have a different observable shape:
material upkeep should track continuously with baseline activity level,
while conceptual tool-updating should appear as discrete, separable
spikes largely uncorrelated with ordinary throughput.

This asymmetry produces a further, real consequence for survival. A
conceptual container can survive the total destruction of its physical
tool, since the tool was never part of the container to begin with: the
energetic signature persists, and the messengers acquire or construct a
replacement. A material container cannot survive the destruction of its
own boundary in the same way, since that boundary is not a tool the
container uses but a component of the container itself; its collapse is
the collapse of the whole system.

Because conceptual containers lack a boundary capable of physically
repelling disruption on its own, human civilizations construct
higher-order conceptual enforcement grids --- legal systems, property
registries, governmental frameworks --- specifically to couple a
conceptual containers symbolic artifacts (a deed, a charter, a permit)
to real material consequences. These artifacts carry no intrinsic
physical force; the enforcement grid supplies it, converting a
messenger's defiance of the conceptual container's own rules into a
genuine material penalty (loss of physical freedom, seizure of assets)
via a downward projection from the grid onto the defying messenger. The
grid is itself entirely conceptual, yet it functions by manufacturing,
deliberately and at real cost, the kind of consequence a material
container's own boundary would enforce automatically.

2.10.1 The Living-Boundary/Inert-Tool Spectrum

The material/conceptual distinction above is the clean limiting case of
a more general axis, one that reappears within material systems
themselves and does not resolve into a strict binary.

The relevant question for any given structure a system depends on is not
simply whether the system is material or conceptual, but whether that
specific structure is a living, self-precipitated boundary ---
inseparable from the system, continuously self-repairing, non-swappable
--- or an inert, acquired tool --- external in origin, static, freely
replaceable without threatening the system underneath.

A hermit crab's own exoskeleton is the living-boundary case: grown,
molted, and repaired as part of the organism's own ongoing physiology,
exactly analogous to a cell's membrane. The shell it scavenges and later
abandons for a larger one is the inert-tool case, structurally identical
to an office building: acquired, static, and freely replaceable, with
the organism underneath surviving its loss completely intact. Depth of
behavioral attachment to a tool does not itself move a structure from
one side of this line to the other: a beaver's dam-building instinct can
be genetically hardwired, essential to survival, and passed down for
millions of years, and the dam remains fully external and replaceable
regardless --- strength of instinctual drive toward a tool-type is an
independent property from whether the tool has become part of the
container.

Between these two poles sits a genuine third position, not a failure of
the distinction but a real structure it needs to accommodate: an
endogenously-produced but only partially fused structure, illustrated by
coral. A coral polyp secretes its own reef structure, unlike a scavenged
shell, and the colony genuinely lives embedded within it --- but the
living boundary proper remains the individual polyp's own tissue,
situated beneath the structure it produces, not fused into it the way a
membrane is fused into a cell. Pruning dead reef leaves the living
polyp's own boundaries untouched and can even benefit the colony,
exactly as replacing an old building leaves a business unharmed; but
destroying enough of the reef compromises the structural support living
polyps actually depend on, crossing from "replace the tool" into "damage
the organism's own boundary conditions." This is a real threshold effect
neither the shell nor the building exhibits at any degree of
destruction.

The axis is therefore better described as a spectrum of structural
dependency rather than a binary: full separability at any degree (shell,
building --- no threshold exists at which their loss becomes the
organism's own collapse), threshold-gated partial dependency (coral ---
separable up to a point, then genuinely fused to survival), and total
fusion at every degree (membrane, exoskeleton --- no separable "tool"
exists to lose in the first place).

2.11 Material Systems With Persistent Energetic Signatures

§2.10 describes conceptual containers constructing boundaries out of
otherwise-inert material. The reverse phenomenon is equally real and
worth naming in its own right: a material system's own energetic
signature --- the schema its messengers would, if active, be organized
around --- can persist through an interval in which those messengers are
not currently active at all, in at least three structurally distinct
ways.

The first is a pure blueprint, requiring wholly foreign machinery to be
enacted at all: a viral genome outside a host cell has no boundary and
no potential messengers of its own whatsoever, and remains fully inert
until it hijacks another organism's entire transcription and translation
apparatus to be read. DNA generally shares this passive, template
character --- it does nothing on its own, requiring RNA polymerase,
ribosomes, and the rest of a cell's machinery to act on it --- though
DNA residing in its own cells nucleus sits closer to the second case
below than the pure viral extreme, since it is already surrounded by
machinery that is, in an ordinary sense, its own.

The second is stable, self-sufficient dormancy: a bacterial spore or a
dormant seed contains both the blueprint and at least some of its own
latent cellular machinery --- ribosomes, organelles, membrane-bound
compartments --- sufficient to resume activity on its own once
conditions permit, without needing to commandeer a foreign cells
apparatus the way a virus does. This is typically a stable, often
extremely long-lived, designed-for state rather than an emergency one.

The third is emergency-edge dormancy of a system's own prior active
state, illustrated by a brain in ischemic penumbra: the same neurons,
the same synaptic architecture that were previously active, persisting
through a near-zero-activity interval imposed by oxygen deprivation,
capable of resuming their own prior function --- not a foreign function
--- if oxygen returns in time, or failing structurally if it does not.
Unlike the viral case, nothing foreign is required to read this schema;
unlike the spore case, this is not a stable, designed-for resting state
but a genuine emergency in which the outcome is undetermined.

What all three reveal, most clearly in the third case, is that a
material container's boundary (B) and its active exchange (W(t)) are
separable quantities capable of coming apart, rather than the boundary
being simply assumed to track activity level at all times. A container's
activity can approach zero while its boundary persists intact and
potentially reactivatable --- a real, distinct state, worth
distinguishing explicitly from both full activity and full dissolution:
B intact, W(t) ≈ 0, with genuine uncertainty as to whether reactivation
or structural failure follows.

2.11.1 Persistent-Signature Influence After Permanent Termination: Four
Continuous Variables

A related but distinct case from the three above, since it involves
permanent termination rather than dormancy: a container's persistent
signature can continue exerting real, corrective influence on another,
still-active container even after the source container has permanently
ceased to exist, with no possibility of reactivation. This is not a
separate mechanism requiring its own name (an authored letter, a book, a
film, a created object, and the memory others hold of a person are all
instances of the same underlying phenomenon), but the strength of that
influence varies continuously along at least four real, separable
variables, rather than there being a categorical distinction between an
ordinary artifact and an especially influential one: (1) targeting
specificity --- diffuse and unaddressed versus deliberately aimed at a
specific, named receiving container; (2) content precision relative to
the receiver's actual, known circumstances at the time of receipt ---
generic content versus content specifically tuned to the receiving
containers particular situation; (3) the receiving container's own state
at the moment of contact --- ordinary, low-salience reception versus a
moment of acute, heightened receptivity, such as one triggered by the
source container's own termination becoming known to the receiver at
that same moment; and (4) relationship value-weighting, reusing V(C,t)
from §10.5b.11b's Sacrificial Lifeboat value channel --- influence
scales with how much the receiving container valued the source,
independent of the other three variables. A source containers final
communication, addressed by name, precisely tuned to the receiver's own
trajectory, delivered at the exact moment of the source's own death
becoming known, from a highly-valued source, sits at an extreme on all
four variables simultaneously --- which is what makes such cases appear
qualitatively different from an ordinary inherited artifact, when the
actual difference is one of degree across these four continuous dials,
not of kind.

All remaining quantities---including informational divergence,
permeability, rigidity, fragmentation, adaptive capacity, resilience,
collapse, and Feedback Divergence Field Mechanics itself---will be
derived from these primitives in subsequent sections.

\hypertarget{part-3.-foundational-axioms}{%
\section{Part 3. Foundational
Axioms}\label{part-3.-foundational-axioms}}

The primitive objects introduced in the previous section define the
mathematical universe of Recursive Information--Container Dynamics
(RICD). The present section specifies the fundamental principles
governing those objects.

\textbf{Axiom 1. Information Requires a Container.}

For every informational state P there exist a container C and a time t
such that P = P\_t, the informational coordinate of C's realized state
X\_t = (P\_t, R\_t, B\_t, M\_t) (Definition 2.2). Write P ∈ C as
shorthand for this membership relation: an information state belongs to
a container by being a coordinate of one of its realized states, never
by being a component of C's fixed identity.

Information does not float freely through the theory; every probability
distribution belongs to some adaptive organization. There is no such
thing as a disembodied adaptive information state.

\textbf{Axiom 2. Every Container Possesses Finite Capacity.}

For every container C and every t, 0 ≤ R\_t \textless{} ∞, where R\_t is
the resource coordinate of C's realized state X\_t (Definition 2.4) ---
a coordinate of the evolving state, not an operator applied to C.

Resource capacity is always finite. Every adaptive decision incurs
opportunity cost.

\textbf{Axiom 3. Information Processing Has Nonzero Cost.}

Let \emph{P\textsubscript{t}} → \emph{P\textsubscript{(t+δt)}} denote an
informational update. Then Cost(\emph{P\textsubscript{t}},
\emph{P\textsubscript{(t+δt)}}) \textgreater{} 0 whenever
\emph{P\textsubscript{t}} ≠ \emph{P\textsubscript{(t+δt)}}.

Information dynamics and energetic dynamics cannot be treated
independently.

\textbf{Axiom 4. Boundaries Regulate Exchange.}

No interaction occurs independently of boundary operators. For every
interaction 𝒥 : Cᵢ → Cⱼ occurring at time t,

𝒥 ∈ Adm(B\_t\^{}(i)) ∩ Adm(B\_t\^{}(j)),

where B\_t\^{}(i) = B̂\_\{C\_i\}(H\_t) is container i's realized boundary
(Definition 2.3) and Adm(B\_t) denotes the set of exchanges admissible
under the specification B\_t: every interaction is admissible under the
realized boundaries of both containers it connects.

Boundary evolution becomes a primary mechanism of adaptation.

\textbf{Axiom 5. Adaptive Systems Are Recursive.}

Every adaptive container may itself become a component of a larger
adaptive container:

\emph{A} : \{\emph{C\textsubscript{1}}, \ldots,
\emph{C\textsubscript{n}}\} → \emph{C}.

The resulting container C satisfies the same axioms as every lower-level
container. One mathematical language describes every organizational
scale.

\textbf{Axiom 6. Memory Influences Future Dynamics.}

Adaptive systems are generally non-Markovian:

\emph{P}(t + δt) = \emph{F}(\emph{H\textsubscript{t}}),

where Hₜ = \{P(s) : 0 ≤ s ≤ t\}. Path dependence becomes fundamental,
not exceptional.

\textbf{Axiom 7. Adaptive Stability Is Dynamic.}

Adaptive systems seek bounded trajectories maintaining organizational
integrity despite continual perturbation, not static equilibrium. Let
X(t) denote the complete state of a container. Adaptive stability
requires:

\emph{X}(t) ∈ 𝒮

for some invariant stability manifold 𝒮, rather than convergence toward
a fixed equilibrium point. Collapse should later be defined as loss of
stable trajectory, not departure from equilibrium.

\textbf{Axiom 8. Observable Quantities Are Emergent.}

For any observable quantity Y, there exists an observation operator O\_C
(Definition 2.8) such that:

Y\_t = O\_C(X\_t),

the observable at time t being a function of the container's realized
state X\_t, not of its fixed identity C.

\textbf{Axiom 9. Health-Seeking Is the Default Orientation.}

Every container's default orientation is toward negentropic alignment
--- accurate self-representation, coupling that exchanges rather than
merely extracts, and the seeking of connection with other containers in
its network --- not toward entropic alignment. Entropic alignment is not
a baseline condition requiring no explanation; it is itself the outcome
requiring explanation, produced either by developmental corruption
(§10.5b.2's genesis-branching) or, alternatively, by structural fault
present from a container's own formation without corruption acting on a
prior healthy state (§10.5b.1.1's Type A). This is the axiom's full
structural content, and it is a foundational commitment of the theory
rather than an empirical claim: it establishes what a container is
oriented toward by default and what an entropic outcome requires
explaining, without asserting how often either pathway actually occurs
in practice.

Axiom 9a, the asymmetry of feedback errors, is stated with the
healthy-entropy account it grounds (Definition 10.5b.1b.3): inaccurate
negentropic feedback is the more dangerous error, which is why a healthy
container's official layer leans slightly entropic.

\emph{\textbf{Empirical default hypothesis, stated separately.}} In the
domains presently motivating RICD, entropic organization is
provisionally modeled as more often arising through corruption of a
prior negentropic trajectory than through entropic structure present at
formation --- across structural, developmental, and conceptual cases
alike, a container corrupted from an original negentropic orientation is
treated as the more typical route to entropic status, with a container
entropic from inception, with no corrupted prior state to point to,
treated as the less common case. This prevalence claim is empirical, not
derivable from Axiom 9 itself, and may be rejected or reversed by
domain-specific evidence without the axiom's structural content being
affected either way; it is a modeling default this document adopts, not
a mathematical consequence of the axiom.

This does not mean entropic containers cease seeking connection. They do
not: the drive toward coupling is retained even where alignment is
corrupted, and what changes is only what that coupling becomes capable
of --- ranging from parasitic (extractive-displacing, §8.6a) to
predatory (destructive, §10.5b.9d) rather than reciprocal. A container
that has stopped seeking connection entirely (§8.6a.6, generalized in
§10.5b.2.7 below) is in a genuine deviation from this axiom's default,
not expressing it; dormancy is a real, sometimes necessary state, but it
is not what containers do by default, at any entropy level.

\emph{Terminology.} Predator and prey, parasite and host, and extraction
are used throughout this document in their ecological sense, the sense
of the Lotka--Volterra predator--prey equations (Lotka 1925; Volterra
1926) and of the host--parasite literature that followed them: names for
structural roles in a flux of resources and disruption between
containers, applicable alike to cells, organisms, populations, firms,
institutions and states, and not descriptions of any one kind of system.

\textbf{The Principle of Recursive Universality.}

Any adaptive system can be represented as a recursively embedded
collection of bounded information-processing containers whose evolution
is governed by finite resources, boundary-mediated exchange, historical
memory, and recursive organization.

\emph{\textbf{Representation failure condition. This principle is a
foundational commitment of the framework, not a result derived within
it; it is a conditional claim, not a tautology,}} and is falsifiable in
the following specific sense: a system is outside RICD's current scope
if no admissible mapping can simultaneously supply all of the following
--- a bounded, information-bearing entity; a state representation P over
some outcome space Ω; a meaningful boundary-mediated exchange relation
with its surroundings; finite or normalizable resource capacity; a
history representation (Definition 2.5's memory operator); and, wherever
multiscale modeling is claimed for that system, a recursion or
aggregation relation connecting its levels (§5.2). A domain need not
resemble any example already given in this document, and boundaries,
resource mappings, observation operators, and recursion depth are all
free for a domain adapter to choose --- but a domain for which no
admissible choice of these can jointly satisfy every item above is a
genuine failure of representation, not merely an unusual application.
Without this condition, ``container'' risks being broad enough that
anything can always be called one, which would make the universality
principle unfalsifiable rather than genuinely general.

\textbf{Collapse-Layer Axioms}

The collapse layer rests on the following axioms, with the definition
and proposition Axiom C1's scope requires. Axioms C3 and C4, which are
stated in terms of verticality and horizontality, are in the catalog
with that account (§10.5b.0); the mechanisms these axioms govern are in
the catalog, §10.5b.15.

\emph{\textbf{Axiom C1 (Truth-Only Reorganization).}}

States of internal agreement are the states with Φ\_t = 0; since Φ\_t ≥
0, each is a minimum of Φ\_t, and where it is interior it is a
stationary point of Φ\_t, to which Proposition 6.4b(i) applies. Where a
container\textquotesingle s exchange is open (Π\_f \textgreater{} 0) and
its grounding is effective and non-cancelling (w\_k ≥ 0, at least one
accurate party with positive weight, the accurate parties agreeing on
the same true signal P*, and their contributions not cancelling, g\_G ≠
0 wherever O\_p(P) ≠ P* among the relevant interior states; Proposition
6.4b shows agreement on P* together with J\_\{O\_p\}ᵀ·∇D ≠ 0 suffices),
the feedback operator ℱ therefore has no unforced fixed point at a false
signal among the interior states of internal agreement, and at each such
state the grounding drift points toward the true signal; a false
internal-agreement configuration cannot remain an unforced stationary
state under the stated grounding conditions; forcing (F\_conf, or an
extractive input per §8.6a) can hold it. This does not rule out
temporary persistence along a trajectory, or other false local minima of
Φ\^{}G\_t. The axiom excludes these false equilibria; it does not claim
that the whole flow converges to the true signal from every starting
point, which would need further conditions (a Lyapunov function for
Φ\^{}G\_t and no competing minima of it). Where exchange is open but
ungrounded, ℱ can settle into internal agreement on a false signal;
where exchange is closed, ℱ holds the container where it is. The axiom
does not claim that every false configuration is excluded for arbitrary
projection operators: an adapter whose O\_o, O\_p and D produce further
local minima must identify them (Part 14).

Epistemic status of ``true underlying signal.'' This is an ontological
quantity within the theory --- what the axiom asserts ℱ's unforced fixed
points converge toward --- not something automatically identified with
whatever the model currently estimates. Its empirical identification
requires an admissible grounding procedure (G(t), §6.3.2) and may itself
remain latent or only estimated, subject to the same identifiability
limits already stated there: an estimate agreed upon by several
apparently independent parties is not thereby confirmed to be the true
signal, only agreed-upon. Stating this explicitly here forecloses a
reading in which the theory smuggles its own desired answer into the
landscape by silently treating ``the model's current best estimate'' and
``the true underlying signal'' as the same thing.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.0.0b (Unmet Restorative Drive).}} Let
r(t) = ‖∇\_g Φ\_t(P(t); P\_m(t), M\_t)‖\_g ≥ 0 be the magnitude of a
container's restorative gradient toward the landscape minimum of §6.4,
the informational target of Axiom C1 as grounded above, and let Π\_f(t)
∈ {[}0,1{]} be its feedback permeability (§6.5). Define the unmet
restorative drive

U(t) = r(t)·(1 − Π\_f(t)),

the portion of the restorative drive the container cannot act on through
exchange with its environment, and its accumulated form 𝒰(t) = ∫₀ᵗ
K(t,s)·U(s) ds, with K the memory kernel of §6.7.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.0.0c (Divergence as the Residue of
Blocked Convergence).}} Let D(t) = D\_op(t) + D\_om(t) + D\_pm(t) be the
container's aggregate layer divergence. Let realized convergence proceed
at a rate proportional to the drive the container can act on,
κ\_c·Π\_f(t)·r(t), and let unmet drive accumulate as divergence pressure
at rate κ\_u·U(t), with κ\_c, κ\_u \textgreater{} 0 fitted. Then

dD/dt = r(t)·{[}κ\_u·(1 − Π\_f(t)) − κ\_c·Π\_f(t){]} + ξ\_D(t),

with ξ\_D(t) collecting the forcing terms already defined elsewhere
(extraction, knocks, demand). Three consequences follow directly. (i)
∂(dD/dt)/∂Π\_f = −r(t)·(κ\_u + κ\_c) \textless{} 0: at fixed drive,
divergence grows fastest as Π\_f → 0, so a closed container is pushed
toward its default as strongly as an open one, and the whole push
becomes divergence. (ii) Setting the bracket to zero gives the threshold
Π* = κ\_u/(κ\_c + κ\_u), which fixes the sign of the restorative
contribution: below Π*, the restorative drive deepens divergence; above
it, the same drive produces convergence, with no change in the drive
itself. The sign of the total derivative depends in addition on ξ\_D(t):
sustained extraction, knocks or demand can hold dD/dt \textgreater{} 0
above Π*, and the threshold fixes the sign of dD/dt itself only where
\textbar ξ\_D(t)\textbar{} \textless{} r(t)·\textbar κ\_u·(1 − Π\_f(t))
− κ\_c·Π\_f(t)\textbar. (iii) Raising permeability, not adding force, is
therefore what converts a high-entropy trajectory: forced percolation
(§10.5b.13.2) and the Lazarus path (§10.5b.18.31) are two routes by
which Π\_f crosses Π*, not separate mechanisms. Divergence in a
high-entropy container is in this sense not an independent force but the
accumulated residue of a convergence it is driven toward and cannot
complete; its continued coupling attempts are the same restorative
drive, still directed at a default it cannot reach (Axiom 9).

\emph{\textbf{Axiom C2 (Runaway Acceleration).}}

As a container approaches a hull-breach crossing (§10.5a) while
remaining in Flip Condition A, its instantaneous divergence pressure
accelerates rather than approaching the threshold at constant or
decelerating rate. This is stated as a falsifiable claim: it predicts
that d(D\_op)/dt and dS/dt should show a measurable, characteristic
upward inflection within a fitted pre-breach window {[}t\_breach −
w\_C2, t\_breach{]}, w\_C2 fitted or domain-defined, distinguishable
from a null model in which divergence approaches the threshold as an
undifferentiated random walk. Fixing w\_C2 in advance, rather than
choosing it after inspecting the data, is what keeps this a genuine
prediction rather than a pattern any analyst could locate
retrospectively in a sufficiently flexible window.

\emph{\textbf{Corollary C2.1 (Continued Oscillation Through Collapse).}}
Axiom C2's acceleration is not a claim that oscillation itself ceases
once the threshold region is entered --- a real, separate claim this
document had left implicit. A container past the point Axiom C2
describes continues to oscillate, per §10.5b.9's own coherence and
momentum dynamics, for the full remaining duration of its approach to
and passage through terminal lock: σ\_p(t) and σ\_o(t) (§10.5b.1's
trailing-window variances) should remain elevated and measurably nonzero
throughout the pre-breach window, not decay toward a smooth,
non-oscillating trajectory as the container is propelled toward
collapse. The propulsion Axiom C2 describes and the oscillation §10.5b.1
already tracks are not alternatives --- a collapsing container is
propelled by its own accumulating momentum while continuing to waver,
the way a falling object continues vibrating from its own structural
stress on the way down rather than falling in smooth, rigid silence. An
adapter observing oscillation decay to near-zero immediately before a
confirmed hull breach should treat that pattern as evidence against
ordinary Flip Condition A dynamics, not as confirmation of them.

\emph{\textbf{Axiom C5 (Bidirectional Closure --- the Horseshoe
Principle).}}

A container's feedback operator ℱ (§6.4) tracks truth only when its rate
of adaptive updating is genuinely responsive to real divergence pressure
--- moving more when divergence is high, settling when divergence is
low. A container whose rate of updating is decoupled from divergence
pressure is in a closed configuration in the sense of Axiom C1,
regardless of which direction that decoupling runs: a container pinned
near zero velocity regardless of divergence (rigid closure) and a
container pinned near maximal velocity regardless of divergence
(compulsive closure) are the same failure --- both require continuous
external or self-generated forcing to sustain, and both decay toward
genuine, divergence-responsive tracking once that forcing is withdrawn.

\hypertarget{part-4.-information-geometry}{%
\section{Part 4. Information
Geometry}\label{part-4.-information-geometry}}

The primitive object introduced in Part 2 was the information state,
represented as a probability measure over a state space. The present
section equips the collection of information states with mathematical
structure.

Rather than representing adaptive systems by vectors in Euclidean state
space, Recursive Information--Container Dynamics models adaptive
organization as evolution on a manifold of probability distributions.
This choice reflects the fact that adaptive systems rarely possess
complete certainty regarding either their internal organization or their
environment. Their dynamics therefore consist not only of changes in
state, but of changes in belief, uncertainty, expectation, and
informational organization.

\hypertarget{information-manifold}{%
\subsection{4.1 Information Manifold}\label{information-manifold}}

Let Ω denote the measurable state space associated with a container, and
𝒫(Ω) the space of probability measures on Ω.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 4.1 (Information Manifold).}}

An information manifold is a differentiable manifold ℳ = (𝒫(Ω), g),
where g is a Riemannian metric defined on the tangent space of
probability distributions.

\textbf{Scope condition.}

𝒫(Ω) does not automatically carry a differentiable structure for
arbitrary Ω. Definition 4.1 applies under one of the following
restrictions, which must be specified per application:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Ω is finite and 𝒫(Ω) is restricted to distributions with full or fixed
  support, so that 𝒫(Ω) is realized as an open simplex of dimension Ω −
  1 --- a finite-dimensional differentiable manifold in the ordinary
  sense. (For countably infinite Ω the full-support simplex is
  infinite-dimensional, and this condition does not apply; a countably
  infinite Ω is admitted only through the parametric-family condition
  below, or through an explicitly infinite-dimensional treatment, which
  this document does not supply);
\item
  the container's admissible states are restricted to a
  finite-dimensional parametric family \{P\_θ : θ ∈ Θ ⊆ ℝⁿ\} (e.g., an
  exponential family), so that ℳ is the parameter manifold Θ pulled back
  through θ ↦ P\_θ, with g the induced Fisher information metric; or
\item
  Ω is infinite-dimensional and 𝒫(Ω) is treated as a Fréchet manifold,
  in which case the differentiability, completeness, and curvature
  constructions of §4.4--§4.6 require the additional analytic hypotheses
  standard to that setting (e.g., a fixed reference measure and an L² or
  Orlicz-type tangent space) and are not assumed to hold without further
  argument.
\end{itemize}

Every subsequent construction in this section --- velocity,
acceleration, curvature, and geodesics --- presupposes one of these
restrictions has been fixed for the container in question. Each point on
the manifold corresponds to an entire informational organization rather
than to a single deterministic configuration. Movement on the manifold
represents learning, adaptation, forgetting, reorganization, or
environmental change.

\hypertarget{informational-distance}{%
\subsection{4.2 Informational Distance}\label{informational-distance}}

To compare informational organizations, the manifold requires a notion
of distance. Rather than prescribing one universal metric, Recursive
Information--Container Dynamics introduces an abstract informational
distance functional.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 4.2 (Informational Distance Functional).}}

An informational distance is a mapping D : 𝒫(Ω) × 𝒫(Ω) → ℝ≥0 satisfying:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Non-negativity: D(P, Q) ≥ 0.
\item
  Identity: D(P, P) = 0.
\item
  Continuity: small perturbations in probability distributions produce
  correspondingly small changes in informational distance.
\item
  Monotonicity under information loss: coarse-graining or irreversible
  compression cannot increase distinguishability.
\end{itemize}

Depending upon the application, suitable choices include Fisher--Rao
geodesic distance, Jensen--Shannon divergence, Wasserstein distance
(provided the coarse-graining map and underlying metric structure
satisfy the corresponding contraction conditions --- Wasserstein
distance does not carry the same unconditional
data-processing/coarse-graining property as the standard divergences
automatically, and this qualification is stated here rather than left
implied), Hellinger distance, total variation distance, and symmetrized
Kullback--Leibler divergence.

\textbf{Terminology: divergence versus metric.} Definition 4.2's
``informational distance functional'' is deliberately broader than a
true metric, and admits functionals of both kinds; the two should not be
called by the same name where the distinction matters (as it does in
Proposition 12.7's identity-separating hypothesis). Precisely:
(unsymmetrized) Kullback--Leibler divergence is neither symmetric nor
triangle-inequality-satisfying and is accordingly a divergence, not a
metric; its symmetrized form used here (D\_op, D\_om, D\_pm throughout
Part 6) restores symmetry but not the triangle inequality, so it remains
a divergence in the technical sense even though ``symmetrized'' suggests
otherwise. Jensen--Shannon divergence is itself not a metric, but its
square root, √JS, is a true metric (satisfies the triangle inequality);
where JS is used as D directly rather than as √JS, it should be treated
as a divergence, not a metric. Total variation distance and Wasserstein
distance are both true metrics in the ordinary sense (symmetric,
triangle inequality, zero iff equal), subject for Wasserstein to the
underlying ground metric being itself a genuine metric. Fisher--Rao
distance is a true (geodesic) metric, being the Riemannian distance
induced by the Fisher information metric g. Hellinger distance is also a
true metric. Where this document or an application needs the triangle
inequality (Proposition 12.7's identity-separating condition already
only requires D(P,Q)=0 iff P=Q, which every functional above satisfies,
but a use requiring the full triangle inequality does not follow
automatically from Definition 4.2's admissibility criteria alone), the
choice of D must be restricted to one of the true-metric options above,
stated explicitly by the application rather than assumed.

\textbf{Relationship between D and g.}

These choices are not interchangeable once the manifold structure of
§4.1 is put to use. The scalar quantities of Part 6 (pairwise
divergence, informational integrity, informational stress) require only
an admissible D in the sense of Definition 4.2, and any of the six
listed functionals may be used there. The Riemannian constructions of
§4.4--§4.6 (velocity norm, covariant acceleration, curvature,
geodesics), by contrast, are defined with respect to the specific metric
g of Definition 4.1, and only Fisher--Rao distance is the geodesic
distance generated by that metric on a statistical manifold; the induced
local quadratic approximation of Fisher--Rao (equivalently, one-quarter
the squared Hellinger distance in the appropriate limit) is the metric g
used throughout §4.4--§4.6 unless otherwise stated. Wasserstein distance
corresponds to a different Riemannian structure (the Otto metric on the
space of measures) with its own, distinct geodesics and curvature, and
total variation and symmetrized KL are not themselves Riemannian
distances at all --- KL is not symmetric or a metric, and TV is not
induced by a Riemannian metric in the relevant sense. An application
choosing Wasserstein or TV as its working D must either restrict Part
4's differential-geometric machinery to the Fisher--Rao case for a
separate purpose, or explicitly substitute the Otto geometry (with its
own geodesic and curvature definitions) throughout §4.4--§4.6; the
theory does not yield curvature or geodesics under TV or plain KL. This
constraint should be stated explicitly in any application of the
framework, since it is easy to read Definition 4.2 as offering six
freely interchangeable options when in fact only one is compatible with
the differential geometry built on top of it.

\hypertarget{information-velocity}{%
\subsection{4.3 Information Velocity}\label{information-velocity}}

Adaptive systems do not merely occupy informational states. They move
through informational space.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 4.3 (Information Velocity).}}

Let \emph{P}(t) be a trajectory on the information manifold. The
instantaneous information velocity is:

\emph{v}(t) = d\emph{P}/dt.

Its norm, ‖\emph{v}(t)‖\_g, measures the instantaneous rate of
informational reorganization.

High informational velocity may represent rapid learning, crisis
response, organizational restructuring, software patch deployment, or
emergency adaptation. Low informational velocity represents
informational stability. High velocity is neither inherently beneficial
nor detrimental; its effect depends upon subsequent resource
availability and structural organization.

\hypertarget{information-acceleration}{%
\subsection{4.4 Information
Acceleration}\label{information-acceleration}}

Adaptive systems may themselves change the rate at which they
reorganize.

Notational convention, fixed for the remainder of Part 4. Every
covariant operation below is written with respect to a single
connection, denoted ∇\^{}\{(g\_t)\}, induced by whichever metric is in
force for the container at time t: the static g of Definition 4.1 in the
ordinary case, or the history-dependent g\_\{C,t\} of Definition 4.8
(§4.10) wherever that section's scope condition is satisfied.
∇\^{}\{(g)\} --- the static-metric case --- is the special case of
∇\^{}\{(g\_t)\} in which g\_t is constant in t; no separate notation is
introduced for it. Where the text below writes ∇\^{}\{(g\_t)\}, this is
one fixed convention applied uniformly, not a per-equation choice
between two possible connections left to the reader. A further
specification is required, and stated here explicitly rather than left
implicit: ∇\^{}\{(g\_t)\} denotes the Levi-Civita connection of g\_t
treated as frozen at each instant t --- the ordinary metric-compatible,
torsion-free connection determined by g\_t alone at that instant --- not
a connection on some further extended, spacetime-style bundle that would
additionally incorporate ∂g\_t/∂t's own contribution into the
parallel-transport rule itself. This choice is not free of consequence:
a genuinely spacetime-style connection accounting for the metric's own
time-variation would in general add extra terms to a(t) beyond what the
frozen-instant Levi-Civita connection produces, the same way a literally
time-dependent metric in general relativity picks up additional
Christoffel-symbol contributions a frozen-slice treatment omits. The
frozen-instant reading is adopted as the default specifically because it
keeps a(t)'s computation at each t dependent only on g\_t's own value at
that t, not on g\_t's own history of change, which matches how every
other Part 4 construction already treats g\_t (evaluated per instant,
with all history-dependence routed through M\_t per §4.10, not through
the connection's own definition). An adapter requiring the
spacetime-style alternative for a specific application must state so
explicitly and supply the resulting correction term; the default
computational instantiation throughout this document uses the
frozen-instant connection exclusively.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 4.4 (Information Acceleration).}}

Information acceleration is \emph{a}(t) = ∇\^{}\{(g\_t)\}\_t
\emph{v}(t), the covariant derivative of the velocity field under the
connection fixed above.

Acceleration measures changes in adaptive responsiveness rather than
changes in state alone.

\hypertarget{information-curvature}{%
\subsection{4.5 Information Curvature}\label{information-curvature}}

Adaptive landscapes possess geometry. Some informational regions are
smooth. Others contain ridges, valleys, or unstable transition surfaces.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 4.5 (Information Curvature).}}

The curvature tensor associated with the information manifold, denoted
𝓡\_ℳ (distinguished here from the resource measure R of §2.4),
characterizes local geometric distortion under the connection
∇\^{}\{(g\_t)\} fixed above §4.4. Regions of high curvature correspond
to informational organizations in which small perturbations may produce
disproportionately large adaptive consequences.

Examples include tipping points, institutional crises, software
instability, ecological thresholds, and cascading failures. Curvature
therefore provides a geometric precursor to collapse before collapse
itself is formally defined.

\hypertarget{information-geodesics}{%
\subsection{4.6 Information Geodesics}\label{information-geodesics}}

Adaptive systems generally attempt to reorganize efficiently.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 4.6 (Information Geodesics).}}

A geodesic γ(t) is a trajectory satisfying ∇\^{}\{(g\_t)\}\_γ̇ γ̇ = 0
under the same fixed connection. Geodesics represent locally minimal
informational reorganization subject to the manifold geometry.

Under the history-dependent metric of §4.10, these are geodesics of the
instantaneous connection, its autoparallels. The length-minimizing
reading holds for a fixed metric; under a time-varying metric, the
metric's own variation enters the variational problem, and a minimizing
interpretation requires a separately derived time-dependent formulation.

Geodesics should not be interpreted as optimal behavior. Rather, they
describe informational trajectories requiring minimal intrinsic
restructuring. External constraints introduced later may prevent systems
from following geodesic paths.

\hypertarget{information-fields}{%
\subsection{4.7 Information Fields}\label{information-fields}}

Individual containers rarely evolve independently. The information state
therefore becomes a field over the collection of containers.

Let 𝒞 = \{C\_i\} denote the recursive collection of containers. Define
P\_i(t) as the informational state associated with container C\_i. The
resulting assignment C\_i ↦ P\_i(t) constitutes an information field
distributed across the recursive architecture. Local informational
change therefore possesses both spatial and recursive structure. This
observation motivates the later development of Feedback Divergence Field
Mechanics.

\hypertarget{information-coherence}{%
\subsection{4.8 Information Coherence}\label{information-coherence}}

Not every collection of informational states forms a coherent
organization.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 4.7 (Information Coherence).}}

Given a collection of containers \{C\_1, \ldots, C\_n\}, define a
coherence functional:

𝒞\_info = Γ\_coh(\emph{P}₁, \ldots, \emph{P}ₙ), where 0 ≤ 𝒞\_info ≤ 1.

High coherence indicates mutually compatible informational organization
across containers; low coherence indicates fragmentation or
contradiction. The precise form of Γ\_coh is intentionally left
unspecified at this stage and will later be constrained by the recursive
coupling operators developed in subsequent sections.

\hypertarget{why-geometry-comes-before-dynamics}{%
\subsection{4.9 Why Geometry Comes Before
Dynamics}\label{why-geometry-comes-before-dynamics}}

A common approach in adaptive modeling is to begin with differential
equations and subsequently define quantities such as divergence or
stability. Recursive Information--Container Dynamics adopts the opposite
approach: the geometry of informational organization determines which
trajectories, divergences, and transformations are mathematically
meaningful. Only after the manifold structure has been established ---
and, per §4.1--§4.2, only within the scope conditions and single-metric
commitment specified there --- can the dynamical equations governing
adaptation be derived.

\hypertarget{history-dependent-metric-and-time-varying-geometry}{%
\subsection{4.10 History-Dependent Metric and Time-Varying
Geometry}\label{history-dependent-metric-and-time-varying-geometry}}

The constructions of §4.1--§4.6 fix a single metric g and build
velocity, acceleration, curvature, and geodesics on top of it. But a
container's accumulated history can genuinely change the geometry it
experiences going forward: a system that has survived a given kind of
disturbance before does not experience a repetition of that disturbance
the same way a naive system would. This is not a claim about
trajectories moving differently through a fixed landscape --- it is a
claim that the landscape itself reshapes as a function of what has
already happened.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 4.8 (History-Dependent Metric).}}

Let M\_\{C,t\} denote container C's own memory operator value at time t
(Definition 2.5, instantiated concretely in §9.3). The history-dependent
metric is container-specific, not a single metric shared globally across
the manifold:

g\_\{C,t\} = g(M\_\{C,t\}),

a Riemannian metric on 𝒫(Ω) that is itself a functional of accumulated
history rather than fixed once and for all, evaluated per container
rather than once for the whole space. Where context makes the container
unambiguous, the subscript is abbreviated to g\_t as elsewhere in this
document; the full form is given here to remove any reading of g\_t as a
single global metric applied identically to every container. All
constructions in §4.3--§4.6 --- velocity norms, covariant acceleration,
curvature, and geodesics --- generalize by replacing the static g with
g\_t throughout, provided g\_t remains, at each fixed t, a valid
Riemannian metric satisfying the scope conditions of §4.1.

This is a substantive strengthening, not a notational one. Under a
static metric, the covariant derivative ∇\_t in Definition 4.4 depends
on t only through the trajectory P(t) itself; under g\_t, the connection
coefficients (Christoffel symbols) generating ∇\_t are themselves
time-dependent, since they are built from a metric that is changing. Two
containers occupying identical instantaneous informational states P can
therefore have different acceleration, different curvature, and
different geodesics at that same state, if their histories M\_t differ
--- which is precisely the behavior needed to support the claim,
developed further in §9.10 and §10.5b, that a container's identity is
constituted by the continuity of its history-dependent organization
rather than by any single instantaneous configuration.

\textbf{Scope condition.}

Definition 4.8 requires that g(M\_t) remain positive-definite and smooth
in P for every fixed t along the trajectory under consideration; the map
M\_t ↦ g(M\_t) is otherwise unconstrained by the theory and must be
specified per application (a default is given where the memory kernel is
instantiated, §9.3). Where this scope condition is not separately
verified, the constructions of §4.3--§4.6 should be read as applying to
the static-metric case only, with g\_t retained as a directional,
qualitative refinement rather than a load-bearing quantitative one.

This resolves what would otherwise be a genuine tension in the
framework: §10.5b's account of gradual regime transition --- a
container's internal composition shifting toward healthier organization
over a long history of sustained steadying, or conversely toward
entrenchment over a long history of unaddressed extraction --- requires
exactly this kind of history-dependent reshaping of what counts as a
small versus large informational move. A container with a long history
of successful correction and a container with a long history of
unaddressed divergence should not be judged by the same geometric
yardstick even when momentarily in the same state, and g\_t is what
allows the theory to say so formally rather than narratively.

\hypertarget{structural-grain}{%
\subsection{4.11 Structural Grain}\label{structural-grain}}

\emph{\textbf{Reader's note.}} §4.11--§4.12 are scope-control and
mechanism-justification material, not part of the mathematical spine
(see the Mathematical Architecture Map, Part 1): they explain why a
narrow directional construction was built instead of a fuller
directional geometry, and under what conditions the fuller version would
be worth building. A reader tracking only the core theory may skip to
Part 5 and return here if §10.5b.7's tipping-momentum formulas raise the
question of directionality.
