layer, however, makes more than one kind of claim about the state: a
college states a price and a completion rate, a bank states its capital
and its asset quality, a hospital states its prices and its outcomes.
Each claim has its own realization in the operational layer, in its own
units. Comparing the claims one by one keeps Definition 6.1's
requirement literal (each official reading is checked against the
operational reading of the same thing), and it preserves the information
of which claim diverged. Some claims are also stated as a range rather
than a point (an operating band, a tolerance, a figure whose exact value
depends on how an unreported quantity is counted), so the official side
of a claim may be a set of distributions rather than one. This section
adds both to the Spine. It changes no existing definition when a
container makes one point claim.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 6.3.4 (Claim Dimensions).}}

A container's official and operational layers are read along n\_cl ≥ 1
claim dimensions, indexed q = 1, \ldots, n\_cl. For each dimension q
there is a finite outcome space Ω\_q (so that 𝒫(Ω\_q) is a compact
simplex; an adapter needing an infinite space states the topology in
which its claim sets are compact and its distance continuous), an
operational projection O\_p\^{}(q) : 𝒫(Ω) → 𝒫(Ω\_q), and an official
claim given in one of two forms:

(i) a point claim, an official projection O\_o\^{}(q) : 𝒫(Ω) → 𝒫(Ω\_q),
with P\_o\^{}(q) = O\_o\^{}(q)(P); or

(ii) a set claim, a set-valued official projection whose value
𝒞\_o\^{}(q)(P) ⊆ 𝒫(Ω\_q) is non-empty, closed and convex for every P:
the set of distributions the official layer's claim is consistent with.

A point claim is the set claim 𝒞\_o\^{}(q)(P) = \{P\_o\^{}(q)\}.
P\_p\^{}(q) = O\_p\^{}(q)(P) is the realization of the claim in the
operational layer. All projections are deterministic functions of the
same underlying state P, with the regularity Definition 6.1 states (for
a set claim, the set varies continuously with P in the Hausdorff sense).
Where the meta-representation layer is read along dimension q, its
reading is a distribution P\_m\^{}(q)(t) on Ω\_q, an aggregate over the
layer's own population as in Definition 6.1, sourced independently of
the official and operational readings, and referring to the same time
step, the same container and the same quantity as the official and
operational readings of dimension q; a reading that is supplied but does
not match in these respects does not count as a meta reading of
dimension q. The dimensions are adapter-defined (§15.2); the theory
requires only that within one dimension every reading lands in the same
space, so that each official reading is compared with the operational
reading of the same quantity. {[}Derived from Definition 6.1, applied
per dimension; the set form is Model-derived{]}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 6.3.4a (Dimensional Divergence and
Availability).}}

With D the distance of Definition 6.2 (by default symmetrized, smoothed
Jensen--Shannon divergence, base 2) on 𝒫(Ω\_q), the distance from a set
claim 𝒞 to a distribution Q is

D(𝒞, Q) = min\_\{R ∈ 𝒞\} D(R, Q),

the minimum attained because 𝒞 is closed in the compact simplex and D is
continuous. For a point claim it is D(P\_o\^{}(q), Q). The dimensional
divergences are

D\_op\^{}(q)(t) = D( 𝒞\_o\^{}(q), P\_p\^{}(q) ), D\_om\^{}(q)(t) = D(
𝒞\_o\^{}(q), P\_m\^{}(q) ), D\_pm\^{}(q)(t) = D( P\_p\^{}(q),
P\_m\^{}(q) ),

with D\_om\^{}(q) and D\_pm\^{}(q) defined only where P\_m\^{}(q)(t) is
available. Let ι\_q(t) ∈ \{0, 1\} record whether dimension q can be
computed for the container at t (the official claim and P\_p\^{}(q)(t)
both supplied and passing the adapter's pre-specified validity rules),
with the reason recorded whenever ι\_q(t) = 0, and define the available
set and the metered set

K\_cl(t) = \{ q : ι\_q(t) = 1 \}, K\_m(t) = \{ q ∈ K\_cl(t) :
P\_m\^{}(q)(t) supplied by route (i) or (ii) of §6.4 \}.

The meta channel is available, ι\_m(t) = 1 in Definition 6.3.3, exactly
when K\_m(t) is non-empty. Since K\_m(t) ⊆ K\_cl(t), an empty claim set
means the meta channel is unavailable, whatever meta data exist.
{[}Derived for the set distance; Model-derived for the availability
rule{]}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 6.3.4b (Aggregate Divergence).}}

Where K\_cl(t) is non-empty, the official--operational divergence is the
maximum over available dimensions,

D\_op(t) = max\_\{q ∈ K\_cl(t)\} D\_op\^{}(q)(t).

Where in addition K\_m(t) is non-empty (three-channel mode), the meta
divergences, and the official--operational divergence restricted to the
metered dimensions, are

D\_om(t) = max\_\{q ∈ K\_m(t)\} D\_om\^{}(q)(t), D\_pm(t) = max\_\{q ∈
K\_m(t)\} D\_pm\^{}(q)(t), D\_op\^{}m(t) = max\_\{q ∈ K\_m(t)\}
D\_op\^{}(q)(t).

Where K\_m(t) is empty, D\_om(t) and D\_pm(t) are not computed and
Definition 6.3.3's two-channel read-out applies: D\_msg(t) = D\_max(t) =
D\_op(t), labeled two-channel. Where K\_cl(t) is empty, D\_op(t) is not
computed and nothing is defaulted in its place; the container is
reported as unscored at t, with the reason, whatever meta data exist. In
three-channel mode, D\_msg and D\_max of Definition 6.3.3 are formed
from D\_op, D\_om and D\_pm, and every value is labeled with K\_cl(t)
and K\_m(t); where K\_m(t) is smaller than K\_cl(t), the dimensions
outside K\_m(t) are in two-channel status and are labeled so. The
dimension attaining each maximum is recorded: q*\_op(t) ∈ K\_cl(t) for
D\_op, and q*\_om(t), q*\_pm(t) ∈ K\_m(t) for D\_om and D\_pm (the
smallest index among ties), and every dimensional value is reported with
the aggregate. Terminology: a dimension is in two-channel status at t
when it is available but not metered (q ∈ K\_cl(t) ∖ K\_m(t)); a
container is in two-channel mode at t when no dimension is metered
(K\_m(t) empty), which is Definition 6.3.3's two-channel mode.
{[}Model-derived: the maximum is chosen over a weighted mean because it
needs no fitted weights and lets one dimension's divergence register
fully when the others are quiet (Proposition 6.3.4f); it matches the
maximum summary D\_max already used across channels{]}

Notation (nested dimensions). An adapter may group several elementary
dimensions under one claim, for reporting: a claim whose realization is
checked against several outside measures, for example. The Spine's
dimensions are always the elementary ones, and a group's value is the
maximum over its members; since max\_g max\_\{q ∈ g\} = max\_q over the
union of the groups, grouping changes reporting and nothing else.
Outputs identify both the group and the elementary dimension attaining
each maximum.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 6.3.4c (Reduction to One Claim).}}

Suppose n\_cl = 1, ι\_1(t) = 1, Ω\_1 = Ω under the identity
identification, the claim is a point claim, and O\_o\^{}(1), O\_p\^{}(1)
and P\_m\^{}(1) coincide with Definition 6.1's O\_o, O\_p and P\_m. Then
D\_op, D\_om and D\_pm equal their single-claim values of Definition
6.2, ι\_m(t) is as in Definition 6.3.3, and every quantity of Part 6 is
identical to its single-claim value. {[}Derived{]}

Proof. K\_cl(t) = \{1\}, and the maximum over a single element is that
element. With a point claim, D(𝒞\_o\^{}(1), Q) = D(P\_o\^{}(1), Q), and
under the stated identifications P\_o\^{}(1) = P\_o, P\_p\^{}(1) = P\_p
and P\_m\^{}(1) = P\_m, so each dimensional divergence is the
single-claim divergence of Definition 6.2. K\_m(t) is non-empty exactly
when P\_m is supplied by route (i) or (ii), which is Definition 6.3.3's
condition. ∎

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 6.3.4d (Boundedness and the Relaxed Triangle
Inequality).}}

Suppose D has upper bound D̄ and relaxed triangle constant ρ\_Δ
(Definition 6.3.3a) on every 𝒫(Ω\_q) in use (an adapter verifies the
constant for each dimension it includes rather than carrying one
established for another space; for Jensen--Shannon divergence ρ\_Δ = 2
on every finite space), and write diam(𝒞) = max\_\{R, R′ ∈ 𝒞\} D(R, R′)
for the spread of a set claim (zero for a point claim). Then, for every
t:

(a) each computed aggregate lies in {[}0, D̄{]};

(b) in three-channel mode, D\_op\^{}m(t) ≤ ρ\_Δ · ( D\_om(t) + D\_pm(t)
) and D\_om(t) ≤ ρ\_Δ · ( D\_op\^{}m(t) + D\_pm(t) );

(c) in three-channel mode, D\_pm(t) ≤ ρ\_Δ · D\_op\^{}m(t) + ρ\_Δ² · (
D\_om(t) + max\_\{q ∈ K\_m(t)\} diam(𝒞\_o\^{}(q)) ), which reduces to
D\_pm(t) ≤ ρ\_Δ · ( D\_op\^{}m(t) + D\_om(t) ) on dimensions whose
claims are all points.

Proposition 6.3.3b uses only the first inequality of (b), so it holds
for the aggregates on the metered dimensions, with the same ρ\_Δ and D̄.
{[}Derived{]}

Proof. (a) Each dimensional value is a minimum or a value of D, so it
lies in {[}0, D̄{]}, and so does a maximum of finitely many of them. (b)
Fix q ∈ K\_m(t) and write 𝒞 = 𝒞\_o\^{}(q), P\_p = P\_p\^{}(q), P\_m =
P\_m\^{}(q). Let R\_m ∈ 𝒞 attain D(𝒞, P\_m). Then D(𝒞, P\_p) ≤ D(R\_m,
P\_p) ≤ ρ\_Δ · ( D(R\_m, P\_m) + D(P\_m, P\_p) ) = ρ\_Δ · ( D\_om\^{}(q)
+ D\_pm\^{}(q) ). Taking q to be the metered dimension attaining
D\_op\^{}m(t), and using D\_om\^{}(q) ≤ D\_om and D\_pm\^{}(q) ≤ D\_pm
because both maxima run over the same set K\_m(t), gives the first
inequality. The second is the same argument with the roles of P\_p and
P\_m exchanged, choosing R\_p ∈ 𝒞 attaining D(𝒞, P\_p). (c) Let R\_p and
R\_m attain D(𝒞, P\_p) and D(𝒞, P\_m). Applying the relaxed triangle
inequality twice gives, for each q ∈ K\_m(t), the dimension-specific
bound D\_pm\^{}(q) = D(P\_p, P\_m) ≤ ρ\_Δ · ( D(P\_p, R\_p) + D(R\_p,
P\_m) ) ≤ ρ\_Δ · D\_op\^{}(q) + ρ\_Δ² · ( D(R\_p, R\_m) + D(R\_m, P\_m)
) ≤ ρ\_Δ · D\_op\^{}(q) + ρ\_Δ² · ( diam(𝒞\_o\^{}(q)) + D\_om\^{}(q) ).
Each term on the right is at most its maximum over K\_m(t), so taking
the maximum of the left side over q ∈ K\_m(t) gives the bound. For a
point claim R\_p = R\_m = P\_o\^{}(q) and Definition 6.3.3a applies
once. ∎

Remark (why the spread term is needed, and why the bounds use the
metered set). For a set claim, the operational and meta readings can
both lie inside the claimed set and still differ from each other: with
the claim the band of two-point distributions with mass between 0.6 and
0.8 on 1, an operational reading of 0.6 and a meta reading of 0.8 give
D\_op = D\_om = 0 and D\_pm ≈ 0.035 under base-2 Jensen--Shannon
divergence. Part (c)'s spread term accounts for exactly this. The bounds
in (b) and (c) are stated for D\_op\^{}m, the official--operational
divergence on the metered dimensions; the full D\_op can be attained on
a dimension with no meta reading, where no inequality tying it to D\_om
and D\_pm is available, which is why the unmetered dimensions are
labeled two-channel.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 6.3.4e (Regularity of the Aggregate).}}

Suppose each operational projection and each point claim's official
projection is continuously differentiable, and take D to be
Jensen--Shannon divergence. Set claims fall into two cases. (i) A set
claim that does not depend on P: its distance function is locally
Lipschitz and Clarke regular wherever its arguments are interior, by the
convexity argument below. (ii) A set claim that moves with P: Hausdorff
continuity of the set does not supply regularity, so local Lipschitz
continuity and Clarke regularity of the composed distance function P ↦
D(𝒞\_o\^{}(q)(P), Q) on the interior are an adapter-specific hypothesis,
verified by the adapter for each such claim (as §15.2.1 does for its
completion band, Proposition 15.2.12(f)). On the region of states at
which every reading entering the active dimensions lies in the interior
of its simplex (every probability strictly between 0 and 1), each
dimensional divergence is locally Lipschitz and Clarke regular, and each
aggregate is locally Lipschitz and Clarke regular, with

∂D\_ij(P) = conv( ∪ \{ ∂D\_ij\^{}(q)(P) : q active, D\_ij\^{}(q)(P) =
D\_ij(P) \} ).

At states where an active reading lies on the boundary of its simplex,
the divergence need not be locally Lipschitz (for two-point
distributions its derivative is unbounded as a probability approaches 0
or 1), and the update of §6.4 uses Definition 6.1's derivative-free
provision, or Definition 6.2's smoothing applied with a fixed ε
\textgreater{} 0, which keeps every reading in the interior.
{[}Derived{]}

Proof. On the interior of the simplex, Jensen--Shannon divergence is
continuously differentiable and jointly convex in its two arguments (it
is an f-divergence, D\_f(P ‖ Q) = Σ Q·f(P/Q), with the convex generator
f(u) = ½{[}u·log₂(2u/(1 + u)) + log₂(2/(1 + u)){]}, and every
f-divergence with convex f is jointly convex: Csiszár 1967; Liese and
Vajda, Convex Statistical Distances, 1987). For a point claim the
dimensional divergence is a composition of a C¹ function with C¹ maps,
hence C¹ and so locally Lipschitz and regular. For a set claim that does
not depend on P, Q ↦ min\_\{R ∈ 𝒞\} D(R, Q) over a closed convex set is
convex in Q (a partial minimization of a jointly convex function over a
convex set), and a convex function is locally Lipschitz and regular on
the interior of its domain; composed with a C¹ map it remains regular
(Clarke, Optimization and Nonsmooth Analysis, 1983, Theorem 2.3.10). For
a set claim that moves with P, regularity is the stated hypothesis. A
pointwise maximum of finitely many locally Lipschitz regular functions
is locally Lipschitz and regular, with Clarke subdifferential the convex
hull of the active subdifferentials (Clarke 1983, Proposition 2.3.12).
At a boundary point the derivative of H\_2(p) = −p log₂ p − (1 − p)
log₂(1 − p) is unbounded, which is the failure stated. ∎

Remark (what the regularity result does and does not give). It says the
potential of §6.4 has a well-defined generalized gradient where its
arguments are interior. It does not say that a step along an arbitrary
element of that subdifferential decreases the potential: in a nonsmooth,
nonconvex setting a projected subgradient step is a procedure with its
own convergence conditions, not a guaranteed descent. Where those
conditions cannot be checked, the adapter declares the derivative-free
update. Separately, a tracker that uses the dimensional divergences only
as computed features, with no gradient taken through them, needs none of
this.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 6.3.4f (One Dimension Can Raise the Alarm
Alone).}}

For every threshold θ ∈ {[}0, D̄{]}, D\_op(t) ≥ θ if and only if
D\_op\^{}(q)(t) ≥ θ for some available q, and likewise for D\_om and
D\_pm over the metered set. No weighted mean with every weight below 1
has this property: for weights w\_q with Σ\_q w\_q = 1 and max\_q w\_q
\textless{} 1, there are dimensional values with one D\^{}(q) ≥ θ
\textgreater{} 0 and the weighted mean below θ. This establishes that
the maximum preserves single-dimension threshold crossings; it does not
establish that the maximum is better than every alternative for every
purpose. {[}Derived{]}

Proof. The first statement is the definition of a maximum. For the
second, take a dimension q with weight w\_q \textless{} 1, set its value
to θ and all others to 0; the weighted mean is w\_q · θ \textless{} θ. ∎

Remark (comparing a container with itself over time). Because each
aggregate is taken over the dimensions available at each step, a change
in K\_cl(t) or K\_m(t) can move an aggregate without any change in the
container: a dimension that becomes available can raise the maximum. A
trend, slope or change in an aggregate is therefore computed only over
steps sharing the same available set, and a step at which the set
changes starts a new segment. This rule concerns aggregate series only;
each dimensional series D\_ij\^{}(q)(t), and any quantity an adapter
derives from one dimension's inputs, follows its own comparability rule,
set by that dimension's inputs. {[}Derived from Definition 6.3.4b{]}

Remark (why not one combined outcome space). The dimensions could
instead be combined into a single space Ω\_1 × ⋯ × Ω\_n\_cl with one
divergence. Jensen--Shannon divergence does not decompose into a sum or
a maximum over the factors of a product space, so that single value
would not show which claim had diverged, which the separate reporting of
Definition 6.3.4b and the adapters' mixed-signal readings (§15.2.1)
depend on. {[}Derived{]}

\hypertarget{feedback-operator}{%
\subsection{6.4 Feedback Operator}\label{feedback-operator}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 6.3 (Feedback Operator).}}

Define the feedback operator ℱ : P → P, incorporating environmental
observations, recursive memory, internal prediction, and resource
constraints, aimed at reducing expected future informational error. ℱ
need not decrease divergence at every update --- temporary increases may
occur during learning, organizational restructuring, crisis response, or
innovation.

\textbf{Computational instantiation (concrete default for ℱ).}

Learning from experience is not a straight-line correction toward lower
divergence. Real containers oscillate --- including sustained periods
moving in the wrong direction --- and a nontrivial fraction never reach
low-divergence organization at all. This behavior is captured by
treating ℱ not as a deterministic update rule but as a stochastic
gradient flow on a divergence potential, additively forced by every
control-shaped and influence-shaped term this document defines
(§10.5b.15's F\_conf, F\_boom, F\_demand, and every later addition,
collected here as F\_total(t) = Σ F\_i(t)):

F\_total(t) is likewise an extension point: the catalog's forcing terms
(F\_conf, F\_boom, F\_demand and the rest) enter it additively when
their mechanisms are in use, and are otherwise zero.

d\emph{P}/dt = −γ\_ℱ · Π\_f(t) · grad\_\{g\_\{C,t\}\}Φ\^{}G\_t(P; P\_m,
M) + F\_total(t) + ξ(t),

where Φ\_t(P; P\_m, M) : 𝒫(Ω) → ℝ≥0 is a divergence potential --- given
explicitly, using Definition 6.2's already-established divergence
function D and the projection operators O\_o, O\_p (§6.1) applied to the
candidate state P:

Φ\_t(P; P\_m, M) = D(O\_o(P), O\_p(P)) + D(O\_o(P), P\_m) + D(O\_p(P),
P\_m),

i.e.~D\_op + D\_om + D\_pm evaluated at P, with P\_m held as the
independently-tracked contemporaneous state per the conditioning note
below rather than derived from P. This requires no additional modeling
choice beyond what Definition 6.2 already fixes: an implementer with
O\_o, O\_p, and D already specified (as any working adapter must have)
can evaluate Φ\_t and its gradient directly. The regime structure
invoked by §10.5b is not claimed as an intrinsic multi-basin property of
this landscape. Since D ≥ 0 with equality only for identical arguments,
Φ\_t(P; P\_m, M) ≥ 0, attaining 0 exactly at states where O\_o(P) =
O\_p(P) = P\_m: the low-entropy baseline is Φ\_t's global minimum. The
three-term form does not, however, fix a count or topology of further
local minima for arbitrary O\_o, O\_p, and P\_m --- in the degenerate
case O\_o = O\_p = identity, Φ\_t(P; P\_m, M) = 2·D(P, P\_m), whose
unique minimizer is P = P\_m. The high- and medium-entropy regimes are
accordingly regions held away from that minimum by forcing ---
F\_total(t), including F\_conf and extraction input (§8.6a) --- as
§10.5b.1 states, and §10.5b's regime classification identifies which
forcing-maintained region a trajectory occupies, not a basin Φ\_t
produces on its own; whether a particular adapter's O\_o, O\_p, and D
generate additional local minima is an adapter-level question (Part 14).
γ\_ℱ \textgreater{} 0 is a fitted learning-rate parameter; and ξ(t) is
stochastic forcing representing environmental shocks, resource strain,
or ordinary noisy experience that does not resolve cleanly. The explicit
conditioning on P\_m and M is not decorative notation: P\_m (§6.1) is an
emergent aggregate over the meta-representation layer's own population,
not a function of P the way P\_o and P\_p are, so at each timestep
P\_m(t) must be supplied through one of three explicitly distinct
routes, not collapsed into the single ambiguous label ``observed or
latent'': directly observed, where the adapter has real, independent
data for P\_m(t) at each step; latent but estimated, where P\_m(t) is
not directly observed but is produced by a specified state-space model
with its own stated likelihood or transition structure, not merely
asserted to exist; or unavailable, in which case any machinery
downstream of P\_m(t) is indeterminate for that container until one of
the first two routes is supplied, except that quantities entering only
through Messaging Divergence are computed in two-channel mode
(Definition 6.3.3), from D\_op alone, with the bounds of Proposition
6.3.3b where its hypotheses apply, and the reduced potential of
Proposition 6.3.3c; a fixed placeholder substituted for P\_m(t) is not a
route at all and leaves the container in two-channel mode (Proposition
6.3.3d). ``Latent'' alone, without specifying which of the second or
third case applies, means only ``somehow supplied,'' which is not an
adequate implementation contract given how load-bearing P\_m(t) is here.
In no case is P\_m(t) derived from P(t) itself --- an implementer
deriving P\_m from P at this step would silently undo §6.1's
independence patch regardless of which of the three routes otherwise
applies. grad\_\{g\_\{C,t\}\} is the gradient taken with respect to
container C's own history-dependent metric g\_\{C,t\} = g(M\_\{C,t\})
(§4.10), not the ordinary coordinate gradient: this is what actually
propagates Part 4's claim --- that two containers occupying identical P
can evolve differently because their histories differ --- into the
equation governing P's own evolution. An ordinary coordinate gradient
here would make that claim true of curvature and geodesics but not of
the container's actual dynamics, silently disconnecting the two.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 6.4a (Grounding Term and the Grounded
Potential).}} Φ\_t measures disagreement among a container's own three
representations, and §6.3.2 shows that all three can agree while all
being wrong. The flow above therefore descends a grounded potential that
adds the container's divergence from what accurate external parties
report about it:

Γ\_t(P) = λ\_G·Π\_f(t)·Σ\_k w\_k(t)·A\_acc(k,C,t)·D(O\_p(P), P\_k(t)),
Φ\^{}G\_t(P; P\_m, M) = Φ\_t(P; P\_m, M) + Γ\_t(P),

with the sum over external parties k coupled to C that hold an
independent representation P\_k(t) of C's state (§6.3.2; the external
feedback channel of §10.5b.18.9), w\_k(t) ≥ 0 their normalized coupling
weights, A\_acc(k,C,t) the accuracy of k's access (§10.5b.18.9.1), and
λ\_G \textgreater{} 0 fitted. Γ\_t is the dynamical counterpart of
grounding G(t): it is zero exactly where the operational representation
agrees with every accurate external report, and it enters only through
open exchange, since it is scaled by Π\_f(t). Where no external party is
coupled, Γ\_t ≡ 0 and Φ\^{}G\_t = Φ\_t.

In §6.4\textquotesingle s flow, dP/dt = −γ\_ℱ·Π\_f(t)·grad Φ\^{}G\_t +
F\_total + ξ, the feedback permeability Π\_f multiplies the whole
gradient. With J\_\{O\_p\}(P) the Jacobian of O\_p at P, write the
grounding gradient

g\_G(P) = Σ\_k w\_k(t)·A\_acc(k,C,t)·J\_\{O\_p\}(P)ᵀ·∇D(O\_p(P),
P\_k(t)).

\textbf{Proposition 6.4b (Grounded Convergence).} Assume λ\_G
\textgreater{} 0, that every accurate party\textquotesingle s report is
compared through the same distance D, and that D(·, Q) is differentiable
with nonzero gradient away from Q, and that the projection preserves it:
J\_\{O\_p\}(P)ᵀ·∇D(O\_p(P), Q) ≠ 0 whenever O\_p(P) ≠ Q, that is, the
gradient is not orthogonal to the column space of the Jacobian. (A
full-rank Jacobian is not enough when O\_p maps into a space of higher
dimension than P\textquotesingle s; a square invertible Jacobian is.)
(i) Open and grounded: if Π\_f \textgreater{} 0 and g\_G(P) ≠ 0 at a
state P that is an interior minimizer of Φ\_t with O\_p(P) ≠ P*, that
state is not an unforced fixed point. With w\_k ≥ 0 and at least one
accurate party (A\_acc \textgreater{} 0) carrying positive weight,
g\_G(P) ≠ 0 holds in particular whenever every party with A\_acc
\textgreater{} 0 reports the true signal, P\_k = P*: their gradient
terms are then aligned, g\_G = a·J\_\{O\_p\}ᵀ·∇D(O\_p(P), P*) with a =
Σ\_k w\_k·A\_acc \textgreater{} 0, and at that state the distance to the
true signal is strictly decreasing, d/dt D(O\_p(P), P*) =
−γ\_ℱ·Π\_f·λ\_G·Π\_f·a·‖J\_\{O\_p\}ᵀ·∇D(O\_p(P), P*)‖² \textless{} 0.
This is a local result at the specified state: once the trajectory
leaves the internal-agreement minimizer, grad Φ\_t no longer vanishes
and can oppose the grounding term, so it is not a proof that the
distance decreases along the whole trajectory; it can fail when accurate
parties report different signals and their pulls cancel. (ii) Open but
ungrounded: if Π\_f \textgreater{} 0 and no coupled party has accurate
access, Γ\_t ≡ 0 and the unforced flow descends Φ\_t alone; it can
settle at any state of internal agreement, Type B confused alignment
included. (iii) Closed: if Π\_f = 0, the unforced deterministic flow is
zero, so every state is stationary: a closed container does not converge
at all, toward truth or toward internal agreement, and moves only under
forcing and noise. Permeability enters twice, once in the flow and once
inside Γ\_t (Definition 6.4a), so the grounding drift scales as Π\_f².
This is intended: permeability gates both whether outside reports enter
the container\textquotesingle s potential at all and how freely the
container moves along it, so a partly closed container is doubly slow to
respond to outside correction. Proof: (iii) is immediate from the factor
Π\_f; (ii) from Γ\_t ≡ 0; for (i), Φ\^{}G\_t = Φ\_t + Γ\_t (Definition
6.4a), so grad Φ\^{}G\_t = grad Φ\_t + grad Γ\_t; at an interior
minimizer of the differentiable Φ\_t, grad Φ\_t = 0, leaving grad
Φ\^{}G\_t = grad Γ\_t, which by Definition 6.4a\textquotesingle s Γ\_t =
λ\_G·Π\_f·Σ\_k w\_k·A\_acc·D(O\_p(P), P\_k) and the chain rule is
λ\_G·Π\_f·g\_G. ∎

\emph{\textbf{Simplex preservation.}} P is a probability distribution
over Ω; raw additive noise ξ(t) can push it outside the simplex 𝒫(Ω)
(P\_i ≥ 0, Σ\_i P\_i = 1) with no correction otherwise stated. Following
the same convention already used for §8.5's network propagation, the
computational default is discrete projection: after each discretized
update,

P(t+Δt) ← Proj\_\{Δ\^{}m\}{[} P(t) −
Δt·γ\_ℱ·Π\_f(t)·grad\_\{g\_\{C,t\}\}Φ\^{}G\_t(P; P\_m, M) +
Δt·F\_total(t) + ξ\_t {]},

projecting the unconstrained update back onto the simplex at every step
by the Euclidean projection Proj\_Δ(x) = argmin\_\{y ∈ Δ\} ‖y − x‖, the
single rule used here and in §8.5 (clipping negative components and then
renormalizing is not used, since it generally returns a different
point), rather than leaving an implementer to independently decide what
ξ(t) means for a distribution-valued state. This is the same pragmatic
convention §8.5 already uses, applied consistently rather than
introducing a second, heavier construction (tangent-space-constrained
diffusion) that nothing else in this document requires.

\emph{Noise specification.} The default law of ξ is ξ\_t =
σ\_ξ·√Δt·Π\_T·ζ\_t, with ζ\_t independent standard Gaussian vectors
across timesteps, σ\_ξ ≥ 0 fitted, and Π\_T the projection onto the
tangent space of the simplex (components summing to zero), so that the
continuous limit is a stochastic differential equation with diffusion
σ\_ξ·Π\_T and the discrete update is its Euler--Maruyama step before
projection. Departures from the default (temporally correlated noise,
state-dependent variance, heavy-tailed shocks) are adapter-declared
choices, not implicit ones, because escape from a local region of Φ\^{}G
depends on them: with independent Gaussian noise the expected escape
time over a barrier of height ΔΦ grows roughly as exp(2ΔΦ/σ\_ξ²) (the
Kramers--Arrhenius scaling), and correlated or heavy-tailed noise
changes that scaling. F\_total(t) is likewise required to lie in the
tangent space of the simplex; forcing supplied with a nonzero component
sum is projected by Π\_T before use. With both conditions, the
continuous equation is a well-defined flow on the simplex's interior,
and the projection handles only contact with its boundary.

\emph{\textbf{What landing exactly on the boundary means, and what it
does not.}} A component reaching exactly zero under this projection is
not always the same event, and the two cases should not be conflated.
Where a component's own continuous dynamics genuinely converge toward
zero --- the underlying discrepancy or divergence they represent is
actually being resolved --- the projection landing there reflects a
real, substantive correction: the state rests at the boundary because
the disturbance it measures has genuinely gone to zero. Where instead a
discretization step or noise term ξ(t) overshoots past zero on a
component whose true continuous-time dynamics would have approached the
boundary asymptotically without fully reaching it, or would have stayed
strictly positive, clipping to zero is a numerical safeguard against
step-size artifacts, not evidence that the underlying disturbance has
actually resolved. Both are real uses of the same projection operator,
and an implementation or interpretation should not treat every instance
of landing on the boundary as proof of successful correction; only
sustained, non-overshoot convergence recorded across successive steps
supports that reading, exactly the same discipline already applied
elsewhere in this document to distinguishing a single instantaneous
measurement from a confirmed, sustained state. The drift term pulls P
downhill toward lower divergence at a rate scaled by current
permeability --- correction happens fast when Π\_f is high and is nearly
absent when Π\_f is low, even when the discrepancy driving it is large.
The noise term is what makes the resulting trajectory genuinely
non-monotonic rather than merely slow: whether a container ever escapes
a shallow local region of Φ to reach a deeper one depends on whether
ξ(t) is large enough, for long enough, to carry it over the intervening
barrier --- the same qualitative phenomenon as a ball in a bumpy
landscape needing a large enough kick to leave a local dip. This is why
bounded oscillation between entropy regimes (§10.5b) is the expected
behavior for a nontrivial fraction of real containers, not a failure of
the model.

This instantiation also gives Axiom C1 (§10.5b.15) a concrete mechanism
rather than an assertion: a false-signal configuration is a local valley
of Φ that the trajectory is held within only because something external
is actively forcing it to remain there --- a confrontation-forcing term
or an extractive input (§8.6a). Withdraw that forcing and the trajectory
is no longer held in that valley, so ordinary gradient descent resumes
toward the grounded minimum of Φ\^{}G\_t (Definition 6.4a), which
coincides with the true signal where the container's exchange with
accurate external parties is open (Proposition 6.4b), without any
additional mechanism being required.

\hypertarget{feedback-permeability}{%
\subsection{6.5 Feedback Permeability}\label{feedback-permeability}}

Not every informational discrepancy successfully modifies the underlying
information state. Some discrepancies are ignored, suppressed,
forgotten, or prevented from propagating.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 6.4 (Permeability Operator).}}

Define Π\_f : D\_ij ↦ {[}0,1{]}. For any informational discrepancy, Π\_f
denotes the proportion successfully transmitted into adaptive updating.
Π\_f = 1 represents complete informational openness; Π\_f = 0
corresponds to complete informational closure.

Permeability is not a probability of communication. It is an operator
governing how much informational discrepancy actually influences
subsequent adaptation.

\textbf{Computational instantiation (concrete default for Π\_f).}

Given observable correction-and-response data for the container, define
a raw permeability score

r\_f(t) = {[}corrective actions(t) / max(error signals(t), ε\_E){]} −
{[}response lag(t) / τ\_ref{]}, ε\_E \textgreater{} 0 fitted (guards the
ratio against division by zero when error signals(t) = 0, rather than
leaving it undefined), τ\_ref a fitted or domain-specified reference lag
normalizing the second term into the same dimensionless scale as the
first --- the raw form (dimensionless ratio minus a time-valued lag) was
dimensionally inconsistent and is corrected here

\emph{Status.} r\_f is a proxy estimate of permeability, not a direct
measurement of it: a high ratio of corrective actions to error signals
can also reflect many actions, repeated ineffective actions, or few
signals. Where possible it is calibrated against independently observed
transmission of error into implemented change.

(lag measured in the container's natural time unit, e.g.~years to policy
change), then map it into the required {[}0,1{]} codomain via a logistic
squashing function,

\emph{Π}\_f(t) = 1 / (1 + exp(−κ\_Π · r\_f(t))),

with κ\_Π a fitted scale parameter (Part 15). This is domain-general:
``corrective actions,'' ``error signals,'' and ``response lag'' are
placeholders the adapter fills in per container (audit recommendations
implemented / issued / time-to-implement, for the justice-system case),
but the aggregation formula itself does not change across domains.

\hypertarget{informational-integrity}{%
\subsection{6.6 Informational Integrity}\label{informational-integrity}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 6.5.}}

Let I = G(D\_op, D\_om, D\_pm), where G : ℝ³≥0 → {[}0,1{]} is a
decreasing functional satisfying I = 1 only when all pairwise
divergences vanish.

Informational integrity summarizes the internal coherence of the
representation system. Alignment becomes one particular manifestation of
informational integrity rather than an independent primitive.

\textbf{Computational instantiation (concrete default for G).}

\emph{I}(t) = exp{[}−α·(D\_op(t) + D\_om(t) + D\_pm(t)){]}, α
\textgreater{} 0 fitted.

This satisfies Definition 6.5 exactly: I ∈ (0,1{]} always, I = 1 iff all
three pairwise divergences vanish, and I is strictly decreasing in each
divergence --- so Proposition 12.3 (Bounded Informational Integrity)
holds by construction rather than by separate proof for this
instantiation. This is RICD's three-channel generalization of a single
collapsed messaging-divergence term: rather than combining O/P/μ
mismatch into one scalar before combining it with stress and feedback,
this formula keeps the three pairwise divergences distinguishable, which
is a genuine strength of RICD's representation (§6.3's interpretation of
D\_op, D\_om, D\_pm as diagnostically different failure modes) worth
preserving rather than flattening.

\hypertarget{informational-stress}{%
\subsection{6.7 Informational Stress}\label{informational-stress}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 6.6.}}

Informational stress is a path-dependent functional:

\emph{S}(t) = ∫₀ᵗ K(t, s) (1 − Π\_f(s)) D(s) ds,

where K(t, s) is a memory kernel.

Stress accumulates whenever informational discrepancies persist while
adaptive feedback remains partially blocked. This naturally incorporates
technical debt, institutional denial, chronic inflammation, unresolved
software defects, and bureaucratic drift without ad hoc latent
variables.

\textbf{Computational instantiation (concrete default for K, with a
recursive update).}

Take K(t,s) = λ·exp{[}−λ(t−s){]}, λ \textgreater{} 0 fitted (the
standard exponential-memory choice, and the only one of §9.3's
candidates admitting an O(1)-per-step update). With D(t) = D\_op(t) +
D\_om(t) + D\_pm(t) and a fixed simulation step Δt, this reduces to the
recursion

\emph{S}(t+Δt) = S(t)·exp(−λΔt) + (1 − exp(−λΔt))·(1 − Π\_f(t))·D(t),

under the convention that q(t) = (1 − Π\_f(t))·D(t) is piecewise
constant over each step. This is exact under that convention, not an
Euler approximation: the coefficient is (1 − exp(−λΔt)), not the
first-order approximation λΔt, which the two coincide with only for Δt
small relative to 1/λ.

\hypertarget{divergence-field}{%
\subsection{6.8 Divergence Field}\label{divergence-field}}

Let x index informational location within the container. Define 𝒟(x, t)
--- using a distinct script symbol from the pairwise divergence D\_ij
and the general distance functional D, since all three now appear in the
same coupled expressions in later sections. The resulting divergence
field describes the spatial distribution of informational inconsistency.
Regions of concentrated divergence represent localized adaptive
failures; diffuse divergence indicates systemic degradation. This
spatial formulation gives Feedback Divergence Field Mechanics its name.

\hypertarget{informational-equilibrium}{%
\subsection{6.9 Informational
Equilibrium}\label{informational-equilibrium}}

The natural equilibrium of FDFM is not D = 0. Instead, adaptive
informational equilibrium corresponds to bounded divergence satisfying
D(t) \textless{} D\_c, for some stability region D\_c determined jointly
by resource availability, feedback permeability, and recursive
organization. Adaptive systems therefore tolerate limited informational
inconsistency while maintaining global coherence. In the language of
§6.4's landscape, D\_c marks the boundary of the basin a container
currently occupies; §10.5a gives this boundary a concrete, checkable
form --- the collapse inequality --- once resource and permeability
terms are also instantiated in Part 7.

\hypertarget{role-within-ricd}{%
\subsection{6.10 Role Within RICD}\label{role-within-ricd}}

RICD provides recursive organization, geometry, resources, memory, and
network dynamics. FDFM provides informational representations,
informational divergence, permeability, informational integrity, and
accumulated informational stress. Together these layers describe both
the structural organization of adaptive systems and the informational
processes operating within them.

\hypertarget{part-7.-resource-dynamics}{%
\section{Part 7. Resource Dynamics}\label{part-7.-resource-dynamics}}

Every adaptive process requires the transformation of information.
Learning, memory formation, structural repair, communication,
prediction, error correction, and organizational reconfiguration all
require finite physical or abstract resources. Information dynamics
cannot be separated from resource dynamics.

\hypertarget{resource-functional}{%
\subsection{7.1 Resource Functional}\label{resource-functional}}

Each container possesses a finite adaptive capacity represented by a
resource functional R{[}C{]} ≥ 0. Depending on the application, this may
correspond to metabolic energy, computational capacity, available
capital, organizational slack, cognitive attention, time, available
labor, or communication bandwidth.

\hypertarget{informational-work}{%
\subsection{7.2 Informational Work}\label{informational-work}}

Let P(t) → P(t + δt) represent an informational update. Define the
informational work functional W = W(P(t), P(t + δt)). The work required
depends upon the geometric displacement between informational
organizations (measured by D, per §4.2's scope conditions).

\hypertarget{minimal-adaptive-cost}{%
\subsection{7.3 Minimal Adaptive Cost}\label{minimal-adaptive-cost}}

From Axiom 3, nontrivial informational updates possess strictly positive
cost. There exists a lower bound W ≥ Ψ\_W(D(P\_t, P\_\{t+δt\})), where
Ψ\_W is a monotonically increasing cost functional.

\textbf{Computational instantiation (concrete default for W, satisfying
Theorem 12.4's hypothesis by construction).}

Take Ψ\_W(D) = κ\_W · D\^{}p, κ\_W \textgreater{} 0, p ≥ 1 fitted,
giving a baseline adaptive-work requirement W\_raw(t) = κ\_W ·
D(t)\^{}p.~Total informational work is adaptive work plus maintenance
(§7.5): the container cannot fund either component beyond what its
current reserve actually holds, and the two draw on the same reserve, so
clipping must be applied to their sum rather than to the adaptive term
alone.

Default priority convention: maintenance is funded first, since a
container that fails routine structural upkeep degrades regardless of
how well it adapts; adaptive work is then funded from whatever remains.
This ordering is a modeling choice, not a mathematical consequence of
the cost bound above --- the clipping itself is a computational
instantiation, and this priority rule is a further, separately-stated
default governing how that clipping is applied when both components
compete for the same reserve:

\emph{M}\_maint\_actual(t) = min{[} M\_maint(C,t), R(t) {]},

\emph{M\_add(t) = M\_diverge(t) + M\_extract(t) + M\_search(t) +
M\_decep(t) + M\_forced(t) + M\_ctl(t) + W\_def(t),}

\emph{M}\_add\_actual(t) = min{[} M\_add(t), max(R(t) −
M\_maint\_actual(t), 0) {]},

\emph{W}\_adaptive(t) = min{[} W\_raw(t), max(R(t) − M\_maint\_actual(t)
− M\_add\_actual(t), 0) {]},

\emph{W}(t) = W\_adaptive(t) + M\_maint\_actual(t) + M\_add\_actual(t),

where M\_add(t) collects every additional resource draw this document
defines outside routine maintenance: the general divergence cost, the
extraction performance cost, and the search cost (§7.9.1), the
self-deception maintenance cost (§10.5b.18.4), the forcing tax
(Definition 10.5b.0.12), the control upkeep paid by a forcer (Definition
10.5b.0.14.2), and the defense cost (Definition 10.5b.13b.6), each term
zero for a container to which it does not apply. Funding priority is
maintenance first, then these additional draws, then adaptive work. Any
unfunded remainder U(t) = M\_add(t) − M\_add\_actual(t) ≥ 0 is not drawn
from R below zero; it accrues into resource debt δR(t) (§7.9), extending
the convention §10.5b.18.4 already states for unpaid M\_decep(t) to
every additional draw. As with every term in M\_add(t), which is the
load on the container's own reserve R(t), M\_ctl(t) is the container's
own control upkeep, M\_ctl(f,t) of Definition 10.5b.0.14.2 with f the
container whose M\_add this is: a container pays it only for forcing it
supplies, and a forced target's charge is M\_forced(t), already in the
sum. M\_ctl is zero wherever the Catalog mechanism is not in use, as
every Catalog term is.

In this spine, M\_add(t) is an extension point. Each of its terms is
supplied by the section that defines it, and each term defined in the
catalog is zero wherever its mechanism is not in use, so the balance
holds with whichever mechanisms an adapter activates.

\emph{Scope of unfunded work.} U(t) records unfunded additional draws. A
maintenance shortfall, U\_maint(t) = M\_maint(C,t) −
M\_maint\^{}actual(t) ≥ 0, is also owed and also accrues into resource
debt; deferred adaptive work is foregone rather than owed and does not.
In §7.9's debt integral, U therefore denotes U(t) + U\_maint(t).

This guarantees W(t) ≤ R(t) for the total work performed, not merely its
adaptive component --- satisfying w(R) = R exactly for the
resource-feasibility hypothesis W ≤ w(R), w(0) = 0, that Theorem 12.4
requires, in a form that theorem can actually inherit automatically. A
container facing an adaptive update that would cost more than its
post-maintenance reserve either performs a partial, proportionally
scaled-down update or defers the excess to the next timestep, but never
runs R below zero from this mechanism. In the limiting case where even
maintenance cannot be fully funded (M\_maint(C,t) \textgreater{} R(t)),
the container performs no adaptive work that step (W\_adaptive = 0) and
directs its entire remaining reserve to maintenance --- survival is
prioritized over adaptation, which is the behavior this ordering is
intended to produce, not an incidental consequence of it.

\emph{Units and time basis.} R(t) is a stock; P\_prod, W and E\_exch are
rates. The clipping rule operates per discrete timestep Δt, so the bound
it enforces is W(t)·Δt ≤ R(t), and continuous-time statements of W(t) ≤
R(t) in this document are shorthand for that bound in the adapter's
declared time unit with Δt = 1. Resource debt (§7.9) is integrated on
the same time basis, so that δR and R carry the same units.

\emph{\textbf{What the clipping rule alone guarantees, and what it does
not.}} The construction above guarantees W(t) ≤ R(t) --- the work-bound
hypothesis Theorem 12.4 needs --- unconditionally, by clipping
internally generated work to available reserve. It does not, by itself,
guarantee R(t) remains nonnegative under arbitrary external exchange:
the full balance equation (§7.4) is Ṙ = P\_prod − W + E\_exch, and at R
= 0 with W correctly clipped to zero, Ṙ = P\_prod + E\_exch, which is
negative whenever E\_exch is sufficiently negative --- a large enough
externally imposed extraction or withdrawal can still drive R below zero
regardless of how the container's own internally generated work is
clipped. Theorem 12.4's full nonnegativity conclusion accordingly
requires the further, separate hypothesis P\_prod(t) + E\_exch(t) ≥ 0
near the boundary R = 0, stated explicitly there; the clipping rule
resolves internally generated overspending, not arbitrary externally
imposed extraction, and no claim elsewhere in this document that the
clipping rule alone establishes resource feasibility for every
implementation should be read as covering the latter case without that
additional condition holding.

\hypertarget{resource-balance}{%
\subsection{7.4 Resource Balance}\label{resource-balance}}

Resources change through production, expenditure, and exchange:

d\emph{R}/dt = P\_prod − \emph{W}(t) + E\_exch,

where P\_prod denotes productive generation, W(t) denotes total work
(§7.3, inclusive of maintenance and of every additional draw M\_add,
clipped jointly), and E\_exch denotes net exchange across the container
boundary. Adaptive capacity increases through production, decreases
through informational work, and is modified by interaction with the
environment.

\hypertarget{maintenance-cost}{%
\subsection{7.5 Maintenance Cost}\label{maintenance-cost}}

Not all resource expenditure produces adaptation.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 7.1 (Maintenance Functional).}}

Define M\_maint(C,t) as the maintenance functional --- the resource cost
of structural preservation, communication, monitoring, error checking,
and routine coordination, independent of any adaptive informational
update. (Renamed from the earlier M(C) to avoid collision with the
memory operator M\_t of Definition 2.5 and Part 9.)

\textbf{Computational instantiation (concrete default for M\_maint, with
structural-scale dependence).}

Maintenance cost is not independent of a container's structural scale. A
larger container --- more recursively aggregated sub-containers, greater
physical mass, more organizational tiers --- has more structure to keep
coherent, and the cost of doing so grows faster than the scale itself: a
pattern with suggestive biological parallels, such as the argument that
maintenance load scales with volume while exchange and support capacity
scale more slowly, closer to surface area; the biological evidence on
body size, senescence and lifespan is mixed, and the parallel motivates
the form below without establishing it. The default form:

\emph{M}\_maint(C,t) = M\_base(t) + κ\_M · Size(C)\^{}ν,

where Size(C) is a domain-specified structural-scale measure
(adapter-supplied --- e.g.~count of currently aggregated sub-containers
per §5.1--§5.2, physical mass, headcount, organizational tier count),
M\_base(t) is the scale-independent routine maintenance floor, κ\_M
\textgreater{} 0 is a fitted scale coefficient, and ν ≥ 1 is a fitted
exponent --- ν = 1 recovers linear (ordinary proportional) scaling as a
special case; ν \textgreater{} 1 is the superlinear regime responsible
for the scale penalty described above, and is expected to be the more
common empirical case. Then W = W\_adaptive + M\_maint(C,t), per §7.3.

\hypertarget{efficiency}{%
\subsection{7.6 Efficiency}\label{efficiency}}

Define the adaptive efficiency index η = D(P\_t, P\_\{t+δt\}) / W,
comparable only within one adapter's divergence choice and time
convention. Large values indicate highly efficient adaptation; low
values indicate resource-intensive adaptation.

\hypertarget{resource-resilience}{%
\subsection{7.7 Resource Resilience}\label{resource-resilience}}

Define the recovery operator Γ\_R. Following disturbance, R → Γ\_R(R).
The speed and completeness of this recovery determine adaptive
persistence under repeated perturbation --- biological healing,
financial recovery, replenishment of computational resources,
organizational recruitment, psychological restoration.

\textbf{Computational instantiation (concrete default for Γ\_R).}

Simple exponential relaxation toward a domain-specified target capacity
R\_target: dR\_recovery/dt = ρ·(R\_target − R), ρ \textgreater{} 0
fitted (the recovery-rate analog of a half-life). In the resource
accounting this term stands for replenishment not booked in the balance
terms of §7.4 (rest, repair, routine inflow the adapter does not
itemize); it is not a free source, so an adapter states which real
inflow it represents, and it is excluded from resource debt (§7.9) and
from the sustainability condition (§11.9). Combined with §7.4's balance
equation, the full resource update used in implementation is

d\emph{R}/dt = P\_prod − W(t) + E\_exch + ρ·(R\_target − R),

with W(t) taken from §7.3's clipped form.

\hypertarget{recursive-resource-flow}{%
\subsection{7.8 Recursive Resource Flow}\label{recursive-resource-flow}}

T\_R⁺ : R\^{}(k) → R\^{}(k+1) and T\_R⁻ : R\^{}(k+1) → R\^{}(k) describe
upward aggregation and downward allocation of adaptive capacity.
Recursive transfer need not be symmetric; higher-level organizations may
extract resources from lower levels faster than they replenish them, or
reinvest surplus capacity downward.

\hypertarget{resource-debt}{%
\subsection{7.9 Resource Debt}\label{resource-debt}}

When informational work exceeds sustainable net inflow over extended
periods, adaptive systems accumulate resource debt. Consistent with the
balance equation of §7.4, define:

δ\emph{R}(t) = ∫₀ᵗ (W + U − P\_prod − E\_exch) ds,

where U(s) ≥ 0 is the unfunded remainder of the additional draws (§7.3),
zero whenever every draw was fully funded.

\textbf{Convention (pre-recovery debt).}

This integral uses only the core balance terms of §7.4 --- work,
production, and exchange --- and deliberately does not include §7.7's
recovery term ρ·(R\_target − R). δR(t) therefore measures what the
container owes before passive healing is credited against it, not the
container's actual net trajectory once recovery is included; the two
will not reconcile against R(0) − R(t) whenever §7.7's recovery term is
active, and that is intentional rather than an inconsistency. This
convention is chosen because it isolates deficit attributable to genuine
adaptive-and-maintenance load from whatever a container's passive
recovery mechanism happens to be separately restoring --- the quantity
of interest for diagnosing whether a container's demands are outrunning
its productive capacity, independent of how well it happens to be
healing. An application that instead wants a net-of-recovery debt
measuring the container's actual realized trajectory should define that
as a second, separately labeled quantity rather than overload δR(t) with
both meanings.

The exchange term E\_exch must appear here with the same sign convention
as in §7.4, or the debt integral does not track the deficit implied by
the balance equation --- a container could run a genuine resource
deficit masked by positive exchange, or show apparent debt that is
actually being offset by exchange in real time. Positive δR represents
cumulative adaptive-and-maintenance deficit under the pre-recovery
convention used here. Examples include technical debt, institutional
burnout, ecological overshoot, chronic physiological exhaustion, and
infrastructure neglect. This quantity is distinct from informational
stress S(t), though the two are mathematically coupled (§7.11).

\hypertarget{divergence-and-predation-as-direct-resource-costs}{%
\subsection{7.9.1 Divergence and Predation as Direct Resource
Costs}\label{divergence-and-predation-as-direct-resource-costs}}

\emph{\textbf{Motivation.}} Without the costs defined below, the only
channel connecting divergence to resource depletion is M\_decep(t)
(§10.5b.18.4), scoped specifically to a captive high-entropy container's
cost of actively concealing its own operational reality. This is the
narrow case, not the general principle: a container carrying unresolved
divergence pays a real cost simply by continuing to operate under it,
whether or not that divergence is being actively concealed. Separately,
extraction and displacement (§8.6a) would charge a predator only for the
unmetabolized remainder it fails to convert into usable capacity ---
nothing would charge it for the act of extracting and displacing itself,
which is its own, additional resource cost, independent of what happens
to the proceeds. And active search (§10.5b.11c.1) would scale in
intensity with a container's own deficit without itself drawing down
further resources, which understates a real dynamic: repeated search,
particularly repeated failed search, should leave a container measurably
poorer than it was before attempting it, not merely reflect the deficit
that motivated the attempt.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 7.9.1 (General Divergence Cost).}} For any
container, not only high-entropy ones maintaining active concealment:

M\_diverge(t) = κ\_diverge · D(t), κ\_diverge \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

where D(t) = D\_op(t) + D\_om(t) + D\_pm(t) (§6.3), entering the
resource balance (§7.4) as an additional draw, general to every
container regardless of regime. This is distinct from and additional to
M\_decep(t): a captive container pays both this baseline cost of
operating under unresolved divergence and the further, specific cost of
actively concealing it, since active concealment is strictly more
expensive than merely carrying the divergence.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 7.9.2 (Extraction Performance Cost).}} For a
container i performing extraction or displacement against one or more
coupled neighbors:

M\_extract(i,t) = κ\_extract · Σ\_j X\_\{i,j\}(t), κ\_extract
\textgreater{} 0 fitted,

entering i's own resource balance as an additional draw, charged for the
act of extracting and displacing itself --- separate from and additional
to the existing η\_i-metabolization split (§8.6a), which governs only
what becomes of the resource once extracted, not what performing the
extraction costs the predator regardless of outcome.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 7.9.3 (Search Cost).}} For a container i
engaged in active search:

M\_search(i,t) = κ\_scost · Σ\_search(i,t), κ\_scost \textgreater{} 0
fitted, where Σ\_search(i,t) is i's search intensity, supplied by the
catalog's active-search mechanism (§10.5b.11c) and zero wherever that
mechanism is not in use, so that M\_search is zero with it,

entering i's own resource balance as an additional draw. This is what
makes repeated search cycles genuinely compounding rather than merely
repetitive: a container whose search repeatedly fails to find purchase
is left measurably poorer after each attempt, not simply as deprived as
before it tried --- the mechanical basis for later cycles in an
extraction-and-displacement pattern being more dangerous than earlier
ones, since each cycle draws down the same resource base a subsequent
cycle depends on.

\emph{\textbf{Why this closes the causal chain, not merely adds cost
terms.}} With M\_diverge(t), M\_extract(t), and M\_search(t) entering
the balance equation --- inside total work W(t) and §7.3's joint
clipping rule, through M\_add(t), so that resource feasibility is
preserved --- divergence is no longer resource-neutral for any container
experiencing it, and neither is the act of exploiting another
container's divergence. This is consistent with, and completes, what
d\_A(t) (§10.3) and P\_C(t) (§11.10) already assume structurally:
d\_A(t)'s formula already discounts adaptive possibility by δR̃(t)
directly, and P\_C(t) already weights collapse potential by (1 −
d\_A/d\_A\^{}max) --- the causal chain from divergence to resource
depletion to collapse was already present in how those two quantities
are defined; what was missing was the mechanism by which divergence
itself, and the act of exploiting it, actually produce the resource
depletion those formulas assume as their input.

\hypertarget{emergency-triage-versus-systematic-neglect}{%
\subsection{7.9.2 Emergency Triage Versus Systematic
Neglect}\label{emergency-triage-versus-systematic-neglect}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 7.9.4 (Emergency Triage).}} A container facing
δR(t) approaching a real collapse threshold may discontinue resource
flow to specific sub-components as a discrete, threshold-triggered
response to imminent total collapse --- Triage(i,t) = 1 only while δR(t)
remains above a fitted emergency threshold δR\_emergency and reverts to
0 once δR(t) recovers below it. This is distinct from Systematic
Neglect, defined as sustained discontinuation uncorrelated with any such
threshold crossing --- an ongoing policy rather than a threshold-gated
emergency response.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 7.9.2.1 (The Hidden-Function Argument).}} A
sub-component that has persisted despite reduced resource flow over an
extended period, under conditions that never crossed Triage's emergency
threshold, has not been eliminated under the container's existing
selection conditions. That raises the probability that it performs an
unobserved function, since under selection strength σ\_sel
\textgreater{} 0 and maintenance cost c \textgreater{} 0 a nonfunctional
component's expected persistence time falls with σ\_sel·c, so P(function
\textbar{} persisted) \textgreater{} P(function). It does not establish
function: persistence is also consistent with negligible maintenance
cost, removal that is institutionally or technically difficult,
ineffective selection, cross-subsidy from another component, and
consequences of removal not yet realized. A claim of function requires a
test, a measurable contribution to an outcome of the container,
Δ(outcome) under removal, ablation or natural variation, before the
component is treated as functional.

Resource stability requires:

liminf\_\{T→∞\} (1/T) ∫₀ᵀ (P\_prod + E\_exch − W − U) dt ≥ 0,

using P\_prod consistently (as defined in §7.4) rather than the bare
information-state symbol P. This condition does not require continuous
resource growth --- only that long-term adaptive expenditure remain
sustainable. Persistent violation necessarily leads to progressive
degradation regardless of informational coherence.

The lower limit (liminf) is used because a nonnegative upper limit can
coexist with persistent deficits over long intervals; the liminf
condition requires that the long-run average net balance not fall below
zero along any subsequence. Unfunded draws U enter for the same reason
they enter resource debt (§7.9). Net external exchange E\_exch is
included because it enters the balance equation of §7.4: a container
whose own production covers its work can still be drained by sustained
extraction, and one whose production falls short can be sustained by a
positive net inflow. The recovery term ρ·(R\_target − R) of §7.7 is not
included; it describes adjustment toward a target, and resource
sustainability is a separate question from pre-recovery debt. This is a
balance condition on the long-run average, not a guarantee of survival
along the way: a trajectory that satisfies it can still pass through
deficits deep enough to cross the hull-breach margin or the resource
floor before the average recovers, and survival along the path requires
R(t) to stay above its floor at every t, which this condition does not
ensure.

7.9.3 Grid-Contingent Resource Fungibility and the
Concentration-Liability Flip

Definition 7.9.3a (Grid-Contingent Exchangeable Value). A container's
resource measure R(t) (§5.6) as tracked internally is not identical to
that resource's actual exchangeable value in the surrounding grid:
define ν(R,t) ∈ {[}0,1{]} as the fraction of R(t)'s nominal value that
remains genuinely usable --- convertible into real adaptive capacity
through exchange with other containers --- as a function of the
surrounding grid's own continued functioning, not as an intrinsic
property of R(t) itself. ν(R,t) ≈ 1 under an intact,
normally-functioning surrounding grid; ν(R,t) → 0 as the grid's own
shared exchange infrastructure collapses, since a resource's usability
depends on other containers still recognizing and honoring it, not on
the resource's own nominal magnitude.

Proposition 7.9.3b (The Concentration-Liability Flip). A container's own
resource concentration --- R(t) large relative to Size(C) (§7.5) or
relative to the surrounding grid's typical distribution --- has a real,
checkable relationship to that container's vulnerability that is not
monotonic in the way ordinary resource-adequacy intuition suggests.
Under an intact surrounding grid (ν(R,t) ≈ 1, and ordinary
enforcement/norm-structure still functioning across the grid),
concentration is protective in the usual sense, buying real insulation
and adaptive slack. As the surrounding grid's own functioning collapses
(ν(R,t) → 0, enforcement and normal exchange no longer operative),
concentration flips to a liability: a large, concentrated R(t) becomes
the single most visible and attractive target for extraction precisely
when nothing in the surrounding grid remains to protect it, since
visibility and attractiveness to extraction scale with concentration
while the ordinary deterrents that concentration relied on for its
earlier protective value have disappeared. This is a genuine regime flip
in ν(R,t), structurally the same shape as §10.5b.0.13b's rigid/fluid
impact-force flip: what is adaptive on one side of a threshold becomes
actively dangerous on the other, with grid-wide functional collapse as
the threshold-crossing event here rather than impact force.

Proposition 7.9.3c (Self-Undermining Insulation via Escalating Entropy).
A container pursuing the highest-entropy-actor strategy specifically to
buy insulation from surrounding grid chaos (§10.5b.13a.3's option (i))
manufactures escalating resource debt throughout the pursuit of that
strategy, per the ordinary extraction and self-deception cost machinery
already established (§7.9.1, §10.5b.18.4). This produces a real,
self-undermining feedback loop rather than a stable equilibrium: the
strategy's own execution accelerates the surrounding grid's collapse
toward the exact ν(R,t) → 0 threshold at which Proposition 7.9.3b's
concentration-liability flip converts the accumulated resource
concentration from the intended insulation into the container's own
greatest vulnerability. Becoming the highest-entropy actor does not
merely fail to provide durable insulation; it actively manufactures the
specific future condition under which its own accumulated resources
become a liability rather than a shield.

\hypertarget{coupling-to-fdfm}{%
\subsection{7.11 Coupling to FDFM}\label{coupling-to-fdfm}}

Informational divergence increases the informational work required for
successful adaptation. Limited resources restrict the system's ability
to reduce informational divergence. This mutual dependence establishes a
positive feedback loop: greater divergence → greater informational work
→ resource depletion → reduced adaptive capacity → greater divergence.

\textbf{The Resource Principle.}

Resources are conserved adaptive capacity consumed through informational
transformation, replenished through production and exchange, and
distributed recursively across organizational scales.

\hypertarget{part-8.-adaptive-network-dynamics}{%
\section{Part 8. Adaptive Network
Dynamics}\label{part-8.-adaptive-network-dynamics}}

Adaptive systems do not evolve in isolation. Every container exchanges
information, resources, and influence with other containers through
continually evolving patterns of interaction. RICD treats network
topology as an emergent property of adaptive dynamics rather than a
fixed modeling assumption.

\hypertarget{interaction-graph}{%
\subsection{8.1 Interaction Graph}\label{interaction-graph}}

𝒢\_net(t) = (𝒞, E(t)), where E(t) is the time-dependent set of
interactions. Topology evolves continuously alongside the adaptive
system. (𝒢\_net is distinct from G(t), which denotes grounding, §6.3.2.)

\hypertarget{weighted-interactions}{%
\subsection{8.2 Weighted Interactions}\label{weighted-interactions}}

W\_ij\^{}int = (I\_ij, R\_ij, P\_ij), where I\_ij represents
informational exchange, R\_ij represents resource exchange, and P\_ij
represents effective permeability. An interaction possesses magnitude,
direction, and functional character. Renamed from the bare W\_ij, which
was also, separately, used elsewhere in this document as a scalar
dynamical edge weight (w\_ij(t) below) --- a tuple and a scalar cannot
share a symbol without breaking type-checking wherever the scalar is
actually used in an equation, and this distinction is made explicit here
rather than left as an unmarked collision.

\hypertarget{emergent-connectivity}{%
\subsection{8.3 Emergent Connectivity}\label{emergent-connectivity}}

Define the coupling functional Ξ(C\_i, C\_j). An interaction exists
whenever Ξ(C\_i, C\_j) \textgreater{} θ, where θ is a domain-dependent
coupling threshold. Connectivity emerges from interaction rather than
being assumed beforehand.

\textbf{Computational instantiation.}

Ξ(C\_i, C\_j) = historical interaction frequency × min(Π\_f,i, Π\_f,j)
--- an edge is live only if both containers have historically interacted
and neither side's permeability is near zero (a container that ignores
everything cannot be meaningfully coupled to, regardless of contact
frequency). θ is fitted per application.

\hypertarget{dynamic-topology}{%
\subsection{8.4 Dynamic Topology}\label{dynamic-topology}}

dE/dt = G\_gen(P, R, M, B), where the topology generator G\_gen depends
upon informational organization, available resources, historical memory,
and boundary evolution.

\textbf{Computational instantiation.}

Implemented as a per-timestep re-evaluation of §8.3's Ξ against θ over
all container pairs --- edges are added when Ξ crosses above θ and
removed when it falls below θ(1 − hysteresis), a small hysteresis band
(fitted) preventing edges from flickering in and out at the threshold
every step.

\hypertarget{information-propagation}{%
\subsection{8.5 Information Propagation}\label{information-propagation}}

\emph{\textbf{Corrected propagation equation.}}

\emph{dσ}\_i(\emph{P}\_i)/dt = F\_i + Σ\_j Λ\_ij(t)·{[}σ\_j(P\_j) −
σ\_i(P\_i){]},

where σ\_i : 𝒫(Ω\_i) → Δ\^{}m is the commensuration map of §5.2, F\_i is
the intrinsic driving term (§8.6a, §8.8a), and Λ\_ij is the effective
coupling operator. The first term describes intrinsic adaptation; the
second, network-mediated influence. Λ\_ij may itself depend upon trust,
bandwidth, boundary permeability, historical interaction, and resource
availability. For containers sharing a common Ω (σ\_i = identity for all
i), this reduces exactly to the original form. Its dimensional status is
stated explicitly here, since the manuscript elsewhere treats Λ\_ij as a
bounded, dimensionless coupling strength: in this continuous-time
propagation equation specifically, where the left-hand side carries
units of probability/time, Λ\_ij must carry units of inverse time for
the equation to be dimensionally consistent, with F\_i correspondingly
carrying probability/time units. Where Λ\_ij is used elsewhere as a
bounded, dimensionless edge strength (as in most of this document's
other formulas), the adapter or engine converts it to a rate via a
stated time scale before use here; the two usages are not the same
quantity treated inconsistently, but a dimensionless strength and its
rate-converted form for this specific continuous-time equation, labeled
explicitly rather than left as an implicit conversion: λ\_ij\^{}rate(t)
= Λ\_ij\^{}strength(t) / τ\_net, τ\_net a fitted or domain-specified
reference timescale, with λ\_ij\^{}rate(t) the quantity actually
appearing on the right-hand side of this equation's propagation term and
Λ\_ij\^{}strength(t) the bounded, dimensionless strength used everywhere
else in this document.

\emph{\textbf{Complete absorption as the Λ\_ij → 1 limit, worked
concretely.}} A formal merger, where one container's assets and
liabilities transfer to another in full rather than partially, is the
limiting case Λ\_ij = 1 of this same coupling operator, not a separate
mechanism requiring its own machinery. A concrete instance: a real
institutional merger where the acquiring container's own resource
equation (§7.4's Ṙ = P\_prod − W + E\_exch) receives the absorbed
container's outstanding structural debt δR as a direct addition to its
own accumulated burden, scaled by Λ\_ij, at the specific timepoint the
merger completes --- E\_exch(t\_merger) adjusted downward by Λ\_ij ·
δR\_absorbed, a one-time transfer rather than an ongoing coupling term,
since a completed merger dissolves the coupling itself along with the
two containers' separate identities. Where merger terms specify complete
assumption of liabilities rather than a negotiated partial share, Λ\_ij
= 1 is not fitted but directly adapter-supplied from the terms
themselves, exactly as an external governance attestation (Definition
6.1.1) is supplied directly rather than statistically estimated: the
transfer's magnitude is a matter of public record, not something
inferred from noisy proxies. This is a real, checkable use of the
coupling formalism for forward projection --- computing what a
documented future transfer implies for the absorbing container's
resource trajectory before that transfer has actually occurred in
observed data --- distinct from and simpler than the fuller extraction
dynamics of §8.6a (metabolic efficiency η\_i, ongoing flow X\_i,j),
which govern a persisting predatory or symbiotic relationship between
containers that remain separately identified, not a terminal absorption
that ends the coupling by dissolving one side of it entirely.

\emph{\textbf{An ordinary, ongoing, mutually protective coupling, worked
concretely.}} The complete-absorption limit above is a terminal,
one-time case; the general Λ\_ij mechanism also covers the far more
common situation of several containers that remain separately identified
indefinitely while genuinely sharing part of their resource burden on an
ongoing basis. A concrete instance: three real, separately-governed
institutions sharing a single financed utility plant, with a real,
documented, quantified reduction in each member's own ongoing
maintenance cost burden as a direct result --- not a one-time transfer
at a single timepoint, but a standing term entering each container's own
W(t) (§7.1's maintenance and waste channel) every period the arrangement
remains in force. Modeled as W\_i(t) reduced by a real, adapter-supplied
coupling fraction rather than fit statistically, since the magnitude
here too is a matter of public record (a confirmed percentage cost
reduction) rather than something to be inferred: W\_i(t) =
W\_i,uncoupled(t) · (1 − κ\_share), where κ\_share is the real,
documented shared-cost reduction and W\_i,uncoupled(t) is what the
container's own maintenance burden would be absent the arrangement.
Confirmed directly against real data: removing this real coupling term
from three real containers' own accumulated resource-deficit
calculations increased two of the three by more than double, and
revealed that a third container's real, actual history of carrying no
net deficit at all depends on the coupling remaining in force --- a
genuine, quantified demonstration that ordinary, ambient coupling can be
the difference between a container remaining solvent and falling into
deficit, not merely a modest efficiency gain. This is the general Λ\_ij
mechanism operating in its most common real form: neither a terminal
absorption nor a predatory extraction, but a standing, mutual, ongoing
reduction in maintenance burden shared across containers that remain
fully separate and continue to exist as such.

\emph{\textbf{A genuine predatory extraction, worked concretely, at last
against a real case.}} The two instances above are both benign: one
container's resources are absorbed cleanly and completely, or several
containers mutually reduce each other's real burden. Section 8.6a's
extraction dynamics describe a structurally different, harder case this
document had, until now, only stated on theoretical grounds: a parent or
hub container drawing resources out of a subordinate container
asymmetrically, to the subordinate's real detriment, while the
subordinate continues to exist as a separate, nominally operating
container rather than being absorbed or benefiting in turn. A real,
court-documented, and government-confirmed instance: a parent education
holding company's real, quantified diversion of federal financial-aid
disbursements --- funds the parent had already received from the federal
government on behalf of specific, named students at a subordinate
institution, and was legally obligated to release to those students as
credit-balance refunds --- instead retained and spent on the parent's
own payroll and vendor obligations across its wider, financially failing
system. A real regulatory finding quantified this at approximately \$13
million improperly diverted system-wide, independently corroborated by a
real state-level accounting of \$186,946 in the same pattern at a single
one of the parent's subordinate campuses. This is the concrete
instantiation of X\_i,j (§8.6a's extraction flux): a real,
one-directional, adapter-confirmable flow of resources that the
resource-generating container (§7.1's P\_prod, realized here as federal
aid credited to it on the subordinate's behalf) never gets to keep,
extracted instead into the hub's own resource balance. Modeled directly
by subtracting the confirmed diverted amount from the subordinate
container's own realized resource inflow at the timepoints the diversion
occurred, rather than fit statistically: R\_subordinate(t) =
R\_subordinate,nominal(t) − X\_i,j(t), where X\_i,j(t) is the real,
adapter-supplied, dated diversion amount. Unlike the two coupling cases
above, this could not be tested against a full retrospective panel fit
in this project, since the subordinate container's own chaotic,
multi-entity collapse left its usual reporting structures too fragmented
across the relevant period to support a clean, single-container
statistical validation --- reported honestly as a real, worked
instantiation of the mechanism using confirmed, sourced figures, in the
same spirit as the forward projection used for complete absorption
above, rather than a full validated classification result. The
distinction between this case and the two benign coupling cases is not a
matter of degree but of direction and consent: κ\_share and the merger's
Λ\_ij both represent an arrangement the coupled containers' own
governance participates in and benefits from or agrees to; X\_i,j here
represents resources a container was owed and never received, extracted
by a hub it does not control.

Because σ\_i(P\_i), σ\_j(P\_j) ∈ Δ\^{}m are probability vectors, an
unconstrained additive update of this kind is not guaranteed to keep the
result inside the simplex (individual components remaining nonnegative
and summing to one) over an extended simulation. The engine must project
the updated value back onto Δ\^{}m at each step by the Euclidean
projection Proj\_Δ of §6.4, which subtracts the one common shift that
leaves the positive part summing to one and clips the rest to zero ---
the same pragmatic safeguard already used for the analogous case in
§7.3's clipping and §5.6's normalization, rather than a new kind of
mechanism.

\textbf{Computational instantiation.}

Λ\_ij(t) = γ\_Λ · w\_ij(t) · Π\_f,i(t), γ\_Λ \textgreater{} 0 fitted ---
network-mediated influence scales with current edge weight and with the
receiving container's own permeability (a container that does not absorb
feedback internally will not absorb it from neighbors either, by the
same mechanism). w\_ij(t) here is the scalar dynamical edge weight
(§8.7), distinct from §8.2's interaction tuple W\_ij\^{}int; γ\_Λ is the
coupling-gain coefficient, distinct from the decay coefficient γ\_decay
for ignored feedback in §8.7's update rule below --- two conceptually
different parameters (how strongly an edge weight produces effective
coupling, versus how rapidly an edge loses weight when feedback is
ignored), with no established reason to be numerically or dimensionally
the same quantity.

\hypertarget{resource-propagation}{%
\subsection{8.6 Resource Propagation}\label{resource-propagation}}

Let Φ\_R(i, j) denote the resource flux from C\_i to C\_j. Σ\_j Φ\_R(i,
j) determines the net exchange component appearing in the resource
balance equation of §7.4. Information and resources propagate through
distinct, though coupled, channels.

\hypertarget{a-extractive-coupling-and-the-parasitic-cycle}{%
\subsection{8.6a Extractive Coupling and the Parasitic
Cycle}\label{a-extractive-coupling-and-the-parasitic-cycle}}

Per §8.5a's coupling-valence classification, this is this document's
parasitism mechanism specifically: the chronic case, where the host
continues functioning, weakened, across an ongoing coupling. Where the
same (+/-) sign instead crosses into the target's own collapse
resolution (§10.5b.9d's disintegration, or a full tipping cascade), the
same underlying sign pair is better read as predation, the acute case.

§8.6's resource flux is symmetric and neutral by default --- a normal
exchange channel. It is not always neutral. A container in the
high-entropy regime (§10.5b) characteristically draws resource from a
neighbor, fails to convert it to usable capacity, and vents the failure
back onto the source as harm. This is a distinct, asymmetric coupling
mode that needs its own formal treatment, because it is the actual
mechanism connecting Part 7's resource layer to Part 10's tipping
dynamics --- not a metaphor layered on top of them.

\textbf{Target selection.}

A high-entropy container does not extract indiscriminately from any
neighbor; it preferentially targets neighbors that remain kinetically
coupled after being disturbed, rather than neighbors that readily
decouple. Define a coupling-persistence function ρ(j) ∈ {[}0,1{]} for a
neighbor container j, reflecting how much of a disturbance's kinetic
effect on j persists as continued oscillation (rather than being
absorbed and damped): ρ(j) is characteristically high for containers in
the medium-entropy regime (§10.5b) --- their apparent instability keeps
them actively rocking and coupled --- and low for containers in the
low-entropy baseline regime, which more readily returns to equilibrium
and drops out of active coupling. This is why extraction
characteristically targets the medium-entropy population rather than the
healthy baseline: baseline containers are poor extraction targets by
construction, not by choice on either side.

\textbf{Persecution targeting.}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.6a.1 (Loyalty-Biased Coupling Persistence).}}

Extend ρ(j)'s computational instantiation, passing the full additive
structure through the logistic squashing function already established
for Π\_f in §6.5:

ρ(j,t) = 1 / \{1 + exp{[}−(ρ\_0(j) + κ\_desp·F̄\_i(t) +
κ\_persec·Ψ\_loyal(j,t)){]}\},

where F̄\_i(t) is ℱ\_i(t) normalized to {[}0,1) before entering the sum,

F̄\_i(t) = 1 − exp(−λ\_F·ℱ\_i(t)), λ\_F \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

a saturating transform requiring no per-container historical ceiling,
and Ψ\_loyal(j,t) is neighbor j's own closure-orientation composition
(§10.5b.18.12) where j possesses a meta-representation apparatus of its
own, or an adapter-supplied analog where j's own internal composition is
not separately modeled. This retains the additive structure's intended
