behavior --- desperation and persecution both push targeting upward,
independently and simultaneously --- while guaranteeing ρ(j,t) ∈
{[}0,1{]} by construction, consistent with the logistic guardrail
already established for Π\_f in §6.5. Squashing changes what κ\_desp and
κ\_persec mean: under this corrected form, they are log-odds
contributions, not direct additive weights on a {[}0,1{]} quantity, and
values fitted against an unbounded additive form do not carry over
numerically.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 8.6a.1 (Persecution Is Threat-Responsive, Not
Resource-Responsive).}}

Unlike ordinary target selection, which favors kinetically-persistent
neighbors as better extraction sources, κ\_persec·Ψ\_loyal(j,t)'s
contribution to ρ(j) does not depend on j's own resources or
vulnerability. A high-Ψ\_loyal(j,t) neighbor is targeted more heavily
specifically because of what its own composition threatens to do to the
surrounding network --- correct nearby containers via φ\_fb
(§10.5b.18.9), or serve as a genuine external anchor (§10.5b.13,
§10.5b.13.1) --- not because it offers more to extract. This is the
formal content of a system hunting its own corrective response rather
than merely exploiting whatever is easiest to exploit.

\emph{Domain instances.} Tumors suppress the immune effector cells best
placed to attack them; states target dissidents rather than the richest
neighbors; firms retaliate against whistleblowers whose presence
threatens the organization's own composition.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.6a.2 (Network-Level Nonconformist
Fraction).}}

At the network level, define f\_NC(t) ∈ {[}0,1{]}, the resource-weighted
fraction of containers in a network whose own Ψ\_loyal(t) (or
adapter-supplied analog) exceeds a fitted threshold Θ\_NC. This is a
derived, network-level diagnostic --- not a primitive driving any
dynamics of its own --- computed as an aggregate consequence of
Definition 8.6a.1's targeting bias operating over time across the
network, which should be expected to reduce f\_NC(t) below whatever
baseline it would hold absent persecution-weighted targeting.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.6a.3 (Persecution Deficit Signal).}}

P\_persecute(t) = max(0, f\_NC\^{}target − f\_NC(t)),

where f\_NC\^{}target is a domain-specified expected baseline fraction
(adapter-supplied; no universal value is asserted here). P\_persecute(t)
is a diagnostic reading of Definition 8.6a.1's targeting mechanism's
cumulative effect, to be used as a corroborating signal alongside direct
evidence of elevated ρ(j) bias toward high-loyalty containers --- not as
a free-standing indicator sufficient on its own, since a low f\_NC(t) is
also consistent with causes other than persecution (e.g., a network
genuinely composed of few high-loyalty containers to begin with,
independent of any targeting).

\textbf{Extraction flux.}

For high-entropy container i and a coupled neighbor j:

\emph{X}\_\{i,j\}(t) = χ · Λ\_ij(t) · ρ(j) · R\_j(t) · 𝒱\_\{i→j\}(t) ·
(1 − κ\_self·Ψ\_total(i,t))₊, χ \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

drawing down j's resource directly in the balance equation of §7.4.
𝒱\_\{i→j\}(t) (§8.6a.4) is i's own observation-mapped estimate of j's
vulnerability --- not unmediated access to j's true latent state,
consistent with §8.6a.4's own true-versus-assessed distinction; this
formula is the actual point where that distinction has causal
consequence, and it uses the predator-specific estimate directly rather
than the raw latent quantity. The closing factor is i's own
self-regulation term (§8.6a.5): the same base formula this document has
used throughout, now modulated by both how resilient the specific target
actually is and how far the predator itself has drifted toward its own
negentropic core. 𝒱\_\{i→j\}(t) is a catalog quantity (§8.6a.4) that
enters as a multiplier with inactive value 1, so that where the
catalog's vulnerability-assessment mechanism is not in use,
𝒱\_\{i→j\}(t) = 1. Setting 𝒱\_\{i→j\}(t) = 1 and κ\_self = 0 recovers
the base formula exactly.

\emph{Units and dissipation.} X\_\{i,j\} is a resource-flow rate and
R\_j a stock, so χ carries units of inverse time unless Λ\_ij already
does through §8.5's rate conversion; the adapter states which. Of the
flow X\_\{i,j\} leaving j, η\_i·X\_\{i,j\} enters i's resources and (1 −
η\_i)·X\_\{i,j\} leaves the resource ledger as vented heat, converted
into forcing on i's neighbors by Definition 8.8a.3a: the ledger
conserves resource up to this explicit dissipation sink.

\textbf{Metabolic failure and heat conversion.}

A high-entropy container's own closed, self-propelled internal dynamics
(§10.5b's F\_i-dominance) mean it cannot productively convert an inflow
of externally-sourced open resource into usable capacity --- define a
metabolic efficiency η\_i ∈ {[}0,1{]}, characteristically near zero for
high-entropy containers, giving a heat term for each coupled neighbor j:

\emph{H}\_i,j(t) = (1 − η\_i) · X\_\{i,j\}(t),

representing the fraction of resource extracted from j that cannot be
metabolized and must be discharged rather than retained.

\textbf{Closing the resource ledger.}

The extraction flux X\_\{i,j\}(t) leaves j's balance equation in full
--- j's resource loss does not depend on how much i subsequently manages
to use. What i actually gains is only the metabolized fraction: i's
balance equation (§7.4) receives a positive E\_exch contribution of η\_i
· X\_\{i,j\}(t) from this channel. The unmetabolized remainder,
H\_i,j(t), is not a resource quantity at all once it fails to be
metabolized --- it converts to a forcing quantity (below) and re-enters
the system through §8.5's propagation equation rather than through
anyone's resource balance. This is a genuine unit change, not a
bookkeeping shortcut, and both sides of the transaction --- j's full
loss, i's partial, degraded gain --- must be entered into §7.4 for a
given implementation to conserve resource correctly across the
extraction event.

\textbf{Venting and the origin of the medium-entropy driving force.}

Summing the heat generated across all of i's currently coupled neighbors
gives the total instantaneous venting rate,

\emph{H}\_i(t) = Σ\_j H\_i,j(t) = Σ\_j (1 − η\_i)·X\_\{i,j\}(t),

which is not dissipated harmlessly; it is vented back into the network
as additional forcing, directed at each coupled j in proportion to its
own ρ(j) if j remains coupled, or redistributed across other available
coupled neighbors if a particular j has decoupled. This vented heat is
not a separate force from §10.5b's Σ\_jΛ\_ij(t) driving term for
medium-entropy tipping --- it is that term's actual origin: the
``lateral knocks'' driving a medium-entropy container toward its own
tipping event are, mechanistically, the discharged metabolic failure of
the high-entropy containers extracting from it. §10.5b's
network-mediated drive should accordingly be read as substantially
composed of vented H\_i,j(t) terms from coupled high-entropy neighbors,
not an independent disturbance source.

Proposition 8.6a.4 (Predation as One Act, Two Simultaneous Effects).
Pointless predation --- destruction of another container producing no
genuine metabolic gain, distinct from the resource-neutral case already
covered above --- is the cleanest instance of X\_i,j(t) and H\_i,j(t) as
two effects of one single act rather than sequential events. The
destructive act itself is simultaneously the extraction (X\_i,j(t), the
target's own energy or resource being consumed) and the displacement
(H\_i,j(t), the predator's own unmetabolized chaos discharged through
the same act that destroys), not extraction followed by a separate
cathartic release: the killing motion is the vented heat, for the
predator carrying it out. This is already implicit in H\_i,j(t) =
(1−η\_i)·X\_i,j(t) being computed from the same event at the same t,
stated explicitly here because predation is the case where the identity
is most visible and most consequential to name.

Proposition 8.6a.5 (Healthy Reciprocal Dissipation, the Negentropic
Counterpart). A genuinely healthy, reciprocally-coupled pair of
containers achieves a structurally opposite version of the same
dissipative-structure shape (§8.6a's own framing, per Prigogine): rather
than one container maintaining its own form by dumping metabolic failure
onto the other, each helps the other export incoming entropic pressure
into the shared environment rather than absorbing it internally.
Concretely, healthy negentropic feedback exchanged between coupled
containers reduces each container's own oscillatory response to an
incoming entropic knock (§8.8a's Drive\_B), so that the disturbance
passes through the coupled pair and out into the surrounding grid rather
than striking either container as a full, direct blow --- the knock
ripples across the healthy coupling rather than being absorbed and
re-radiated destructively the way §8.6a's parasitic version discharges
it. This is the same underlying dissipative-structure principle in both
cases --- order maintained by exporting entropy outward rather than
accumulating it internally --- with the sign of the coupling, not the
principle itself, determining whether the outward export helps a
neighbor absorb an external shock or dumps the container's own internal
failure onto one.

Proposition 8.6a.6 (Coupled Recovery Is Faster and More Reliable, Not
Categorically Required). Solo recovery remains genuinely possible: a
single container can and does reach real, low-entropy baseline through
its own unforced B2 reorganization (§10.5b.9) alone, with no coupling
required. What genuine, active coupling with an anchor (§10.5b.13a) or
another healthily-reorganizing container (Proposition 8.6a.5) adds is
not a categorically different endpoint but real gains in speed and
reliability: the shock-absorption and effective-h\_min benefits of
§10.5b.0.4a's own network result apply during the recovery process
itself when coupling is present, reducing the chance of a cycle
resolving toward B1 instead of B2 and shortening the time a given
reorganization takes to complete. Recovery undertaken together is
therefore not a different destination from recovery undertaken alone; it
is a more dependable and quicker route to the same one.

Proposition 8.6a.7 (Matrix Formation Through Active Mutual Healing, Not
Only Pre-Existing Similarity). A horizontal matrix (§10.5b.0.4a) need
not form only from containers that happen to already share comparable
entropy status; it can grow through low-entropy nodes actively engaging
their own reciprocal-dissipation capacity (Proposition 8.6a.5) to help a
coupled medium-entropy neighbor's own recovery specifically, per
Proposition 8.6a.6's speed and reliability gains, down toward
low-entropy status and genuine matrix membership. This is a real growth
mechanism for an existing matrix, not merely a description of how one
first assembles: the matrix's own already-low-entropy members are a real
resource for bringing new members in through active, reciprocal help,
rather than the matrix being limited to whoever already qualified for
membership before joining.

\textbf{Link-severing as the high-entropy container's own collapse
trigger.}

If j successfully transitions to the low-entropy baseline regime, ρ(j)
falls, the coupling weakens (§8.7's rewiring), and X\_\{i,j\} → 0. If i
has no other available coupled target, its heat can no longer be vented
externally --- it has nowhere left to go but back into i's own intrinsic
dynamics, directly increasing the F\_i term already identified in §10.5b
as high-entropy tipping's driving force. This closes a mechanistic loop:
a successfully-decoupling neighbor is not a bystander to a high-entropy
container's eventual collapse, it is a direct contributor to it, and
severing an extraction link is simultaneously a self-protective act for
the decoupling container and a destabilizing one for the container it
decouples from.

\hypertarget{adaptive-rewiring}{%
\subsection{8.7 Adaptive Rewiring}\label{adaptive-rewiring}}

dw\_ij/dt = Γ\_w(I(P), Π\_f, R, M) --- this Part's specific
model-derived instantiation of §9.8's general dependency, with
informational state entering through the already-computed integrity
scalar I(P) rather than the full distribution P.

\textbf{Computational instantiation.}

w\_ij(t+Δt) = max(0, w\_ij(t) + β\_net·(successful joint updates) −
γ\_decay·(ignored feedback across the edge)), the scalar edge weight
(distinct from §8.2's interaction tuple W\_ij\^{}int) and its own decay
coefficient (distinct from §8.5's coupling-gain coefficient γ\_Λ), where
``successful joint update'' and ``ignored feedback'' are counted from
realized decreases versus persistent increases in D\_ij·(1−Π\_f) across
the edge over the step --- the same update rule as §8.5's Π\_f-mediated
correction (§6.4), applied to edge weight instead of node-level state,
since RICD already has per-container Π\_f and needs the analogous update
for the coupling strength between containers specifically.

\hypertarget{cascading-dynamics}{%
\subsection{8.8 Cascading Dynamics}\label{cascading-dynamics}}

Let δ\_i(t) be a localized disturbance. Δ(t) = L\_prop(𝒢\_net(t), δ),
where L\_prop is the propagation operator. Depending upon network
topology, identical local disturbances may dissipate, remain localized,
amplify, or cascade globally.

\textbf{Computational instantiation.}

Implemented directly by simulating §8.5's corrected, commensurated
dP\_i/dt system forward from an initial perturbation at container i; no
separate propagation operator needs to be built, since Λ\_ij already
carries disturbances between containers by construction. L\_prop is
therefore not a new primitive --- it is what the already-specified
system does when integrated.

\hypertarget{a-the-cascading-grid-failure-sequence}{%
\subsection{8.8a The Cascading Grid Failure
Sequence}\label{a-the-cascading-grid-failure-sequence}}

The propagation operator defined in §8.8 describes how localized
disturbances move through a network. It does not, by itself, distinguish
between three qualitatively different propagation regimes: dissipation,
amplification, and cascading grid failure. The distinction arises not
from the topology alone, but from the regime composition of the
containers involved and the sequential coupling of extraction,
decoupling, venting, and tipping. This section formalizes the specific
sequence by which a single high-entropy (high-entropy) container can
trigger a grid-level cascade, and the conditions under which that
cascade terminates in fragmentation rather than reorganization.

\textbf{8.8a.i The Cascade-Initiating Event}

Let container i be in the high-entropy regime (§10.5b), with low
official-layer variance relative to operational-layer variance (σ\_o(i)
≪ σ\_p(i)), active extractive coupling (§8.6a) to one or more neighbors
j ∈ N\_i(t), and a positive vented heat term H\_i,j(t) =
(1−η\_i)·X\_\{i,j\}(t) for at least one coupled neighbor j.

Let at least one such neighbor j decouple from i at time t\_d: coupling
persistence collapses (ρ(j) → 0), the extraction flux vanishes
(X\_\{i,j\}(t) → 0 for t \textgreater{} t\_d), and the edge w\_ij begins
to decay via the rewiring rule of §8.7.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.8a.1 (Decoupling-Triggered Heat
Accumulation).}}

Define the container's own internal heat-generation rate, driven by its
operational divergence rather than by active extraction:

Ω\_i(t) = κ\_Ω · D\_op(i,t), κ\_Ω \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

a real, standing quantity that exists regardless of coupling status ---
a high-entropy container's own corrupted operational layer continues
generating internal chaos needing release whether or not it currently
has anyone to extract from. While coupled (N\_i(t) ≠ ∅), this pressure
is vented as fast as it is generated by displacement onto live neighbors
(Definition 8.8a.3c), not through extraction heat; the accumulator below
is defined only over the genuinely disconnected interval, where
generation continues but venting cannot occur:

\emph{ℐ}\_i(t) = ∫\_\{t\_d\}\^{}\{t\} Ω\_i(s)·𝟙{[}N\_i(s) = ∅{]} ds,

now a genuinely nonzero, strictly increasing quantity whenever
D\_op(i,t) \textgreater{} 0 during isolation, distinct from H\_i(t) (the
instantaneous venting rate of §8.6a, well-defined and nonzero only while
coupled) and from the Ω\_i(t) that feeds it (the rate of generation,
which exists in both regimes but only accumulates when venting is
unavailable).

\emph{\textbf{Lemma 8.8a.1 (Internal Forcing Escalation).}}

For a high-entropy container i, ℐ\_i(t) is a strictly increasing
function of the duration t − t\_d for which N\_i(t) = ∅, provided
D\_op(i,t) \textgreater{} 0 --- not H\_i(t) \textgreater{} 0, which
cannot hold during exactly this interval, since H\_i is the venting
rate, zero by definition whenever N\_i(t) = ∅ (§8.6a). D\_op(i,t)
\textgreater{} 0 is the condition that actually governs Ω\_i(t)'s own
generation rate, and is the real gate on whether ℐ\_i(t) can accumulate
at all. Moreover, ℐ\_i(t) feeds directly into the intrinsic driving term
F\_i(t) of §8.5:

\emph{F}\_i(t) = F\_i(0) + λ\_ℐ · ℐ\_i(t),

where λ\_ℐ \textgreater{} 0 is a fitted coupling constant. Thus, as
targets decouple, the container's own internal oscillation intensifies
--- it is not ``calming down''; it is accelerating toward its own
tipping event.

\textbf{8.8a.ii The Frantic Striking Phase}

As ℐ\_i(t) increases and F\_i(t) escalates, the high-entropy container
enters a frantic striking phase.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.8a.2 (Frantic Striking).}}

A container i is in a frantic striking phase when ℐ\_i(t) exceeds a
fitted threshold ℐ\_threshold (§15.3); N\_i(t) ≠ ∅ but the set of
coupled neighbors is decreasing; and Λ\_ij(t) increases in magnitude for
remaining neighbors, even as w\_ij(t) decays, because the forcing per
edge increases. This is not strategic or adaptive behavior. It is a
thermodynamic discharge --- the container is venting internally
accumulated heat outward, through whatever remaining channels exist. The
transition into this phase is itself a discrete, explicit event, not an
implicit consequence left for an implementer to infer: while N\_i(t) =
∅, ℐ\_i(t) accumulates per Definition 8.8a.1 but the container remains
in ordinary isolation, not frantic striking, regardless of how far
ℐ\_i(t) has risen --- accumulation alone does not trigger discharge. The
formal transition condition is N\_i(t\^{}+) ≠ ∅ ∧ ℐ\_i(t\^{}+)
\textgreater{} ℐ\_threshold ⟹ enter Frantic Striking at t\^{}+, firing
at the first moment a new coupled neighbor becomes available while the
accumulated threshold is already exceeded --- whether that neighbor
arrives through ordinary network rewiring (§8.7) or the container's own
active search (§10.5b.11c). Without this explicit condition, ℐ\_i(t)
could remain elevated indefinitely through complete isolation with no
formal event ever activating discharge once a target does appear; this
closes that gap. A second, independent route into the same behavioral
pattern exists, distinct from the isolation-and-recoupling route above:
an unabsorbed Resolution Kick (§8.8b) landing on an already-coupled
container directly raises that container's own oscillation, σ\_o(t) or
σ\_p(t) (§10.5b.9's trailing-window variance, whichever layer the kicks
accurate content actually reaches) --- a real, sudden destabilization
independent of and additional to whatever isolation-driven heat may or
may not already be accumulating. Where this kick-driven rise itself
exceeds a fitted threshold Θ\_rock, the container enters frantic
striking directly, without requiring N\_i(t) = ∅ to have occurred at
all: σ\_o(t\^{}+) − σ\_o(t\^{}−) \textgreater{} Θ\_rock (or the
analogous σ\_p condition) ⇒ enter Frantic Striking at t\^{}+. The
discharge in this case is not necessarily directed back at the container
that sent the kick; per the vector sum below, it distributes across
whatever coupled neighbors N\_i(t) currently exist, which may include
containers with no involvement in the original extraction at all --- the
formal content of a destabilized predator striking at any nearby
neighbor specifically to discharge its own increased rocking, not in
retaliation against whoever caused it.

\emph{Domain instances.} Failing firms making desperate acquisitions and
filing indiscriminate litigation; failing states striking out at
neighbors; a collapsing power grid shedding load erratically across its
connections.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 8.8a.1 (Frantic Striking as Network
Forcing).}}

During a frantic striking phase, the vector sum of forcings from i to
its remaining neighbors N\_i(t) is:

\emph{Λ⃗}\_i(t) = Σ\_\{j∈N\_i(t)\} Λ\_ij(t)·û\_ij(t),

where û\_ij(t) is the unit direction of the disturbance vector from i to
j within the shared severity simplex Δ\^{}m established in §8.5. This
vector sum is typically non-zero and large, characterized by divergent
directions because i is striking out at multiple neighbors
simultaneously --- there is no single ``target direction'' because the
heat must be discharged in all available directions.

\textbf{8.8a.iii The Medium-Entropy Launch}

These frantic strikes land on the remaining coupled neighbors. The
containers most affected are those in the medium-entropy
(medium-entropy) regime, because they remain coupled longer (ρ(j) is
high for medium-entropy containers), they have large operational mass
(μ\_p \textgreater{} μ\_o, per §10.5b), making them kinetically
responsive to external forcing, and they are visibly chaotic at the
official layer (σ\_o high), which makes them appear as ``active''
participants in the dynamics, even though their operational core is
calm.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.8a.3 (Medium-Entropy Launch).}}

A medium-entropy container j is launched when it receives a
multi-directional forcing input from multiple high-entropy neighbors:

Drive\_B(j,t) = Σ\_\{k∈H\_j(t)\} Λ\_kj(t)·û\_kj(t) ,

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.8a.3a (Heat-to-Forcing Conversion).}} The
vented heat of §8.6a is what pushes neighbors, and this definition
supplies the conversion. Let H\_k\^{}ext(t) = Σ\_j (1 −
η\_k)·X\_\{k,j\}(t) be container k's extraction heat, its unmetabolized
extraction, and Y\_\{k,j\}(t) the internal pressure it displaces onto
neighbor j (Definition 8.8a.3c). With the allocation a\_kj = w\_kj /
Σ\_l w\_kl when Σ\_l w\_kl \textgreater{} 0 and a\_kj = 0 otherwise, the
forcing k exerts on a coupled neighbor j is

F\_\{k→j\}(t) = κ\_H·{[} H\_k\^{}ext(t)·a\_kj(t) + Y\_\{k,j\}(t)
{]}·û\_kj(t), û\_kj(t) = (σ\_k(P\_k) − σ\_j(P\_j)) / ‖σ\_k(P\_k) −
σ\_j(P\_j)‖,

with κ\_H \textgreater{} 0 fitted: the extraction heat is divided among
k's neighbors in proportion to coupling weight, the displaced pressure
goes to the neighbor it was displaced onto, and each pushes the
neighbor's state toward k's own disordered state in the shared severity
simplex, the direction §8.5's propagation already uses. In the Drive\_B
formula above, Λ\_kj(t)·û\_kj(t) is F\_\{k→j\}(t). Where Σ\_l w\_kl = 0
no extraction heat is routed, and where σ\_k = σ\_j the direction is
undefined and the term is zero. The heat leaves the resource ledger as
this forcing, which is its dissipation sink.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.8a.3b (Net Push and Total Load).}} Drive\_B
is a vector, and two scalars are taken from it, carrying different
consequences. The net push N\_B(j,t) = χ\_dir(j,t)·‖Drive\_B(j,t)‖ is
the magnitude of the resultant, so simultaneous forces from opposite
directions cancel; it drives tipping in a direction (Lemma 8.8a.2,
§10.5b.7). The total load L\_B(j,t) = Σ\_k ‖F\_\{k→j\}(t)‖ is the sum of
the individual magnitudes, so nothing cancels; it drives stress and
structural degradation, including multi-source disintegration
(§10.5b.9d). Both accumulate over time, so repeated knocks add up
(Proposition 10.5b.9f.3), and a container knocked from opposing sides
can degrade or shatter through L\_B while N\_B never tips it.

\hypertarget{a.3c-displacement-venting-and-the-predation-share}{%
\subsection{8.8a.3c Displacement Venting and the Predation
Share}\label{a.3c-displacement-venting-and-the-predation-share}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.8a.3c (Displacement Venting and the Predation
Share).}}

Units. Every heat and pressure flow in this item is expressed in
resource-equivalent units per unit time, the units of the resource
balance (§7.4), so that flows can be added: κ\_Ω converts divergence
into a resource-equivalent rate, and the displacement flux Y is a
resource-equivalent rate of pressure discharged. Heat and pressure are
nevertheless never entered in a resource balance; the units only make
them commensurable with one another and with the costs they induce.
§8.8a.1\textquotesingle s internal heat Ω\_i(t) = κ\_Ω·D\_op(i,t) is
pressure generated by a high-entropy container\textquotesingle s
corrupted operational layer. While the container is coupled, it vents
that pressure by displacement onto neighbors (the displacement flux
Y\_\{i,k\} of Definition 8.6a.10), not through its extraction heat;
while uncoupled, it accumulates as ℐ\_i(t) (Definition 8.8a.1). With
N\_i(t) the set of i\textquotesingle s live neighbors (Ξ \textgreater{}
θ, §8.3) and displacement fluxes Y\_\{i,k\}(t) ≥ 0 defined for k ∈
N\_i(t), the coupled-case venting assumption becomes

Σ\_k Y\_\{i,k\}(t) = Ω\_i(t) while N\_i(t) ≠ ∅,

replacing §8.8a.1\textquotesingle s statement that the pressure is
vented through ordinary extraction. This is a closure assumption, that
while coupled all internally generated pressure is discharged by
displacement, not a consequence of the other equations. Let X\_i(t) be
the set of containers i extracts from. The predation share

π\_pred(i,t) = Σ\_\{k ∈ X\_i(t) ∩ N\_i(t)\} Y\_\{i,k\}(t) / Σ\_\{k ∈
N\_i(t)\} Y\_\{i,k\}(t) ∈ {[}0, 1{]}

is the share of displaced pressure that lands on the very containers i
extracts from, delivered through their destruction. It is defined only
when Σ\_k Y\_\{i,k\} \textgreater{} 0, which excludes both a container
with no neighbors and one with no pressure to vent (D\_op = 0, so Ω\_i =
0). And it classifies a relationship only where extraction is occurring:
if X\_i(t) is empty, π\_pred = 0 but there is no extraction to call
parasitic, and the share is not used. π\_pred = 0 is pure parasitism:
clean resource is extracted from one set of neighbors while pressure is
displaced onto others (role-splitting, Definition 8.6a.10). π\_pred = 1
is pure predation: the destruction of the target is both the extraction
and the discharge (Proposition 8.6a.4). The share is continuous in the
flows, while the set of extraction targets is fixed and the denominator
positive, because the line between the two is thin: a container can be
partly parasitic and partly predatory toward the same neighbor. It can
jump when a target is added to or dropped from X\_i(t).

\textbf{Definition 8.8a.3c.1 (Generated and Delivered Heat).}
§8.6a\textquotesingle s extraction heat is unchanged, H\_i\^{}ext(t) =
Σ\_j (1 − η\_i)·X\_\{i,j\}(t). The total discharge i generates is

H\_i\^{}tot(t) = H\_i\^{}ext(t) + Σ\_k Y\_\{i,k\}(t),

and §8.8a.3a\textquotesingle s conversion to forcing keeps the two parts
with their own destinations: extraction heat is distributed by the
coupling weights, and displaced pressure goes where it was displaced,

F\_\{i→k\}(t) = κ\_H·{[} H\_i\^{}ext(t)·w\_ik(t) / Σ\_l w\_il(t) +
Y\_\{i,k\}(t) {]}·û\_ik(t),

so no neighbor receives pressure that was displaced onto another.
Displacement runs only along live edges (Y\_\{i,k\} \textgreater{} 0
only if Ξ(C\_i, C\_k) \textgreater{} θ, §8.3), and where i has no
weighted neighbor (Σ\_l w\_il = 0) the extraction-heat term is zero.
Writing the allocation a\_ik = w\_ik / Σ\_l w\_il when Σ\_l w\_il
\textgreater{} 0 and a\_ik = 0 when Σ\_l w\_il = 0 (weights
non-negative), the forcing\textquotesingle s first term is
H\_i\^{}ext·a\_ik, and the total discharge i delivers to neighbors is
the scalar sum over destinations, H\_i\^{}del = Σ\_k (H\_i\^{}ext·a\_ik
+ Y\_\{i,k\}). It equals the generated total H\_i\^{}tot when i has a
weighted neighbor, and is Σ\_k Y\_\{i,k\} when it has none, in which
case the extraction heat is generated but not delivered, while
internally generated pressure is still delivered to any live neighbors.
Both are sums of magnitudes, not the vector sum of the forces, which
differ in direction û\_ik; it is not a quantity redistributed by the
weights. \textquotesingle Heat\textquotesingle{} here is
RICD\textquotesingle s term in §8.6a\textquotesingle s broad sense,
discharged disorder that becomes forcing on neighbors; extraction waste
and displaced pressure are different kinds of discharge, made
commensurable by their units, not the same physical energy. The two
terms are distinct flows (the waste of what was extracted, and the
pressure generated internally) that may arrive at the same target
through the same act, so each is counted once.

\textbf{Definition 8.8a.3c.2 (The Extraction and Heat Ledger).} Every
flow in an extraction relationship, with its source, its destination and
what kind of quantity it is:

(1) Extraction X\_\{i,j\}: the gross resource removed from j, reducing
j\textquotesingle s balance by X. The gross amount is allocated between
the share i retains, (2), and the dissipated share, (3); it is not
separately added to i. A resource transfer.

(2) Metabolized share η\_i·X\_\{i,j\}: retained by i as usable capacity
(i\textquotesingle s balance +η\_i·X), the sole addition to
i\textquotesingle s resources. A resource transfer.

(3) Extraction waste (1 − η\_i)·X\_\{i,j\}: not retained; becomes heat
H\^{}ext. A dissipation that becomes forcing.

(4) Extraction performance cost M\_extract (§7.9.1): drawn from
i\textquotesingle s own balance. A resource cost.

(5) Internal pressure Ω\_i = κ\_Ω·D\_op: generated by
i\textquotesingle s divergence, not by any resource flow. A pressure,
accumulated as ℐ\_i when it cannot be vented.

(6) Displacement Y\_\{i,k\}: the vented pressure; a share π\_pred lands
on extraction targets and 1 − π\_pred on displacement-only neighbors. A
dissipation that becomes forcing.

(7) Forcing F\_\{k→j\} = κ\_H·{[}H\_k\^{}ext·a\_kj + Y\_\{k,j\}{]}·û\_kj
(§8.8a.3a), extraction heat apportioned by coupling weight and displaced
pressure delivered to its destination: acts on j\textquotesingle s
informational state P\_j, not on its resources directly. An
informational effect, whose energy-equivalent cost j pays through the
stress, divergence cost and repair it induces (M\_diverge, §7.9.1),
never as a second deduction of the resource already counted in (1).

The extraction is accounted for in full, X = η·X + (1 − η)·X: X leaves j
once; η·X is retained by i; (1 − η)·X is not retained by anyone and
leaves the resource accounting as dissipation, recorded as the
extraction heat (3). Pressure (5) is generated, not transferred, and
leaves i as displacement (6). Heat (3) and (6) and forcing (7) are never
added to a resource balance; their effect on resources runs only through
the costs they induce. Each heat flow is counted once. {[}Structural.{]}

where H\_j(t) is the set of high-entropy neighbors of j at time t. Per
§10.5b, this is the vector-aware coupling: a single knock does not
literally cancel, since a lone vector simply contributes its own
magnitude --- rather, a single source lacks the multi-directional
reinforcement represented by the vector sum Σ Λ\_kj û\_kj, while
multiple sources with divergent vectors can reinforce into a resultant
substantially larger than any one source's own magnitude.

\emph{\textbf{Lemma 8.8a.2 (Launch Threshold).}}

A medium-entropy container j tips into a tipping event (per §10.5a) by
either of two routes. Sustained route: N\_B(j,t) \textgreater{}
Θ\_launch for some fitted threshold Θ\_launch, continuously for a
duration τ ≥ τ\_launch, τ\_launch ≥ 0 fitted --- an explicit duration
gate replacing the earlier qualitative ``sufficient duration,''
evaluable directly rather than left as narrative. Impulse route:
∫\_\{t−w\_imp\}\^{}\{t\} N\_B(j,s) ds ≥ J\_crit within a short window
w\_imp \textless{} τ\_launch, w\_imp and J\_crit fitted, a single sudden
shock large enough to tip the container at once. The two routes are
distinct hypotheses, not equivalent forms: a sustained force implies the
integrated condition, but a brief large spike satisfies the impulse
route without satisfying the sustained one. A tipping event occurs when
either is met, and an implementation records which route fired. The
disruption-history accumulator of §10.5b.9a.5 lowers both thresholds for
recurring forms of disruption.

\textbf{8.8a.iv The Bifurcation of Medium-Entropy Tipping}

Once launched, the medium-entropy container follows Flip Condition B
(§10.5b), which bifurcates into two distinct outcomes. B1: D\_om → 0,
engaging the minority (official/meta) mass μ\_o, resulting in a terminal
lock at 180° --- the container becomes frozen, requiring external
anchor. B2: D\_pm → 0, engaging the majority (operational/meta) mass
μ\_p, resulting in a full 360° reorganization --- stress and debt reset;
adaptive capacity recovers.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 8.8a.2 (Cascade Propagation via B1 Locking).}}

A medium-entropy container that undergoes B1 locking severs its
remaining couplings (per §8.7) as its adaptive capacity collapses,
ceases to be a sink for extraction heat as its ρ(j) drops to zero, and
transitions from a coupled neighbor to a frozen node that no longer
participates in network dynamics. This severs one more extraction
channel for the original high-entropy container, accelerating its own
heat accumulation and eventual collapse.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 8.8a.3 (Cascade Damping via B2
Reorganization).}}

A medium-entropy container that undergoes B2 reorganization resets its
stress S(t) and debt δR(t) toward zero, recovers adaptive capacity
d\_A(t), and may re-enter the low-entropy baseline regime if the
reorganization is sustained. This container ceases to be a source of
sustained lateral knocks and may become a stabilizing anchor (§10.5b)
for other containers.

\textbf{8.8a.v Grid-Level Fragmentation}

The cascade propagates as: high-entropy containers decouple from their
targets, strike out frantically, launch medium-entropy neighbors, and
those neighbors either lock (B1) or reorganize (B2). Locked nodes are
removed from the active network; reorganized nodes may stabilize. The
network's algebraic connectivity is driven downward as locked nodes
accumulate and their incident edges are removed; whether this reaches
λ₂(L(t)) → 0 (Definition 8.8a.4's own threshold) depends on the topology
and sequence of removals, not on node loss alone --- a claim of
universal decline regardless of structure would overstate what node
removal by itself establishes.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.8a.4 (Cascading Grid Failure).}}

Cascading grid failure occurs when λ₂(L(t)) → 0 and the network
fragments into disconnected components --- the consequence of
high-entropy containers losing extraction targets and self-accelerating,
medium-entropy containers locking (B1) faster than they reorganize (B2),
and the network's capacity for information and resource exchange falling
below the minimum required for coherence (§12.1, Criterion 12.G).

\emph{\textbf{Theorem 8.8a.1 (Cascade Termination Conditions).}}

A cascading grid failure terminates under one of the following
conditions: external intervention (an anchoring container, per §10.5b,
stabilizes high-entropy containers, preventing further frantic
striking); B2-dominated reorganization (a sufficient fraction of
medium-entropy containers complete B2 reorganization and re-enter the
low-entropy baseline, restoring connectivity); or complete fragmentation
(the network fragments until no remaining container has a coupled
neighbor to launch, and the cascade stops --- this is not recovery, it
is dissolution). Fragmentation into several components does not by
itself end the cascade: within any component that still holds a
high-entropy container and coupled medium-entropy neighbors it can
continue, and these conditions apply component by component. The
conditions under which each outcome occurs are determined by the
relative rates of B1 versus B2 transitions, governed by the fraction of
high-entropy to medium-entropy containers in the network, the ρ(j)
persistence of medium-entropy containers, the availability of external
anchors, and the fitted thresholds M\_lock and M\_c of §10.5b.

\textbf{8.8a.vi Observational Signatures}

The cascade sequence produces testable signatures distinguishing it from
simple network degradation. Pre-cascade: a high-entropy container shows
σ\_o low, σ\_p high, and a declining number of coupled neighbors.
Frantic striking onset: multiple medium-entropy containers show a
simultaneous spike in Drive\_B(j,t) from the same high-entropy source.
Bifurcation: locked nodes (B1) show D\_om → 0, I(t) → 0, and d\_A(t)
collapse; reorganized nodes (B2) show D\_pm → 0, S(t) and δR(t) reset.
Grid-level fragmentation: λ₂(L(t)) tends to decline, though not
necessarily monotonically, since removing a node can raise the algebraic
connectivity of what remains, until either stabilization or
fragmentation. These signatures are distinct from those of isolated
container collapse (§10.5b) and can be used to detect cascading failure
in progress, not just after the fact.

\textbf{8.8a.vii Relation to Existing Sections}

This section does not introduce new mathematics beyond ℐ\_i(t). It
provides a formal narrative connecting §8.6a (extractive coupling, heat
venting, decoupling), §8.7 (adaptive rewiring, edge decay), §8.8
(propagation operator, cascading dynamics), §8.9 (network resilience,
algebraic connectivity), §8.11 (network phase transitions,
fragmentation), §10.5b (regime classification, Flip Conditions A and B,
B1/B2 bifurcation), and §11.10 (collapse potential, network coherence).
The cascade sequence is the mechanistic link between these previously
separate layers.

\textbf{8.8a.viii Computational Instantiation}

For an implementation, the cascade sequence is simulated by tracking
ℐ\_i(t) and N\_i(t) for each high-entropy container; computing
Drive\_B(j,t) for each medium-entropy container; simulating the B1/B2
bifurcation per §10.5b; updating the network graph 𝒢\_net(t) and
computing λ₂(L(t)) at each timestep; and detecting cascading grid
failure when λ₂(L(t)) \textless{} λ\_threshold (fitted). No new
integration scheme is required. The cascade emerges from the existing
coupled differential equations when the conditions above are satisfied.

\hypertarget{network-resilience}{%
\subsection{8.9 Network Resilience}\label{network-resilience}}

Define the network resilience functional R\_N = Ψ(G, W, P, R). High
values indicate that information and resources continue to circulate
effectively following perturbation.

\textbf{Computational instantiation.}

R\_N(t) = λ₂(L(t)) · mean(Π\_f across containers), where λ₂(L) is the
algebraic connectivity of the Laplacian of the symmetrized weighted
graph, W\_sym = (W + Wᵀ)/2 --- coupling in this document can be directed
(Λ\_kl ≠ Λ\_lk), and the second-smallest eigenvalue measures
connectivity only for a symmetric Laplacian --- zero exactly when the
graph is disconnected, increasing with connectivity --- scaled by mean
permeability, since a well-connected network of containers that do not
absorb feedback is not actually resilient in RICD's sense. Network
coherence N (§11.8) is set equal to a normalized R\_N ∈ {[}0,1{]} (R\_N
divided by its value on the complete graph with Π\_f = 1) so N and R\_N
are not two independently-defined quantities. The comparison ensemble is
fixed explicitly: the complete graph on the same node set, under the
same total edge-weight budget as the actual network, with that budget
distributed uniformly across every edge of the complete graph ---
admissible only for n ≥ 2 nodes and a positive total comparison
edge-weight budget, since a one-node networks complete graph has
algebraic connectivity zero by definition, and a zero total weight
budget gives the comparison graph zero λ₂ as well, both producing 0/0 in
the normalization below; N(t) is defined as 0 for either degenerate case
rather than left undefined --- not an unconstrained complete graph with
unlimited per-edge weight, and not left free to any other allocation ---
so N measures how well a network uses the connectivity it actually has,
rather than comparing it to a physically unrealistic maximum or an
arbitrarily-weighted one. This fully pins down λ₂ of the comparison
graph: same node set, same total weight, uniform per-edge allocation, no
remaining degree of freedom in how the comparison ensemble is
constructed. This choice of comparison ensemble --- specifically, that
the same total edge-weight budget is the legitimate counterfactual to
hold fixed while asking how well it could have been distributed --- is a
modeling convention, not a mathematical necessity forced by the
definition of algebraic connectivity itself; an application with a
principled reason to hold a different quantity fixed across the
comparison (per-edge weight cap, total node degree, or another resource
constraint entirely) would need its own separately-justified ensemble,
and N(t)'s numeric value is only meaningful relative to whichever
ensemble is actually in force for a given application.

\hypertarget{recursive-networks}{%
\subsection{8.10 Recursive Networks}\label{recursive-networks}}

G\^{}(0), G\^{}(1), G\^{}(2), \ldots{} represent networks operating at
different organizational scales, interacting through the recursive
operators of Part 5.

\hypertarget{network-phase-transitions}{%
\subsection{8.11 Network Phase
Transitions}\label{network-phase-transitions}}

As informational integrity declines or resources become depleted,
gradual changes in local interactions may accumulate into abrupt changes
in global topology --- fragmentation into disconnected components,
collapse of communication pathways, emergence of isolated clusters,
runaway synchronization, centralized bottlenecks. These transitions
arise from the coupled evolution of informational dynamics, resource
constraints, and adaptive rewiring rather than from topology alone.

\textbf{The Network Principle.}

Adaptive networks are continuously evolving organizations whose topology
emerges from informational compatibility, resource exchange, historical
memory, and boundary-mediated interaction.

\hypertarget{part-9.-memory-dynamics}{%
\section{Part 9. Memory Dynamics}\label{part-9.-memory-dynamics}}

Adaptive systems are fundamentally historical. RICD treats memory as an
active operator that continuously modifies informational evolution,
resource allocation, network topology, and recursive organization.

\hypertarget{historical-trajectory}{%
\subsection{9.1 Historical Trajectory}\label{historical-trajectory}}

H\_t = \{P(s) : 0 ≤ s ≤ t\}. Future evolution depends upon H\_t, not
solely upon P(t).

\hypertarget{memory-operator}{%
\subsection{9.2 Memory Operator}\label{memory-operator}}

Definition 2.5 (Part 2) established the memory operator: M\_t : H\_t →
𝒫(Ω). Where this document writes M\_t in a formula, it denotes the value
of that operator at time t --- the container's realized memory state,
given the trajectory actually available up to t --- rather than a fresh
family of operators; the notation is unchanged from Definition 2.5, and
this is a clarification of reading, not a redefinition.

M\_t does not reproduce history exactly; it constructs an adaptive
representation of history. Memory is selective, compressive, and
reconstructive.

\hypertarget{memory-kernel}{%
\subsection{9.3 Memory Kernel}\label{memory-kernel}}

K(t, s) ≥ 0 for 0 ≤ s ≤ t. Normalization requirement, corrected. A bare
M\_t = ∫₀ᵗ K(t, s) P(s) ds does not in general remain in 𝒫(Ω): with the
default exponential kernel K(t,s) = λ·e\^{}\{−λ(t−s)\}, the kernel's own
integral over a finite window {[}0,t{]} is only 1−e\^{}\{−λt\}, not 1,
so even where every P(s) is a genuine probability distribution the
resulting M\_t is a sub-probability measure rather than an element of
𝒫(Ω) --- a real problem given that g\_\{C,t\} = g(M\_\{C,t\}) (§4.10)
requires M\_t to be a valid point on the probability manifold.
Corrected: an initial-memory boundary term restores exact normalization
for any K satisfying the stated decay form, M\_t = e\^{}\{−λt\}·M\_0 +
∫₀ᵗ λ·e\^{}\{−λ(t−s)\}·P(s) ds, with M\_0 ∈ 𝒫(Ω) adapter-supplied
(defaulting to P(0) where no independent prior memory state exists),
since e\^{}\{−λt\} + ∫₀ᵗ λ·e\^{}\{−λ(t−s)\} ds = 1 for every t ≥ 0, and
the two terms are jointly a convex combination of elements of 𝒫(Ω),
which is itself convex, so M\_t ∈ 𝒫(Ω) is exact rather than approximate
at every t. This is the concrete instantiation of Definition 2.5's
operator meeting its own stated codomain, not merely giving M\_t's value
explicitly. Examples of K include exponential decay, power-law memory,
oscillatory memory, finite retention windows, and multi-timescale
kernels; §6.7 and §9.7 adopt the exponential form as the default for
computational use, and any alternative kernel substituted for it must
satisfy the same normalization requirement --- an equivalent boundary
term sized to its own total mass over {[}0,t{]}, not necessarily the
exponential's specific e\^{}\{−λt\} coefficient.

\hypertarget{memory-plasticity}{%
\subsection{9.4 Memory Plasticity}\label{memory-plasticity}}

dM/dt = Υ(P, R, Π\_f), where Υ denotes the plasticity operator (distinct
from information state P and production rate P\_prod --- three uses of
the letter P now appear across this framework and must be disambiguated
by context or, in application-specific derivations, by distinct
subscripts). Read per §9.2, M here denotes the evolving value M\_t, and
dM/dt its rate of change as the underlying history evolves.

Since M\_t is a probability distribution, any implementation of Υ must
preserve the simplex: its components sum to zero, and a component of M
at zero has a nonnegative rate.

\hypertarget{memory-compression}{%
\subsection{9.5 Memory Compression}\label{memory-compression}}

C\_M : H\_t → H̃\_t, where H̃\_t is a lower-dimensional adaptive
representation of the historical trajectory. Compression sacrifices
detail while preserving information judged relevant for future
adaptation.

\hypertarget{memory-reconstruction}{%
\subsection{9.6 Memory Reconstruction}\label{memory-reconstruction}}

R\_M : H̃\_t → P̃. The reconstructed state P̃ may differ from the original
historical state because reconstruction is influenced by current
informational organization, accumulated experience, and contextual cues.

\hypertarget{memory-and-informational-stress}{%
\subsection{9.7 Memory and Informational
Stress}\label{memory-and-informational-stress}}

S(t) = ∫₀ᵗ K(t, s) (1 − Π\_f(s)) D(s) ds. Stress represents a
memory-weighted accumulation of unresolved informational discrepancies,
emerging from the interaction between memory and feedback dynamics
rather than existing independently.

\hypertarget{memory-and-network-evolution}{%
\subsection{9.8 Memory and Network
Evolution}\label{memory-and-network-evolution}}

dw\_ij/dt = Γ\_w(P, R, M, Π\_f) --- the general, structurally-derived
functional dependency: repeated successful interaction strengthens
coupling; repeated unresolved divergence weakens it. §8.7 gives this
Part's specific model-derived instantiation, with informational state
entering through I(P) rather than the raw distribution P directly.

\hypertarget{recursive-memory}{%
\subsection{9.9 Recursive Memory}\label{recursive-memory}}

M\^{}(0), M\^{}(1), M\^{}(2), \ldots{} represent memories operating
simultaneously across organizational scales --- cellular memory,
individual learning, organizational culture, institutional precedent,
evolutionary history.

\hypertarget{part-10.-adaptive-stability-and-collapse}{%
\section{Part 10. Adaptive Stability and
Collapse}\label{part-10.-adaptive-stability-and-collapse}}

Readers Guide: How Part 10 Fits Together. This part contains most of the
frameworks collapse machinery, built up across many subsections; a
reader encountering it for the first time may find the cross-reference
density disorienting without a map. The core causal chain, stripped of
the real complexity each step actually contains, runs as follows: (1)
Hull Breach (§10.5a) --- a single, checkable scalar condition
determining whether a container has crossed from ordinary oscillation
into a genuine tipping event, the one gate everything downstream depends
on being crossed first. (2) Entropy Regime Classification (§10.5b.1) ---
once tipping is confirmed, which of three regimes (low-entropy baseline,
high-entropy, medium-entropy) the container occupies, determined by
σ\_o/σ\_p, not by anything downstream of it. (3) Regime-specific
resolution mechanics --- high-entropy containers resolve through Flip
Condition A (§10.5b.7--§10.5b.9, generally terminal, with real anchoring
and Systems Override exceptions covered separately); medium-entropy
containers resolve through Flip Condition Bs B1/B2 bifurcation
(§10.5b.7), the mass-weighted split between terminal lock (B1) and full
reorganization (B2). (4) Everything else in Part 10 --- extraction
mechanics, cascading dynamics, the metamessage/firewall material, the Do
No Harm Principle, resilience and backslide dynamics --- either feeds
into one of these three steps (as a real, specific cause or modifier) or
describes what happens once one of the two outcomes is reached. When a
subsection feels disconnected from the main argument, checking which of
these four steps it actually feeds is usually enough to place it. Full
development reasoning for individual definitions has been moved to the
companion RICD Development Notes document, referenced by section number
where relevant, to keep this main text focused on the argument itself.

Adaptive systems continually reorganize in response to internal dynamics
and environmental perturbation. Collapse is not the opposite of
stability; it is the progressive loss of the adaptive mechanisms that
make stability possible.

\hypertarget{adaptive-state-space}{%
\subsection{10.1 Adaptive State Space}\label{adaptive-state-space}}

A container's realized state is X(t) = (P, R, B, M) (Definition 2.2):
informational organization, adaptive resources, boundary, and memory.
The container is embedded in the interaction graph 𝒢\_net(t) (§8.1),
through which it couples to other containers. Where that coupling must
be tracked as part of the adaptive state itself, the extended state is

X̄(t) = (P, R, B, M, 𝒢\_net(t)),

in which the boundary B governs which exchanges are admissible and the
graph 𝒢\_net records which are currently live; neither substitutes for
the other. X̄(t) ∈ 𝒜, the adaptive state manifold. Earlier editions wrote
(P, R, G, M), with G the interaction network and the boundary omitted;
that tuple is superseded by X̄.

\hypertarget{stable-adaptive-region}{%
\subsection{10.2 Stable Adaptive Region}\label{stable-adaptive-region}}

𝒮 ⊆ 𝒜. A system is adaptively stable whenever X(t) ∈ 𝒮. 𝒮 is generally a
dynamically evolving region of viable adaptive organization, not a
point.

\hypertarget{adaptive-degrees-of-freedom}{%
\subsection{10.3 Adaptive Degrees of
Freedom}\label{adaptive-degrees-of-freedom}}

Let F\_A(X) denote the set of feasible adaptive transformations
available from state X. Notation split, for clarity. Define
d\_A\^{}geom(X) = dim F\_A(X), the literal topological dimension when
F\_A(X) is given a construction supporting one, and d\_A\^{}eff(X), the
effective scalar measure of feasible adaptive possibility this
document's computational instantiation actually uses throughout (given
explicitly below). Wherever this document writes the bare symbol d\_A
without a superscript, from here on, it denotes d\_A\^{}eff
specifically, not d\_A\^{}geom --- the two are related but not identical
objects, and the split notation exists so that a reader cannot mistake
the computational default for an assertion that it equals the
topological dimension of the ambient adaptive-state manifold 𝒜. The two
coincide only if a given application's F\_A(X) is itself given a
literal-dimension construction, which the default instantiation below
does not assume; d\_A\^{}eff is best read as an order-preserving proxy
for accessible adaptive possibility, not a claim of literal
dimensionality. Large values of d\_A indicate flexibility; small values
indicate increasing rigidity. Collapse begins when adaptive options
disappear faster than they can be regenerated.

\textbf{Computational instantiation (the central missing piece, now
resolved).}

F\_A(X) has no domain-independent enumeration --- ``what transformations
are available'' is irreducibly domain-specific, the same way ``what
counts as an outcome'' (Ω) is. But d\_A itself does not need to be
measured by directly counting transformations; it can instead be derived
from quantities RICD has already made concrete: informational stress
S(t) (§6.7), resource debt δR(t) (§7.9), and recursive/network
fragmentation F(t) (§11.7, §8.9's N). All three are, by construction,
things that consume adaptive options --- accumulated unresolved stress
forecloses responses that would otherwise be available, resource debt
forecloses anything requiring uncommitted capacity, and fragmentation
forecloses coordinated multi-container responses. d\_A itself, as a
theoretical quantity tracking adaptive possibility, is structurally
derived from these considerations; the specific exponential-decay
functional below is this document's default computational instantiation
of that derived quantity, a model-derived choice among functional forms
sharing the same qualitative dependence, not itself forced by the
structural argument alone:

\emph{d\_A(t) = d\_A\^{}max · exp{[} −β\_S · S̃(t) − β\_R · δR̃(t) − β\_F
· F̃(t) − β\_H · {[}c(t){]}₊ {]},}

where d\_A\^{}max \textgreater{} 0 is a domain-specified ceiling (the
maximum plausible number of independent adaptive levers for that
container type, set once per adapter --- see Part 15) --- strict
positivity required explicitly, since d\_A\^{}max appears as a divisor
throughout this document (R\_g = 1 − d\_A/d\_A\^{}max here, and again in
the target-vulnerability and collapse-potential machinery downstream); a
ceiling of exactly zero would both divide by zero and semantically claim
a container has no adaptive possibility whatsoever, which is not a state
this document models as a valid container at all --- S̃, δR̃, F̃ are each
stress/debt/fragmentation normalized to {[}0,1{]} by the adapter's own
historical range, and β\_S, β\_R, β\_F \textgreater{} 0 are fitted
weights. This is not a claim that stress, debt, and fragmentation are
the only things that determine adaptive capacity --- it is a claim that
they are the three RICD already tracks with real formulas, so d\_A can
be computed without inventing a fourth independent measurement pipeline.
An adapter with a genuine independent proxy for feasible options (e.g.,
a literal count of currently-unconstrained policy levers) may substitute
it for d\_A directly; the exponential-decay form above is the default
when no such direct count is available.

\textbf{Headroom term.} c(t) is the cost-coverage ratio of Definition
10.3a.3, the share of a container's inflow already committed to its
total work, and {[}x{]}₊ = max(x, 0). Uncommitted capacity is itself
adaptive possibility: it is what any adaptation requiring new resources
draws on, so committed capacity is lost adaptive possibility even before
any resource debt accumulates. β\_H ≥ 0 is fitted; its inactive value
β\_H = 0 recovers the three-term form exactly. Without this term, d\_A
registers no contraction while a container slides toward break-even,
because δR (§7.9) accumulates only once it is in deficit; §10.3a
develops the consequence.

\emph{\textbf{There is no symmetric notion of ``entropic
possibility.''}} The formula above has one ceiling, d\_A\^{}max, and one
direction of travel: every entropic quantity this document tracks ---
stress, resource debt, fragmentation, and every mechanism that feeds
them --- only ever pulls d\_A(t) down from that ceiling, asymptotically
toward zero. Nothing in this framework's mathematics offers a second
axis along which entropy could instead expand some alternative kind of
richness or openness. Possibility, in this framework's own terms, is
d\_A(t); entropy's entire defined functional role is to contract it.
``Infinite entropic possibility'' is accordingly not a rich concept
symmetric to ``negentropic possibility'' that this document has simply
chosen not to develop --- it is close to a contradiction in terms once
stated precisely, the same category error as calling absolute zero
``infinite heat.'' Chaos, dissolution, or the collapse of structure do
not, within this framework, constitute an expansion of what is possible;
they are what the mathematics of possibility contracting toward its own
floor looks like from the inside. Read this way, RICD's own formalism
stands as a structural argument against treating meaninglessness or the
dissolution of order as a form of liberation or expanded freedom: by
this framework's own account, that dissolution is the asymptotic
narrowing of genuine possibility, not its opening.

\textbf{Recursive aggregation of d\_A (parent from children).}

Given a parent C* = A(C\_1, \ldots, C\_n) per §5.2, define

d\_A(C) = {[}Σ\_i w\_i · d\_A(C\_i){]} · (1 − F̃(C)), F̃(C) the clipped
fragmentation functional of §11.7 (Proposition 12.8) --- the canonical
formula now names the clipped instantiation explicitly, rather than
leaving ``F(C)'' ambiguous between the abstract term and the clipped one
that actually prevents 1−F(C) from going negative,

with w\_i the same resource weights used in §5.2's pooling step and
F(C*) the parent-level fragmentation (§11.7) between the children's
projected states. This makes the parent's adaptive capacity bounded
above by the resource-weighted sum of its children's capacities,
discounted by how poorly those children's adaptive options actually
cohere --- a system whose subsystems each have many options but whose
options do not compose (high fragmentation) has less real flexibility at
the aggregate level than the sum suggests, which is the qualitative
claim Proposition 12.8 needed and, per §11.7's new formula, now has a
mechanism for.

\emph{\textbf{Parent ceiling, specified explicitly.}} The formula above
computes realized d\_A(C*); nothing yet states what d\_A\^{}max(C*) is,
and leaving it unspecified creates a genuine fork --- if independently
adapter-set, recursive aggregation could produce a realized value
exceeding it; if the w\_i are normalized to sum to one, the parent's
realized capacity could never exceed its single most capable child,
which may understate genuine emergent capacity. Resolved by defining the
parent ceiling explicitly, rather than borrowing either child scale or
an unrelated independent guess:

d\_A\^{}max(C*) = ℱ\_max(d\_A\^{}max(C\_1), \ldots, d\_A\^{}max(C\_n),
χ\_cross) --- written ℱ\_max because the default this functional
computes is a resource-weighted sum, not the literal scalar maximum
operator,

where ℱ\_max is an adapter-supplied ceiling-aggregation functional, not
the scalar max operator despite the similar notation this replaces,
(default: the resource-weighted sum of child maxima) and χ\_cross ≥ 0 is
an optional, separately-justified cross-coupling bonus term representing
genuine emergent capacity the parent possesses beyond what any additive
combination of children alone would supply. d\_A(C*) computed above is
then normalized against this parent-owned ceiling, never against a
child's own scale. Admissibility condition, required. For the claimed 0
≤ d\_A(C*)/d\_A\^{}max(C*) ≤ 1 to actually hold rather than merely be
assumed, ℱ\_max must itself satisfy
ℱ\_max(d\_A\^{}max(C\_1),\ldots,d\_A\^{}max(C\_n),χ\_cross) ≥ d\_A(C*)
for every admissible realized state --- a real constraint on which
ceiling-aggregation functionals are valid choices, not automatically
satisfied by an arbitrary ℱ\_max. The stated default (resource-weighted
sum of child maxima) satisfies this whenever each child's own realized
d\_A(C\_i) ≤ d\_A\^{}max(C\_i), since the parent's realized d\_A(C*) is
itself a resource-weighted combination of the children's realized values
(§10.3) bounded above by the same weights applied to their maxima; an
adapter substituting a different ℱ\_max must verify this condition
directly for its own choice rather than inherit it from the default.

\hypertarget{a-drift-type-and-shock-type-collapse}{%
\subsection{10.3a Drift-Type and Shock-Type
Collapse}\label{a-drift-type-and-shock-type-collapse}}

Collapse is the contraction of adaptive possibility d\_A (§10.3):
adaptive options disappearing faster than they can be regenerated. That
contraction can be driven in two ways. In shock-type collapse, a large
discrete event removes adaptive possibility at once. In drift-type
collapse, many small, roughly homogeneous changes remove it steadily.
This section shows that the two are not different kinds of collapse.
They differ in the approach to the tipping threshold, which is slow and
steady under drift and abrupt under shock; past the threshold both
follow the same dynamics, and those dynamics are fast. What is slow in
drift-type collapse is the slide toward the threshold, not the collapse
itself. The definitions hold for any container whose boundary exchange
comes from a population of external partners: students of a college,
patients of a hospital, members of a congregation, residents of a town,
depositors of a bank.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.3a.1 (Exchange Base and Per-Partner
Exchange).}}

A container's exchange base N(t) \textgreater{} 0 is the number of
external partners whose participation generates its boundary exchange,
and its per-partner exchange ē(t) \textgreater{} 0 is the net exchange
each brings, so that §7.4's exchange term factors as

E\_exch(t) = ē(t) · N(t).

Write n = log N, e = log ē and w = log W, with W(t) total work (§7.3),
and let μ\_N, μ\_e and μ\_W denote the drift rates of n, e and w, the
expected change per unit time of each log quantity.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.3a.2 (Base Dynamics: Drift Plus Jump).}}

The exchange base evolves by a slowly varying drift plus a heavy-tailed
jump component, the §15.2 construction for an observable that is
drift-type in ordinary operation but can take rare large steps:

n(t+1) = n(t) + μ\_N(t) + σ\_n · ε(t) + J\_N(t), μ\_N(t+1) = μ\_N(t) +
σ\_μ · η(t),

with ε and η independent standard normal and J\_N(t) drawn from a
Student-t distribution with low degrees of freedom and scale wider than
σ\_n, so that most periods carry no material jump and a few carry a
large one. A large negative J\_N (a program closed, a campus
consolidated, a ward shut, a congregation split) is a shock-type loss of
exchange base.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.3a.3 (Cost Coverage and Headroom).}}

The cost-coverage ratio is the share of exchange already committed to
total work, and the headroom is the share still uncommitted and
available for adaptation:

c(t) = W(t) / E\_exch(t), h(t) = 1 − c(t).

Break-even is c = 1.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.3a.1 (Decomposition of the slide).}}

Between jumps, the expected growth rate of the coverage ratio is

g(t) ≡ d log c / dt = μ\_W(t) − μ\_e(t) − μ\_N(t).

Proof. log c = w − e − n by Definitions 10.3a.1 and 10.3a.3;
differentiate and take expectations. ∎

Each of the three ways a container slides toward break-even is a
separate term: total work rising (μ\_W \textgreater{} 0), per-partner
exchange falling (μ\_e \textless{} 0), and the base eroding (μ\_N
\textless{} 0). g is the slide rate.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.3a.4 (Price--Attrition Coupling).}}

Let p(t) be the container's posted price to its partners and π(t) = d
log p / dt. Raising the price drives partners away at a rate set by an
attrition sensitivity α ≥ 0:

μ\_N(t) = μ\_N⁰(t) − α · {[}π(t){]}₊,

where μ\_N⁰ is the base drift without price pressure. A price raised to
recover lost exchange widens the gap between what the container asks of
its partners and what it delivers to them, which is why it registers as
attrition and not only as exchange.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.3a.2 (Both price responses are drift
terms).}}

Suppose per-partner exchange tracks the posted price, μ\_e = π + μ\_e⁰.
Then a price change π alters the slide rate by

Δg = −π + α · {[}π{]}₊.

A price cut (π \textless{} 0) raises g by \textbar π\textbar: the
container gives up exchange on every partner. A price rise (π
\textgreater{} 0) changes g by (α − 1)·π: it slows the slide only if α
\textless{} 1, and if partners are price-sensitive (α \textgreater{} 1),
every increase speeds the slide it was meant to stop. Past α = 1 the
container has no price response that lowers g. ∎

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.3a.3 (Slow approach, fast collapse).}}

Let T be the present, c\_T = c(T) \textless{} 1, and g \textgreater{} 0
held at its present value. Then

c(t) = c\_T · e\^{}\{g (t − T)\}, break-even at t* = T + (−log c\_T) /
g.

Before t*, headroom shrinks at the rate dh/dt = −g · c(t): steadily, and
faster as c approaches 1. After t*, the container runs a deficit, and
resource debt (§7.9) accumulates at the rate E\_exch(t) · (c(t) − 1).
The excess c − 1 grows at the relative rate

d log(c − 1) / dt = g · c / (c − 1),

which exceeds g and is unbounded as c → 1⁺. The same drift that took
many periods to bring the container to break-even makes the deficit, and
with it the debt, grow without bound in relative terms immediately after
it. Proposition 10.3a.2 compounds this: a container that raises its
price in response to the deficit while α \textgreater{} 1 increases g. ∎

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.3a.5 (Headroom Term).}}

The headroom term of §10.3's instantiation of d\_A is −β\_H ·
{[}c(t){]}₊, with β\_H ≥ 0 and inactive value 0:

d\_A(t) = d\_A\^{}max · exp{[} −β\_S · S̃(t) − β\_R · δR̃(t) − β\_F · F̃(t)
− β\_H · {[}c(t){]}₊ {]}.

\emph{\textbf{Corollary 10.3a.4 (Drift-type collapse is steady
contraction of d\_A).}}

Under Proposition 10.3a.3's conditions, with S and F held fixed, before
t* the headroom term alone contracts adaptive possibility at the steady
rate d log d\_A / dt = −β\_H · g · c(t). After t*, the debt term adds
−β\_R · dδR̃/dt, which grows at the unbounded relative rate of
Proposition 10.3a.3. The slide is steady; the fall is sharp. ∎

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.3a.6 (Drift-Type and Shock-Type Collapse).}}

Let t* be the time a container crosses break-even. Over the approach
window {[}t₀, t*{]}, the log change in coverage, log c(t*) − log c(t₀),
is the sum of a drift part, ∫ g dt, and a jump part, the sum of −J\_N
over the window. The collapse is drift-type if the drift part accounts
for most of the change, and shock-type if a single jump does.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.3a.5 (One collapse, two approaches).}}

After t*, the evolution of δR and d\_A depends only on the state at t*
(E\_exch, c, g and the jump process), not on whether c reached 1 by
drift or by jump. Drift-type and shock-type collapse therefore share the
same post-threshold dynamics and differ only in their approach. ∎

\emph{\textbf{Hypothesis 10.3a.6 (Drift turns into shock near the
threshold).}}

As a drift-type approach nears break-even, the jump component becomes
more active: large negative steps in the exchange base become more
frequent. This is a hypothesis for a domain adapter to test, not a
consequence of the definitions.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.3a.7 (Available Capacity and Anchor
Capacity).}}

A container's available capacity R\_avail(t) is what it can draw on to
cover a deficit from its own means: its resource coordinate R(t) (§2.4)
plus the borrowing it can still obtain, L(t), supplied by the adapter.
Its anchor capacity A(t) is the net deficit that external anchors will
absorb on its behalf, non-negative for a non-extractive anchor and
negative where an anchor's extraction exceeds what it absorbs (the
Catalog's extractive anchor, §10.5b.13d). A(t) is supplied by the
catalog's anchor-capacity mechanism (§10.5b.13c) and has inactive value
0, so that A(t) = 0 wherever that mechanism is not in use; an unanchored
container has A(t) = 0 in any case.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.3a.8 (Point of No Return).}}

Past break-even, a container's distance to the point of no return is

Δ\_PNR(t) = R\_avail(t) + A(t) − δR(t),

and its point of no return is the first time t\_PNR ≥ t* at which
Δ\_PNR(t) ≤ 0: accumulated resource debt has used up everything that
could cover it. Break-even is the tipping point for every container; the
point of no return is where collapse becomes guaranteed, absent a new
anchor. Anchors do not move the tipping point. They move the point of no
return, which is why an anchored container can carry a large deficit
that would be fatal to an unanchored one.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.3a.9 (Recovery Window).}}

The recovery window is the interval {[}t*, t\_PNR): past the tipping
point, before the point of no return. Within it, collapse is not yet
determined. If the container is returned below break-even (c \textless{}
1) and held there, it runs a surplus and its debt is paid down; this is
recovery.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.3a.7 (The window narrows with velocity).}}

Take total work constant after the crossing (μ\_W = 0) and the slide
rate g \textgreater{} 0 constant, so that c(t) = e\^{}\{g (t − t*)\} and
the deficit flow is W · (1 − e\^{}\{−g (t − t*)\}). Let B = Δ\_PNR(t*)
be the capacity remaining at the crossing. The debt added s periods past
the crossing is

ΔδR(s) = W · {[} s − (1 − e\^{}\{−g s\}) / g {]},

which is increasing in g for every s \textgreater{} 0. The window's
length τ\_W, which solves ΔδR(τ\_W) = B, is therefore strictly
decreasing in g. For a slow slide (g·τ\_W small), τ\_W ≈ √(2B / (W ·
g)); for a fast one (g·τ\_W large), τ\_W ≈ B / W + 1/g, and τ\_W never
exceeds B / W + 1/g.

Proof. Integrate the deficit flow from 0 to s. Its derivative in g is W
· ∫₀ˢ u · e\^{}\{−g u\} du \textgreater{} 0. The limits follow from the
expansions of 1 − e\^{}\{−g s\} for small and large g·s, and the bound
from 1 − e\^{}\{−g s\} ≤ 1. ∎

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.3a.8 (The catch needed grows with speed and
time).}}

At a time t in the window, a positive jump J in the exchange base
returns the container below break-even exactly when

J \textgreater{} log c(t) = g · (t − t*).

The correction needed to arrest the fall grows linearly with both the
speed of the slide and the time already spent past the tipping point.
Recovery also requires that the slide not resume: after the jump, g ≤ 0,
or the container crosses break-even again. ∎

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.3a.9 (Rescue and reset).}}

Past the point of no return (Δ\_PNR \textless{} 0), the container
survives only if a new anchor raises A by at least −Δ\_PNR(t), together
with the deficit that accrues until the container is brought below
break-even. A reset is such a rescue together with a reversal of the
slide, g ≤ 0: the rescue clears the debt and the reversal stops it from
re-forming. A rescue without a reversal only moves the point of no
return later. A rescue by an extractive anchor (Catalog §10.5b.13d)
moves the point of no return later only while the anchor's net flow
stays positive; past its sign change it moves the point of no return
earlier. ∎

\textbf{Inactive configuration.} With μ\_N ≡ μ\_e ≡ 0, no jumps, α = 0
and β\_H = 0, this section adds nothing to the engine's dynamics:
exchange evolves as §7.4's E\_exch already does, and §10.3's d\_A has
its three-term form. Definitions 10.3a.7 to 10.3a.9 are read-outs of
existing quantities (R, δR) together with the anchor capacity A, and add
no dynamics of their own.

\hypertarget{adaptive-rigidity}{%
\subsection{10.4 Adaptive Rigidity}\label{adaptive-rigidity}}

R\_g = Φ(d\_A), where Φ′ \textless{} 0. Rigidity increases as adaptive
degrees of freedom decrease, derived from the geometry of adaptive
possibility rather than introduced independently.

\textbf{Computational instantiation.}

R\_g(t) = 1 − d\_A(t)/d\_A\^{}max, the direct normalized complement of
§10.3's d\_A --- monotonically decreasing in d\_A as required, bounded
in {[}0,1{]}, and requiring no separate fitted parameters beyond those
already used to compute d\_A itself.

\hypertarget{collapse-operator}{%
\subsection{10.5 Collapse Operator}\label{collapse-operator}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.1.}}

The collapse operator 𝒬 maps adaptive trajectories toward
lower-dimensional adaptive manifolds: 𝒬 : 𝒮 → 𝒮′, where dim(𝒮′)
\textless{} dim(𝒮). Collapse is dimensional contraction within adaptive
state space.

\hypertarget{a-the-collapse-inequality-hull-breach-condition}{%
\subsection{10.5a The Collapse Inequality (Hull-Breach
Condition)}\label{a-the-collapse-inequality-hull-breach-condition}}

§6.9 left the boundary of informational equilibrium, D(t) \textless{}
D\_c, as an unspecified stability region. With I, S, Π\_f, and d\_A now
all concrete (§6.6, §6.7, §6.5, §10.3), that boundary can be written as
a single checkable inequality:

(1 − I(t)) + S̃(t) \textgreater{} θ\_hull · Π\_f(t)

where S̃(t) is stress normalized to {[}0,1{]} by its own historical range
(matching §10.3's normalization) and θ\_hull \textgreater{} 0 is a
fitted threshold. The left side is total unresolved divergence pressure
--- informational incoherence plus accumulated stress; the right side is
correction capacity --- feedback permeability scaled by how much
correction the container's threshold tolerates. The inequality holding
at time t is a hull breach: an instantaneous signal that
divergence-and-stress pressure has, at that moment, outrun the
container's demonstrated capacity to correct it.

\emph{\textbf{Terminology, fixed throughout this document from here
on.}} Three stages are distinguished, and should not be used
interchangeably. \textbf{Hull breach} is the instantaneous condition
above holding at a single timepoint --- a measurement, not an event.
\textbf{Collapse onset} is a confirmed transition event, defined below,
requiring more than one instantaneous breach. \textbf{Collapse
resolution} is what §10.5b determines once onset is confirmed ---
self-arrest, terminal lock, full reorganization, or the disintegration
and grid-cascade failure modes elsewhere in this document. A single
measurement spike crossing the inequality is a hull breach; it is not,
by itself, a confirmed onset, and should never be read as meaning the
container has necessarily entered any particular resolved state. Stated
explicitly, to foreclose any reading of the inequality as identical to
completed collapse: hull breach is an instability-onset condition,
marking entry into a regime where the adaptive trajectory has exceeded
its ordinary correction capacity, not the collapse operator's own
dimensional-contraction result. Confirmed onset (below) may still
resolve into self-arrest, terminal lock, full reorganization, or
structural disintegration depending on which transition mechanism
applies --- the inequality identifies entry into instability, not which
of those outcomes follows.

\emph{\textbf{Event-confirmation rule.}} A hull breach constitutes
confirmed collapse onset only once the inequality holds continuously for
a fitted minimum duration τ\_confirm, rather than at the instant it is
first crossed --- a single noisy measurement spike that self-corrects
within τ\_confirm does not constitute onset. This is the default
confirmation rule; two alternatives remain available and may be
substituted without altering anything else in this document's collapse
machinery: requiring k consecutive discrete observations rather than a
continuous duration, or requiring posterior confidence in genuine breach
(rather than measurement noise) to exceed a fitted probability
threshold. All three share the same purpose --- preventing a single
spike from triggering onset --- and a domain adapter may choose
whichever fits its own observation cadence; τ\_confirm (or its
discrete/probabilistic equivalent) is fitted per domain. Where the
k-consecutive-observations form is used, k must be converted to an
actual elapsed duration via the adapter's own observation cadence before
being compared against anything stated elsewhere in duration terms --- k
observations spaced a month apart and k observations spaced a year apart
are not the same sustained interval, and ``sustained'' throughout this
document means continuously satisfied over an elapsed duration, not
merely across n samples, unless an adapter has explicitly converted n
into a domain-specific duration first. This applies generally, not only
here: every other sustained-state criterion in this document (Ultimate
Convergence's persistence requirement, progressive collapse's τ\_prog,
and any other minimum-duration condition) is subject to the same
discipline whenever the underlying data are irregularly or variably
sampled across institutions or domains being compared.

\emph{\textbf{Confirmed onset does not itself invoke 𝒬.}} Confirmed
collapse onset and the collapse operator 𝒬's dimensional contraction
(Definition 10.1, Proposition 12.5) are distinct levels of event, not
two names for the same thing. Onset marks confirmed entry into
instability; what follows is resolved by §10.5b's transition mechanisms
into self-arrest, terminal lock, full reorganization, or structural
disintegration, and 𝒬's dimensional contraction applies only to the
outcomes among these that actually constitute completed collapse rather
than a resolved return to stability. Self-arrest in particular is
confirmed onset followed by recovery before 𝒬 ever applies --- an onset
that resolves without the dimensional contraction 𝒬 describes, not a
case where 𝒬 briefly applies and then reverses.

\textbf{Relationship to the confrontation trigger.}

§10.5b.3 introduces a transient forcing term, F\_conf, active near a
confrontation-threshold crossing. F\_conf is not a separate gate from
the inequality above; it is one route by which the inequality's
left-hand side can be driven over threshold. F\_conf amplifies D\_op
transiently, which (via §6.6's formula for I) transiently depresses
I(t), directly increasing (1 − I(t)). A container can therefore cross
hull-breach either through an active F\_conf episode or through ordinary
stress accumulation and permeability decline with no confrontation event
at all --- F\_conf is a common accelerant, not a necessary cause, and
§10.5a's inequality remains the single operative criterion for collapse
onset in every case.

§10.5a establishes when a container crosses from bounded oscillation
into a tipping event. It does not distinguish what happens next ---
whether the container is a mid-transition system that will nonetheless
stabilize, a resource-locked system that briefly de-stabilizes without
permanent damage, or a genuinely different transformation entirely.
§10.5b develops that distinction: the same threshold crossing has at
least three qualitatively different resolutions, determined by which
channels converge, what force drives the event, and how much of that
force survives as usable momentum through the transition.

10.5a.1 Detectability and the Real Cost of a Quiet Approach.

Definition 10.5a.1 (Approach Detectability). Detectability tracks the
full hull-breach margin rather than d/dt{[}(1−I(t)){]}, the rate of
change of informational integrity alone, which is blind to S̃(t) and
Π\_f(t), both of which are part of the hull-breach inequality (§10.5a)
this definition exists to detect the approach to: a container whose I(t)
stays flat while Π\_f(t) collapses suddenly --- a real, sudden,
shock-type approach to breach through the feedback-permeability channel
alone --- would register as Detect(t) ≈ 0 on integrity alone, which is
precisely the case this mechanism most needs to catch. For a container
approaching hull-breach over a window {[}t−w, t{]}, using §16.3's own
