margin(t) = θ\_hull·Π\_f(t) − {[}(1−I(t))+S̃(t){]} (positive when safely
below breach, crossing to negative at breach), define detectability as
the positive part of margin's own rate of decline, normalized against
the same container's own historical baseline volatility (§16.11's
Structural Baseline):

Detect(t) = {[}−d(margin)/dt{]}₊ / σ\_baseline,

using margin(t) as already established at §16.3 and §10.5b.9b rather
than a new symbol, so that a sudden, discrete approach through any of
the three channels --- declining I(t), rising S̃(t), or collapsing
Π\_f(t) --- produces high Detect(t) even close to threshold, while a
slow, homogeneous drift through any of the same three channels produces
low Detect(t) even as it crosses the identical threshold. {[}x{]}₊
denotes max(x,0): margin(t) rising (moving away from breach) contributes
zero to Detect(t) rather than a negative value, since detectability of
an approach is not defined for a container currently retreating from
one. This is the formal content of runaway acceleration itself
functioning as a real, distinct warning signal a quiet, gradual approach
never generates, now genuinely channel-agnostic rather than sensitive
only to I(t)'s own decline.

σ\_baseline is the baseline volatility of d(margin)/dt, so that
Detect(t) is dimensionless; where σ\_baseline = 0, a fitted floor σ\_min
\textgreater{} 0 is used.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5a.1.1 (Noticeable Collapse Is More
Recoverable, Not Less).}} Holding the magnitude of the eventual
hull-breach crossing fixed, the real probability of external
intervention or internal correction being triggered \emph{before or
during} the crossing rises with Detect(t), not with the severity of the
underlying divergence itself:

P(intervention triggered) = ζ\_detect · Detect(t) / (Detect(t) +
κ\_quiet), ζ\_detect ∈ (0,1{]} and κ\_quiet \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

recovering near-zero intervention probability for a genuinely quiet,
undetected drift (Detect(t) → 0) regardless of how severe the underlying
divergence has become, and rising intervention probability as the
approach itself becomes more sudden and noticeable --- the formal
content of the real, counterintuitive asymmetry that a slow collapse,
precisely because it generates no runaway-acceleration signal to trigger
response, can be more dangerous than a fast one of equal or even lesser
eventual severity. This does not revise §10.5a's inequality itself,
which remains the sole criterion for whether a crossing has occurred; it
adds a real, separate account of why crossing sooner and more visibly is
not strictly worse than crossing later and more quietly, contrary to
what severity alone would suggest.

\hypertarget{b-entropy-regime-classification-and-the-convergent-collapsereset-mechanism}{%
\subsection{10.5b Entropy Regime Classification and the Convergent
Collapse/Reset
Mechanism}\label{b-entropy-regime-classification-and-the-convergent-collapsereset-mechanism}}

Hull breach identifies when a container tips. This section identifies
what determines the outcome once it does, why formally similar
convergence events between representation channels produce opposite
outcomes depending on regime, how a container arrives in a given regime
in the first place, what interventions are and are not safe, and --- in
§10.5b.0 --- the structural, geometric origin of the
verticality/horizontality distinction the rest of the section relies on.
Every quantity below is built from primitives already defined elsewhere
in this document, including §8.6a's extractive-coupling mechanism, which
turns out to be load-bearing rather than incidental to what follows.

\textbf{10.5b.1 Entropy Regime Classification}

Let σ\_o(t), σ\_p(t) denote the trailing-window variance of the
period-to-period \emph{changes} in P\_o and P\_p respectively --- not
the variance of their raw level --- both already computable from the
adapter's raw O\_o, O\_p feeds (§15.2), no new observation required.
This distinction is not cosmetic: a smoothly, monotonically trending
channel (official messaging slowly lagging toward a declining reality,
for instance) can show substantial variance-of-level in a trailing
window purely from the trend itself, while a channel that has already
settled to a new level shows low level-variance even if it arrived there
erratically. Level-variance is the wrong notion of volatility for this
purpose and will misclassify exactly the case this mechanism exists to
catch; variance of differences (the standard approach to measuring
volatility, as with financial returns rather than price levels) does not
have this failure mode. Classify a container into one of three regimes,
not two:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Low-entropy baseline regime: D\_op, D\_om, D\_pm all small; Π\_f high
  (near 1, the meta-layer is genuinely bidirectionally permeable); σ\_o
  and σ\_p both low-to-moderate --- a slight, rhythmic rocking rather
  than violent oscillation. D\_om is not required to be zero, and should
  not be: a small nonzero D\_om --- the official layer under mild,
  healthy self-questioning rather than perfect, unexamined confidence
  --- functions as a negative-feedback channel preventing an unchecked,
  grandiose official narrative. D\_om ∈ (0, ε\_healthy{]} is the target
  band, not D\_om = 0. This regime is the reference state the other two
  are diagnosed as deviations from.
\item
  High-entropy regime (official-shielded): σ\_o(t) low, σ\_p(t) high ---
  the official/declared layer presents as stable while the operational
  layer is actually turbulent underneath it. The official layer's
  stability is not evidence of health here; it is the shield, in the
  deceptive sense of Definition 10.5b.13b.8.
\item
  Medium-entropy regime (official-exposed): σ\_o(t) high, σ\_p(t) low
  --- the official layer presents as visibly unstable while the
  operational layer is actually steady beneath it --- the mirror case.
  The official layer's visible trouble is not evidence of deeper damage
  here; the trouble is exposed precisely because nothing underneath it
  is concealing anything.
\end{itemize}

These are not different containers in kind; the same container can
occupy any of the three at different points in its trajectory.
Definition 10.5b.1.7 gives the numerical decision boundaries, an
indeterminate class and the hysteresis that keep the three regimes
mutually exclusive and exhaustive; healthy entropy (Definition
10.5b.1b.3) is a property within the baseline, not a fourth regime.

\emph{\textbf{Terminology note:}} The two regimes are named by entropy
level, matching the naming of the low-entropy baseline, with the
shielded/exposed tag retained parenthetically here as the σ\_o/σ\_p
diagnostic criterion and dropped in subsequent use. The same names
identify these two regimes throughout, including §8.6a's
extraction-target population and §8.8a's cascade sequence.

\emph{\textbf{Corrected, RICD 15.8: contraction-aligned variance.}}
Prior editions treated σ\_o(t) and σ\_p(t) as plain, symmetric variance
of period-to-period changes, with no directional treatment --- the same
failure mode identified and fixed in the higher-ed collapse tracker's
own code, where a resource windfall (a positive shock) and a resource
crisis (a negative shock) of equal magnitude produced identical variance
contributions and were therefore indistinguishable to the classifier.
P\_o and P\_p do carry inherent polarity at the theory level: a positive
shock, once absorbed, expands a container's adaptive possibility, while
a negative shock sharply contracts it (§10.3's d\_A(t)). This
distinction corrects that omission, sourcing the needed direction
externally --- from d\_A(t)'s own already-signed rate of change ---
rather than attempting to give P\_o(t) and P\_p(t)'s own
Jensen--Shannon-style divergence an intrinsic direction, which §4.12
documents as the expensive, cascading path and not a change confined to
this section alone.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.1a (Contraction-Aligned Variance).}} A
period τ within the trailing window is \emph{contraction-aligned} when
d(d\_A)/dt(τ) \textless{} 0 --- the container's own adaptive degrees of
freedom were narrowing over that period, per §10.3's dependence on S̃,
δR̃, and F̃. σ\_o(t) and σ\_p(t), as consumed by every downstream
mechanism in this document (the regime classification above, Frantic
Striking's Θ\_rock trigger of §8.8a.i, R\_class(t) of §16.9.2, and
elsewhere), are redefined as the trailing-window variance of ΔP\_o(τ),
ΔP\_p(τ) restricted to contraction-aligned periods --- τ outside that
set contributes zero to the accumulated sum of squared deviations, the
same semivariance construction the higher-ed tracker's own
dynamics.rolling\_causal\_downside\_variance() already uses for a single
domain's resource channel, generalized here to source its sign from
d\_A(t) rather than from a domain-specific quantity. A window in which
every period is contraction-aligned --- the container was narrowing
throughout --- recovers the original, uncorrected definition exactly,
which is what makes this a correction to how σ\_o/σ\_p are computed
rather than a replacement of what they measure: everything downstream
that consumes σ\_o(t) and σ\_p(t) as given continues to receive the same
two quantities, now restricted to the periods the theory says should
count. A window with no contraction-aligned period gives σ\_o(t) =
σ\_p(t) = 0 by this definition, since nothing contracted, and the regime
rule then reads both channels at its variance floor σ\_floor (Definition
10.5b.1.7) instead of dividing by zero.

\textbf{Relationship to the feedback landscape Φ (§6.4).}

The three regimes above correspond, in the terms of §6.4's stochastic
gradient flow, to three diagnostically distinct regions of the
divergence landscape Φ\_t(P; P\_m, M) --- not an established basin
topology, since Φ\_t's explicit three-term form does not by itself
derive a specific count or depth of local minima for arbitrary O\_o(P),
O\_p(P), and P\_m: the low-entropy baseline is Φ's own genuine global
minimum, and the high- and medium-entropy regimes are the two further
regions reachable from baseline via the shock-branching mechanism below
(§10.5b.2), each held open by a specific forcing mechanism --- F\_conf
and, for the high-entropy case, extraction input (§8.6a) --- consistent
with Axiom C1 (§10.5b.15): withdraw the forcing, and the trajectory is
no longer held away from Φ's baseline attractor, and relaxes back toward
it. §10.5b.9a's resilience accumulator Z(t) governs how deep and how
easily escaped the medium-entropy basin is over repeated excursions.

\emph{\textbf{No circularity between regime and landscape.}} Regime
labels are diagnostic classifications of dynamically realized regions of
state space; they are never inputs to the equations that generate those
regions. Every forcing term entering F\_total(t) above --- F\_conf,
F\_boom, F\_demand, and every later addition --- is triggered by
continuous state variables (Π\_f, D\_op, D\_om, ω\_meta\^{}up/down, and
similar), never by the discrete regime label itself (§10.5b.1's
σ\_o/σ\_p classification). These terms are additive drift in the dP/dt
equation, not modifications to Φ\_t's own three-term formula (§6.4);
describing them as ``reshaping Φ's basins'' refers to their practical
effect on the trajectory --- holding it away from Φ's own baseline
minimum for as long as the forcing remains active, which behaves, from
the trajectory's own vantage point, as though the local landscape had
changed shape --- not a literal claim that Φ\_t's mathematical
definition changes. Regime is read off the resulting trajectory after
the fact; it does not feed back into determining F\_total(t) or Φ\_t
itself.

\textbf{10.5b.1.1 Three Kinds of Full Alignment}

Full alignment --- official, operational, and the metamessenger
population in agreement on the container's own valence --- occurs in
three structurally distinct forms. The first is negentropic: the
operational core is negentropic and all three layers agree, accurately.
This is the Sustained Horizontal Matrix (Definition 10.5b.0.8), toward
which the low-entropy baseline (in which the layers are near-aligned,
with a small, healthy D\_om in the official layer and a meta-layer
functioning as a permeable membrane) tends. The remaining two are
entropic. A container can reach D\_op(t) ≈ 0 with an entropic
operational core in two structurally different ways, distinguishable
only by G(t) (§6.3.2), never by internal agreement alone, since both
produce identical D\_op, D\_om, D\_pm readings. Formally, a container is
fully aligned at t when max\{D\_op(t), D\_om(t), D\_pm(t)\} ≤ ε\_full
and 𝓡(t) = 0 (Definition 10.5b.1.3), ε\_full \textgreater{} 0 fitted,
and the three kinds are separated by the valence of the operational core
and by grounding: negentropic core with G(t) ≥ G\_high; entropic core
with G(t) ≥ G\_high (Type A); entropic core with G(t) ≤ G\_low (Type B),
G\_high \textgreater{} G\_low fitted.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.1.1 (Type A: Self-Accurate Alignment).}}
Operational is entropic and G(t) is high --- the container accurately
knows its own entropic nature. Official agrees because there is nothing
to conceal from itself: an accurate report of an accurately entropic
reality requires no management. No firewall is needed, M\_decep(t) → 0,
and metamessengers require no split composition, since there is no
internal tension anywhere in the container for a population to divide
over. What contracts the container's own adaptive possibility is not
concealment cost, since none is being paid; it is the entropic nature
itself, decaying, with no negentropic energy present anywhere in the
system to resist or contain that decay --- d\_A(t)'s formula (§10.3)
already prices this directly, through S̃(t) and δR̃(t) alone, without
requiring M\_decep(t) to carry any of the explanatory weight it does in
ordinary high-entropy predation. A Type A container may know its own
nature to be destructive without self-deception about that either,
accurately understanding itself as closed and holding no internal means
of reopening. Such containers characteristically lean into their own
entropy and either collapse or are contained by other systems in the
surrounding grid --- isolated, pruned, or eliminated by those systems,
as tissues clear cells that fail to regulate themselves ---
comparatively early, since a container that does not conceal its
entropic nature presents no obstacle to the grid's recognizing it.
Formally, define the grid's containment hazard on a high-entropy
container i,

λ\_cont(i,t) = κ\_cont · Σ\_k Λ\_ki(t) · Ψ\_total(k,t) · A\_acc(k,i,t) ·
Danger\_true(i,t), κ\_cont \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

where Danger\_true(i,t) is i's own current extractive intensity, by
default D\_op(i,t), or ℱ\_i(t) where that is available (the catalog's
perceived-danger term of §10.5b.2 uses the same quantity), summing over
coupled neighbors k, each contributing in proportion to its own
negentropic strength and its observational accuracy into i
(§10.5b.18.9.1). Because A\_acc(k,i,t) = κ\_access(k,i,t) + (1 −
κ\_access(k,i,t))·(1 − D\_op(i,t)), a Type A container, with D\_op(i,t)
≈ 0, presents A\_acc(k,i,t) ≈ 1 to every neighbor regardless of their
observational depth, and its containment hazard is maximal for its level
of danger; a deceptively shielded container (Definition 10.5b.13b.8),
with D\_op ≫ 0, lowers A\_acc for every neighbor lacking deep access,
and with it its own hazard.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.1.2 (Type B: Confused Alignment).}}
Operational is entropic and G(t) is low --- the container's own entropic
core has come to believe itself negentropic, and that false self-belief,
not accurate self-acceptance, is what produces alignment with official.
The surface signature is identical to Type A: low D\_op, no felt need
for a firewall. The underlying condition is the opposite: not the
absence of anything to hide, but the absence of any internal party left
who knows there is something to hide. Type B in its complete form, in
which official, operational, and the entire metamessenger population
have converged on the same self-deception, is rare: it requires that no
rogue metamessenger remain anywhere in the container, since no other
side is left within it for one to align with against operational
reality. Formally, complete Type B requires ω\_meta(t) = 0 exactly
(Definition 10.5b.18.2): no truth-aligned sub-process left. If each of
N\_meta metamessenger sub-processes independently remains truth-aligned
with probability q\_truth \textgreater{} 0, the probability of complete
Type B is (1 − q\_truth)\^{}N\_meta, falling exponentially in the size
of the metamessenger population. This is the most closed of the three
forms of full alignment, receptivity 𝓡(t) (Definition 10.5b.1.3) falling
to zero around a false self-model rather than an accurate one.

\textbf{Structural Account: Verticality and Horizontality}

The topology of verticality and horizontality (catalog, §10.5b.0) gives
the structural account of what the regimes' symptoms are symptoms of.
Under sufficient stress or velocity a container concentrates its energy
into rigid, closed, hierarchical structure (Axioms V1 and C3: as
verticality V(t) rises past a threshold Θ\_V, Π\_f(t) → 0, D\_op(t) → 1,
d\_A(t) → 0 and R\_g(t) → 1), and absent that pressure it relaxes toward
open, distributed exchange (Axioms V2 and C4: horizontality W(t) is
greatest as Π\_f(t) → 1, I(t) → 1 and D\_op(t) → 0 with sustainable
resources). High verticality is the structural counterpart of the
high-entropy regime, and horizontality of the low-entropy baseline. Some
minimum rigidity is constitutive of being a container at all; rigidity
beyond that minimum is costly. The full definitions, the verticality
operator and their consequences are in the catalog.

\textbf{Control Versus Influence: The Do No Harm Principle}

An intervention is control-shaped when it forces P toward one
predetermined target regardless of where the container's own unforced
dynamics would arrive, and influence-shaped when it supplies pressure,
resources or corrective feedback without fixing an endpoint (Definition
10.5b.0.11, catalog). A control-shaped intervention forecloses reachable
states by construction, adds the forcing tax M\_forced to the resource
debt δR(t) and the forced rebellion F\_rebel to divergence pressure, and
so contracts d\_A(t). This is the Do No Harm Principle (Fundamental
Theorem 10.5b.0.5.1): if the container's continued adaptive capacity is
the goal, force is harm, with a single exception at the verge of
imminent terminal collapse. It follows from Axiom 9 together with
§10.3's d\_A(t); the derivation is in the catalog, §10.5b.0.11.

\hypertarget{b.1.7-entropy-regime-decision-rule}{%
\subsection{10.5b.1.7 Entropy-Regime Decision
Rule}\label{b.1.7-entropy-regime-decision-rule}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.1.7 (Entropy-Regime Decision Rule).}}

§10.5b.1 classifies by the volatilities σ\_o, σ\_p of the official and
operational channels (Definition 10.5b.1a), qualitatively. Let σ\_ref
\textgreater{} 0 be the container\textquotesingle s own structural
baseline volatility (§16.11), and define the volatility balance

r\_σ(t) = log(σ̃\_o / σ̃\_p), σ̃ = max(σ, σ\_floor),

with σ\_floor \textgreater{} 0 a calibrated variance floor, so that zero
or near-zero volatility in either channel leaves both quantities
defined. Write each channel\textquotesingle s level against the baseline
as v\_o = log(σ̃\_o/σ\_ref) and v\_p = log(σ̃\_p/σ\_ref); a channel is
volatile when its level exceeds a fitted v₁. The raw rule, with fitted
r₀ \textgreater{} 0, is

• low-entropy baseline: neither channel volatile, v\_o ≤ v₁ and v\_p ≤
v₁ (including the container\textquotesingle s slight, rhythmic baseline
rocking);

• high-entropy: the operational channel volatile and the official
channel much calmer, v\_p \textgreater{} v₁ and r\_σ \textless{} −r₀;

• medium-entropy: the official channel volatile and the operational
channel much calmer, v\_o \textgreater{} v₁ and r\_σ \textgreater{} r₀;

• indeterminate (§15.2): a channel volatile without a clear imbalance,
\textbar r\_σ\textbar{} ≤ r₀.

Given defined inputs, these four are mutually exclusive and exhaustive
(if v\_p \textgreater{} v₁ and r\_σ \textgreater{} r₀, then v\_o
\textgreater{} v\_p \textgreater{} v₁, a medium-entropy case). Absolute
volatility now enters every class, as §10.5b.1\textquotesingle s regime
descriptions require: a container whose channels are both calm is
baseline however their ratio falls.

\textbf{Hysteresis.} To prevent flickering at a boundary, the
classification keeps state, with fitted margins 0 \textless{} Δ\_r
\textless{} r₀ and Δ\_v \textgreater{} 0. Entry predicates (each raw
condition made stricter):

• B\_in: v\_o ≤ v₁ − Δ\_v and v\_p ≤ v₁ − Δ\_v;

• H\_in: v\_p \textgreater{} v₁ + Δ\_v and r\_σ \textless{} −(r₀ +
Δ\_r);

• M\_in: v\_o \textgreater{} v₁ + Δ\_v and r\_σ \textgreater{} r₀ +
Δ\_r.

Exit predicates (each raw condition relaxed):

• B\_out: v\_o \textgreater{} v₁ + Δ\_v or v\_p \textgreater{} v₁ +
Δ\_v;

• H\_out: v\_p ≤ v₁ − Δ\_v or r\_σ ≥ −(r₀ − Δ\_r);

• M\_out: v\_o ≤ v₁ − Δ\_v or r\_σ ≤ r₀ − Δ\_r;

• I\_out (for the indeterminate class): B\_in or H\_in or M\_in.

Transition rule: a container in class X ∈ \{B, H, M\} stays in X until
X\_out holds; it then moves to the class whose entry predicate holds, or
to indeterminate if none does. A container in the indeterminate class
stays there until I\_out holds, and then moves to the class whose entry
predicate holds. The entry predicates are disjoint: B\_in requires both
levels at most v₁ − Δ\_v while H\_in and M\_in each require one above v₁
+ Δ\_v, and H\_in and M\_in require r\_σ on opposite sides of ±(r₀ +
Δ\_r). So the destination is always unique. Each exit predicate is the
negation of a weaker version of its entry predicate, so a container that
has just entered a class cannot immediately exit it. With hysteresis the
classification depends on the previous class as well as on the current
r\_σ, v\_o and v\_p.

Empty-window convention: if no period in the trailing window is
contraction-aligned, the ordinary classifier is bypassed (the floor
would keep its formulas defined, but there is no contraction-aligned
volatility to classify), and the container is classified as baseline if
D\_op, D\_om and D\_pm are within their healthy bands and indeterminate
otherwise. {[}The rule is structural in form; the thresholds, margins
and floor are fitted.{]}

\hypertarget{b.1b-the-metamessenger-system}{%
\subsection{10.5b.1b The Metamessenger
System}\label{b.1b-the-metamessenger-system}}

These quantities are related but distinct, and they are kept separate.

\textbf{Entropy by layer.} E\_o(t), E\_p(t), E\_m(t) ∈ {[}0,1{]}: the
normalized entropy of the official, operational and meta layers,
adapter-supplied. Total container entropy is the mass-weighted mean E(t)
= (μ\_o E\_o + μ\_p E\_p + μ\_m E\_m) / (μ\_o + μ\_p + μ\_m).
§10.5b.1b.3\textquotesingle s entropic lean ϑ = Ê\_o − E compares the
official layer\textquotesingle s assessment of the
container\textquotesingle s total entropy with that total; §10.5b.1b.3
uses this E throughout.

\textbf{Meta-layer thickness.} μ\_m(t), the meta layer\textquotesingle s
mass, and m(t) = μ\_m / (μ\_o + μ\_p + μ\_m), its share of the
container: how large the layer is.

\textbf{Truth-aligned share and mass.} ω(t) ∈ {[}0,1{]}: the share of
the meta layer serving the carrier of the container\textquotesingle s
original values (§10.5b.1b.1), the existing ω\_meta\^{}up(t), redefined.
T(t) = ω·μ\_m is the truth-aligned mass.

\textbf{Firewall thickness.} φ\_F(t) = (1 − ω(t))·m(t): the share of the
container\textquotesingle s mass that is comfort-aligned meta layer,
actively holding information back. Thickness of the layer (m) and
thickness of the firewall (φ\_F) are different: a thick meta layer that
is mostly truth-aligned is a wide channel, not a wall. Feedback
permeability Π\_f (Definition 6.4) stays as defined, measured from
correction-and-response data; the hypothesis is that Π\_f falls as φ\_F
rises, which a working adapter can test, not a redefinition of Π\_f.

\textbf{Three different flows.} Upward throughput J\_up(t) =
Π\_f·κ\_up·T·D\_op is information, gated by feedback permeability: the
rate at which accurate information reaches the official layer.
Conversion C\_ω(t) is members changing sides within the meta layer.
Migration J\_join(t) is mass: truth-aligned members leaving the meta
layer to join the official layer. Throughput drives conversion and
migration (§10.5b.1b.4), but the three are distinct quantities with
distinct units: information per unit time, and members (mass) per unit
time for the other two.

\textbf{Official-layer state.} O\_w(t) ∈ {[}0,1{]}, the overwritten
share of the official layer\textquotesingle s original-values content
(§10.5b.1b.5), and ϑ(t), its entropic lean (§10.5b.1b.3), are state
variables with their own dynamics.

\textbf{The dependency map.}

The mechanisms form one chain, written here in the order of cause:

(a) Divergence D\_op generates mixed messaging, which builds the meta
layer (§10.5b.1b.2).

(b) The truth-aligned mass T determines upward throughput J\_up, and the
comfort-aligned share determines firewall thickness φ\_F (§§10.5b.1b.1,
10.5b.1b.2, 10.5b.1b.4).

(c) Throughput lowers divergence toward a positive floor, converts
comfort-aligned members, and draws truth-aligned members into the
official layer: the migration loop, with its balance point ½, its
tipping share ω* just past it, and its point of no return ω\_PNR
(§10.5b.1b.4).

(d) The comfort-aligned side\textquotesingle s rewriting costs
resources, M\_shield, which enters M\_add, raising resource debt δR and
contracting adaptive possibility d\_A through §10.3; sustained, it
overwrites the official layer\textquotesingle s content (§10.5b.1b.5).

(e) Throughput beyond the official layer\textquotesingle s absorption
capacity floods it (§10.5b.1b.5).

(f) External knocks act on the meta layer during a tipping window,
converting the loyal comfort-aligned share; whether the composition at
launch ω\_L, computed from the pre-knock composition and the
knock\textquotesingle s size, is above or below ω* sets the flip
direction (§10.5b.1b.6).

(g) Mass sets the strength of the selected route; the lift and spin of
the launch, host anchoring and the truth share of the converging pair
decide whether the route can reach reorganization (§§10.5b.9.1,
10.5b.9.2).

(h) How a knock divides into push, lift and spin, and which of three
thresholds it reaches first, decides between the 180° flip, the full
launch and fracture (§§10.5b.9.1, 10.5b.9.2, 10.5b.9d.3); a fracture
leads to reforging or adhesion, never to a reset (§10.5b.9g).

(i) Healthy entropy is where the chain rests in the low-entropy
baseline: thin meta layer, ω near 1, divergence at its positive floor
within the healthy band, and the official layer\textquotesingle s lean
settled at its slightly entropic set point (§§10.5b.1b.3, 10.5b.1b.4).

No arrow in the chain runs from a regime label to an equation: regime
labels classify where the chain has taken a container (§10.5b.1.7), as
§10.5b.1 already requires.

§§10.5b.1b.1 to 10.5b.1b.6 describe one mechanism. Its state is three
quantities: the meta layer\textquotesingle s mass μ\_m(t) (how thick it
is), its truth-aligned share ω(t) (who it serves), and the
operational--official divergence D\_op(t) (how much mixed messaging the
container generates). Healthy entropy is where this mechanism rests in
the low-entropy baseline; the migration loop is how it moves between
regimes; and the flip direction is read from it during a tipping event.

\hypertarget{b.1b.1-truth-alignment}{%
\subsection{10.5b.1b.1 Truth-Alignment}\label{b.1b.1-truth-alignment}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.1b.1 (Truth-Alignment).}}

Let V₀ be a container\textquotesingle s original values: in a human or
institutional container, the values it was founded on; in a non-human
container, the constitutive properties that define what it is. V₀ is
read from the container\textquotesingle s founding record or
constitutive properties (a charter, a mission at founding, a
species\textquotesingle{} or system\textquotesingle s defining
functions), not from what its official layer currently says it values,
so that it can be measured independently of the official layer whose
accuracy is in question. In a self-deceptive container (D\_op above the
healthy band), V₀ is carried by exactly one of the two outer layers, its
carrier layer c\_V(t):

c\_V(t) = operational (p) in the medium-entropy regime, c\_V(t) =
official (o) in the high-entropy regime.

In the medium-entropy regime the operational layer is sound and the
official layer holds a false account of the container\textquotesingle s
character; in the high-entropy regime the official layer still holds the
original values while the operational layer has abandoned them in
practice, treating them as a cover. A metamessenger\textquotesingle s
alignment is the layer whose agenda its output serves, o or p. It is
truth-aligned when it serves the carrier layer. In a container whose
layers agree (D\_op within the healthy band), there is no carrier to
choose between, and truth-alignment means reporting the
container\textquotesingle s actual state; a fully aligned entropic
container reports its own entropy, which is why its truth-aligned share
is high despite its entropy.

ω(t) ∈ {[}0,1{]} is the resource-weighted truth-aligned share of the
meta layer. This replaces Definition 10.5b.18.2\textquotesingle s
\textquotesingle aligned with transmitting operational reality to the
official layer\textquotesingle{} with a definition that holds in every
regime. In both self-deceptive regimes the two coincide: truth-aligned
metamessengers are the ones that carry operational reality upward, the
medium-entropy ones because operational reality is the truth, and the
high-entropy rogues because confronting the official
layer\textquotesingle s original values with operational reality is how
those values are served. ω(t) is therefore the existing
ω\_meta\^{}up(t), redefined, not a new variable. {[}Structural.{]}

\hypertarget{b.1b.2-meta-layer-mass-composition-and-migration}{%
\subsection{10.5b.1b.2 Meta-Layer Mass, Composition and
Migration}\label{b.1b.2-meta-layer-mass-composition-and-migration}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.1b.2 (Meta-Layer Mass, Composition and
Migration).}}

The meta layer has mass μ\_m(t), of which the truth-aligned mass is T(t)
= ω(t)·μ\_m(t). Three flows change it, and they are kept distinct:

• Formation: mixed messaging, generated by divergence, recruits new
metamessengers at rate κ\_mix·D\_op(t). Because mixed messaging pulls
both ways, new members enter split evenly, half truth-aligned and half
comfort-aligned.

• Conversion: existing members change sides, at net rate C\_ω(t)
(§10.5b.1b.4); conversion changes T but not μ\_m.

• Migration: truth-aligned members leave the meta layer and join the
official layer, a mass flow J\_join(t) (§10.5b.1b.4); comfort-aligned
members do not migrate.

With turnover δ\_m at equal rates on both sides:

dμ\_m/dt = κ\_mix·D\_op − J\_join − δ\_m·μ\_m, dT/dt = ½·κ\_mix·D\_op +
C\_ω − J\_join − δ\_m·T, dμ\_o/dt ⊇ + J\_join,

where ⊇ marks an added term in an existing equation. From T = ω·μ\_m,
the composition evolves as

dω/dt = {[} C\_ω + (½ − ω)·κ\_mix·D\_op − (1 − ω)·J\_join {]} / μ\_m.

{[}Model-derived.{]} The three terms are the conversion loop, the pull
of new members toward ½, and the dilution of the truth-aligned share as
truth-aligned members leave. The equation keeps ω in {[}0, 1{]}: at ω =
0 every term is non-negative (given C\_ω ≥ 0 there, §10.5b.1b.4), and at
ω = 1 every term is non-positive.

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.1b.2.1 (Thin in baseline, thick and split when
divergent).} Without migration or conversion, μ\_m relaxes to
κ\_mix·D\_op / δ\_m and ω relaxes to ½. In the low-entropy baseline,
D\_op is within its healthy band, so the meta layer is small, a
permeable membrane (Proposition 10.5b.1.6); in both non-baseline regimes
D\_op is large, so the meta layer is large, and new
members\textquotesingle{} even split pulls its composition toward ω = ½.
This derives a composition near ½ where formation dominates conversion;
where conversion is strong, the loop of §10.5b.1b.4 drives ω toward 0 or
1 instead, and how often non-baseline containers sit near ½ is an
empirical question. ∎ {[}Structural given the flows; that non-baseline
containers typically sit near ½, and that the meta layer is typically
comparable in mass to each outer layer, m ≈ 1/3, are hypotheses.{]}
Definition 10.5b.18.2\textquotesingle s regime list changes accordingly:
in both non-baseline regimes, ω is typically near ½.

\hypertarget{b.1b.3-healthy-entropy-epistemic-humility}{%
\subsection{10.5b.1b.3 Healthy Entropy: Epistemic
Humility}\label{b.1b.3-healthy-entropy-epistemic-humility}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.1b.3 (Healthy Entropy: Epistemic
Humility).}}

E(t) is total container entropy (§10.5b.1b), and Ê\_o(t) is the official
layer\textquotesingle s assessment of it. The official
layer\textquotesingle s entropic lean is ϑ(t) = Ê\_o(t) − E(t) ∈ {[}−1,
1{]}: positive for a self-image more entropic than the facts (humility),
negative for one more negentropic (flattery). A low-entropy container
has healthy entropy, or epistemic humility, when both

0 \textless{} ϑ(t) ≤ ϑ\_h and D\_om(t) ∈ (0, ε\_healthy{]},

hold. The two conditions are not the same: D\_om measures how far the
official and meta layers diverge, in any direction, and ϑ the direction
and size of the official layer\textquotesingle s error about the whole.
Healthy entropy is the conjunction: a small divergence, leaning
entropic. ϑ\_h \textgreater{} 0 is fitted.

\textbf{Axiom 9a (The Asymmetry of Feedback Errors; a foundational
commitment).} Inaccurate negentropic feedback, flattery, carries
vertical scripts (Definition 10.5b.2.1): taken up, they license the
container to extract from and displace onto others, and so corrupt its
operational layer, harming others as well as itself, and are hard to
undo. Inaccurate entropic feedback without vertical scripts corrupts
only the official layer, mainly harming the container itself, and is
easier to correct. This holds unless the container has other healthy
primary couplings, or anchors outside the source of the inaccurate
feedback, that block the script\textquotesingle s uptake. Consequently,
in the hazard below, a\_H \textgreater{} a\_M. The axiom fixes the
direction of the inequality; the ratio a\_H / a\_M is fitted, and its
size is an empirical question.

\textbf{Proposition 9a.1 (How a vertical script closes a container).}
Let u\_s(t) ∈ {[}0,1{]} be the container\textquotesingle s uptake of
vertical scripting (Definition 10.5b.2.12). A container under a vertical
script does not learn reciprocal coupling, so its feedback permeability
is capped, Π\_f(t) ≤ Π\_max·(1 − u\_s(t)), and the accurate feedback it
does take in, entropic or negentropic, decays at a rate δ\_fb·(1 +
κ\_s·u\_s), κ\_s \textgreater{} 0 fitted: it cannot hold on to it. As
u\_s → 1, Π\_f → 0: by Proposition 6.4b(iii) the unforced flow vanishes,
and the gated throughput J\_up = Π\_f·κ\_up·T·D\_op falls to zero, so
neither the feedback flow nor the meta layer generates any correction
internally. Divergence then evolves only under the new-divergence source
s\_D, forcing and noise. Where these produce divergence on net (in
particular where the container\textquotesingle s own extraction, §8.6a,
adds divergence), D\_op rises without limit from within. Only two things
reverse this: an anchor (§10.5b.13), which supplies accurate, reciprocal
coupling from outside, and rogue self-healing, the truth-aligned
metamessengers opening the valve slowly (§10.5b.1b.5). ∎ {[}The closure
follows from the cap and Proposition 6.4b; the cap and the decay rate
are model-derived.{]}

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.1b.3.1 (Why the healthy lean is entropic).}
Let the hazard of leaving the baseline be λ\_lb(ϑ) = a\_H·{[}−ϑ{]}₊ +
a\_M·{[}ϑ{]}₊, and let the realized lean fluctuate about a set point ϑ̄,
ϑ = ϑ̄ + w\_ϑ·ε₀, with ε₀ symmetric about 0, continuous distribution
function F₀ with positive density near the optimum (so that the optimum
is unique), and w\_ϑ \textgreater{} 0 a scale. The expected hazard is
minimized at

ϑ̄* = clip\_\{{[}−1,1{]}\}( −w\_ϑ·F₀⁻¹( a\_M / (a\_H + a\_M) ) ).

By Axiom 9a, a\_H \textgreater{} a\_M, so a\_M / (a\_H + a\_M)
\textless{} ½ and the unconstrained optimum is strictly positive: the
safest set point leans entropic. Within the scale family, the optimum is
proportional to w\_ϑ, so it grows with the size of the fluctuations; if
it would exceed 1, the constrained optimum is the boundary.

Proof. The derivative of E{[}h{]} in ϑ̄ is −a\_H·F₀(−ϑ̄/w\_ϑ) + a\_M·(1 −
F₀(−ϑ̄/w\_ϑ)), increasing in ϑ̄, so E{[}h{]} is convex and minimized where
the derivative vanishes, F₀(−ϑ̄*/w\_ϑ) = a\_M / (a\_H + a\_M); on {[}−1,
1{]} a convex function\textquotesingle s minimum is the clipped
unconstrained one. ∎

\textbf{Definition 10.5b.1b.3.2 (Dynamics of the Lean).} The lean
relaxes toward its set point and is pushed by inaccurate feedback:

dϑ/dt = Proj\_\{{[}−1,1{]}\}{[} −κ\_ϑ·(ϑ − ϑ̄*) + η\_E·f\_E(t) −
η\_N·f\_N(t) {]},

with f\_E, f\_N ≥ 0 the rates of inaccurate entropic and negentropic
feedback received, κ\_ϑ, η\_E, η\_N \textgreater{} 0 fitted, and Proj
the projection that holds ϑ at a boundary of {[}−1, 1{]} when the
bracket would push it out. Between such inputs, ϑ returns to ϑ̄*.
{[}Model-derived.{]}

\hypertarget{b.1b.4-the-migration-loop}{%
\subsection{10.5b.1b.4 The Migration
Loop}\label{b.1b.4-the-migration-loop}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.1b.4 (The Migration Loop).}}

The upward throughput J\_up(t) = Π\_f(t)·κ\_up·T(t)·D\_op(t) is the rate
at which accurate information about the container actually reaches and
is taken up by its official layer: it grows with the truth-aligned mass
and with how much there is to report, and it is gated by the
container\textquotesingle s feedback permeability Π\_f (Definition 6.4),
so that a closed container (Π\_f = 0) takes up nothing however many
truth-aligned metamessengers it has. κ\_up converts truth-aligned mass
times divergence into an information rate. J\_up is information, not
mass, and every process below uses this gated throughput. It acts on the
system in three distinct ways:

• It lowers divergence: dD\_op/dt = s\_D(t) − κ\_rec·J\_up(t), where
s\_D(t) ≥ 0 is the rate at which new divergence arises (a changing
environment, new information, ordinary drift), so that divergence is
never driven to zero by assumption.

• It converts members: the more truth reaches the official layer, the
faster comfort-aligned members cross over, C\_ω(t) =
r\_ω·g\_J(J\_up)·μ\_m·ω(1 − ω)(ω − ω\_c), where g\_J(J\_up) = J\_up /
(J\_up + J\_½) rises with throughput and saturates, and ω\_c ∈ (0,1) is
conversion\textquotesingle s own threshold. Below ω\_c, C\_ω \textless{}
0: the comfort-aligned side converts faster.

• It draws truth-aligned members up: J\_join(t) = j\_m·g\_J(J\_up)·T(t),
j\_m ≥ 0 fitted.

r\_ω, J\_½, κ\_rec \textgreater{} 0 fitted. {[}Model-derived; the cubic
is the simplest conversion law with a threshold.{]} The tipping share ω*
is not conversion\textquotesingle s threshold alone but the threshold of
the whole composition equation of §10.5b.1b.2, where conversion, the
even split of new members and migration\textquotesingle s dilution
balance (Proposition 10.5b.1b.4.1). The balance point is ω = ½; the
tipping share ω* = ½ + ε\_ω, with ε\_ω \textgreater{} 0 small and
fitted, sits a hair past it: an evenly split meta layer drifts toward
the comfort-aligned side unless something tips it past ω*.

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.1b.4.1 (The firewall\textquotesingle s tipping
point).} Take μ\_m \textgreater{} 0, D\_op ≥ 0 and J\_up \textgreater{}
0, so that g\_J(J\_up) \textgreater{} 0. Using J\_join =
j\_m·g\_J(J\_up)·ω·μ\_m, §10.5b.1b.2\textquotesingle s composition
equation is dω/dt = g\_J(J\_up)·ω(1 − ω)·f\_ω(ω) on (0, 1), with

f\_ω(ω) = r\_ω·(ω − ω\_c) − j\_m + φ\_ω·(½ − ω) / (ω(1 − ω)), φ\_ω =
κ\_mix·D\_op / (μ\_m·g\_J(J\_up)) ≥ 0,

evaluated at the container\textquotesingle s current operating state
(D\_op, μ\_m, J\_up). Its interior resting states are the roots of f\_ω.
When J\_up = 0 (a closed container, Π\_f = 0, or no truth-aligned mass),
conversion and migration both vanish and dω/dt = κ\_mix·D\_op·(1 − 2ω) /
(2μ\_m): with formation active, the composition moves toward ½, and
there is no tipping structure. A closed container cannot use the loop to
tip itself into truth alignment; its formation alone tends toward an
evenly split meta layer. (i) If φ\_ω \textgreater{} 0, f\_ω(ω) → +∞ as ω
→ 0 and f\_ω(ω) → −∞ as ω → 1, so f\_ω has at least one root; and since
ω(1 − ω) vanishes at the boundaries while f\_ω keeps these signs near
them, the field points inward there. If φ\_ω = 0, f\_ω(ω) = r\_ω·(ω −
ω\_c) − j\_m is linear and has at most one interior root, which is
unstable since r\_ω \textgreater{} 0 (§10.5b.1b.4). The boundaries are
then equilibria, because ω(1 − ω) vanishes there, and ω = 0 is stable
exactly when f\_ω(0) \textless{} 0 and ω = 1 exactly when f\_ω(1)
\textgreater{} 0. When the root lies in (0, 1), both hold and the stable
states are the two boundaries; without an interior root, only one
boundary is stable, the one the flow points toward. (ii) Write q(ω) = (½
− ω)/(ω(1 − ω)); then q′(ω) = −(ω² − ω + ½)/(ω(1 − ω))² \textless{} 0,
and \textbar q′\textbar{} is smallest at ω = ½, where it equals 4. So
f\_ω′ = r\_ω + φ\_ω·q′ vanishes at no more than two points, and f\_ω has
at most three roots. If r\_ω ≤ 4φ\_ω, f\_ω is strictly decreasing, with
a single stable root: no tipping point. (iii) If φ\_ω \textgreater{} 0,
r\_ω \textgreater{} 4φ\_ω, and there are shares ω₁ \textless{} ω₂ with
f\_ω(ω₁) \textless{} 0 \textless{} f\_ω(ω₂), then f\_ω has exactly three
roots, a stable low state in (0, ω₁), an unstable state ω* in (ω₁, ω₂),
and a stable high state in (ω₂, 1). The stable states lie strictly
inside (0, 1), since formation keeps both from reaching the boundary. ω*
is the tipping share: ω rises toward the high state from above it and
falls toward the low state from below it. ∎ {[}Structural; the precise
conditions are φ\_ω \textgreater{} 0, r\_ω \textgreater{} 4φ\_ω and the
sign condition in (iii).{]}

\textbf{Corollary 10.5b.1b.4.1a (Locating and calibrating the tipping
share).} Setting f\_ω(ω*) = 0 and solving for the conversion threshold
gives the exact calibration

ω\_c = ω* − j\_m / r\_ω + φ\_ω·(½ − ω*) / (r\_ω·ω*(1 − ω*)),

at the operating state at which ω* is fitted. Since ω* \textgreater{} ½,
the last term is negative: formation lowers the conversion threshold
needed for a given tipping share. When formation is negligible there
(φ\_ω small), ω* ≈ ω\_c + j\_m / r\_ω: migration, by removing
truth-aligned members from the meta layer, makes the loop harder to tip.
Because φ\_ω depends on the operating state, the tipping share is a
moving root, ω*(t), recomputed as D\_op, μ\_m and J\_up change; where
they change slowly relative to the composition, it can be treated as
quasi-static. The fitted value ω* = ½ + ε\_ω is the tipping share at a
reference operating state, and fixes ω\_c there through the exact
calibration. ∎

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.1b.4.2 (Near the threshold).} Linearizing the
full composition dynamic at ω*, with the operating state held fixed, a
departure δ = ω − ω* follows the local stochastic model dδ = λ*·δ·dt +
σ\_ω·dW\_t, with W a standard Wiener process, σ\_ω \textgreater{} 0 the
noise intensity in this convention, and λ* = g\_J(J\_up)·ω*(1 −
ω*)·f\_ω′(ω*) \textgreater{} 0 (Proposition 10.5b.1b.4.1), approximately
r\_ω·g\_J(J\_up)·ω*(1 − ω*) when formation is negligible. Without noise,
the time for the departure to reach a resolution threshold δ\_res is
t\_resolve ≈ (1/λ*)·log(δ\_res / \textbar δ(0)\textbar), which grows
without bound as the starting share approaches ω*: a nearly balanced
container lingers. With noise, the probability of eventually resolving
toward the high state is Φ\_N(δ(0)·√(2λ*) / σ\_ω), Φ\_N the standard
normal distribution function: ½ at the threshold, and near 0 or 1 within
a few multiples of σ\_ω / √(2λ*) of it. ∎ {[}Structural for the local
linear model; it describes behavior near ω*, not the whole nonlinear
passage to either stable state.{]}

\textbf{Definition 10.5b.1b.4.3 (The Loop\textquotesingle s Points of No
Return).} Let Δω\_down(ω) be the largest reduction in the truth-aligned
share that one counter-knock can deliver to a container at share ω,
including whatever composition dynamics run during the knock, and
Δω\_up(ω) the largest increase. The reset is locked in above

ω\_PNR⁺ = inf\{ ω \textgreater{} ω* : ω − Δω\_down(ω) \textgreater{} ω*
\},

and the collapse is locked in below ω\_PNR⁻ = sup\{ ω \textless{} ω* : ω
+ Δω\_up(ω) \textless{} ω* \}: past these, even the strongest permitted
counter-knock cannot carry the share back across the tipping share. Both
are comparisons of shares, so they are dimensionless. In
§10.5b.1b.6\textquotesingle s reduced knock model (the
knock\textquotesingle s conversion the only flow during it, and 𝒦\_max
the largest impulse the neighborhood can deliver), a container with no
loyal comfort-aligned members left loses at most the factor
e\^{}\{−ζ\_K·𝒦\_max\} of its share, so ω\_PNR⁺ = ω*·e\^{}\{ζ\_K·𝒦\_max\}
when that is below 1, and when it is 1 or more there is no reset-side
point of no return in {[}0, 1{]}: even a container at share 1 can be
knocked back to the tipping share; and a container whose loyal share
converts in full gains at most (1 − ω)·Ψ\_loyal, so ω\_PNR⁻ = (ω* −
Ψ\_loyal)/(1 − Ψ\_loyal) when Ψ\_loyal \textless{} ω*, with no
collapse-side point of no return otherwise. These closed forms rest on
the reduced knock model\textquotesingle s assumptions about which
members remain to convert and on the largest permitted knock; they are
not consequences of the tipping-point equation alone. {[}Structural
given the knock model; 𝒦\_max is fitted.{]}

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.1b.4.4 (Throughput lowers divergence to a
positive floor).} Take the reset branch, where the loop holds ω near a
value ω̄ close to 1 (ω cannot sit exactly at 1, since new members enter
evenly split, §10.5b.1b.2), and hold ω, Π\_f and s\_D at constant
values. Let μ\_m be at its quasi-steady value under §10.5b.1b.2, μ\_m =
κ\_mix·D\_op / δ\_eff(J\_up), with δ\_eff(J\_up) = δ\_m +
j\_m·ω̄·g\_J(J\_up), since migration removes J\_join =
j\_m·g\_J(J\_up)·ω·μ\_m (§10.5b.1b.2) and ω is held at ω̄; write j\_eff =
j\_m·ω̄. Then J\_up = c₀·D\_op²/δ\_eff(J\_up), c₀ = Π\_f·κ\_up·ω̄·κ\_mix,
and divergence obeys dD\_op/dt = s\_D − f(D\_op) with f(D) =
κ\_rec·c₀·D² / δ\_eff(J\_up(D)). (i) f is continuous, f(0) = 0, f(D) →
∞, and f is strictly increasing: d log f / dD = 2/D −
j\_eff·g\_J′(J\_up)·J\_up′(D)/δ\_eff; differentiating
J\_up·δ\_eff(J\_up) = c₀·D² gives J\_up′(D) ≤ 2U/D, and
j\_eff·J\_up·g\_J′(J\_up) = j\_eff·g\_J(J\_up)·J\_½/(J\_up + J\_½) ≤
δ\_eff·J\_½/(J\_up + J\_½), so d log f / dD ≥ (2/D)·J\_up/(J\_up + J\_½)
\textgreater{} 0. (ii) So for s\_D \textgreater{} 0 there is exactly one
equilibrium D\_op* \textgreater{} 0, and it is stable, since dD\_op/dt
changes sign from positive to negative there. (iii) It lies between the
bounds

√( s\_D·δ\_m / (κ\_rec·c₀) ) ≤ D\_op* ≤ √( s\_D·(δ\_m + j\_m·ω̄) /
(κ\_rec·c₀) ),

with D\_op* = √(s\_D·δ\_eff* / (κ\_rec·c₀)) exactly, δ\_eff* =
δ\_eff(J\_up(D\_op*)). It falls within the healthy band if s\_D ≤
ε\_healthy²·κ\_rec·c₀ / (δ\_m + j\_m·ω̄), and only if s\_D ≤
ε\_healthy²·κ\_rec·c₀ / δ\_m. ∎ {[}Structural within the quasi-steady
approximation for μ\_m and ω: a unique stable equilibrium of this
reduced scalar system, not a global result for the full coupled
dynamics, in which ω is held near 1 by assumption rather than shown to
stay there.{]} The divergence floor is the formal source of the small,
standing divergence healthy entropy requires: a container in a changing
world always has some divergence for its meta layer to carry.

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.1b.4.5 (When a reset reaches healthy
entropy).} A reset reaches healthy entropy (§10.5b.1b.3) if three things
hold: (i) ω passes ω* and the loop takes it toward 1 (Proposition
10.5b.1b.4.1); (ii) the divergence floor of Proposition 10.5b.1b.4.4
lies within the healthy band; (iii) the lean ϑ settles at its set point
ϑ̄*, with 0 \textless{} ϑ̄* ≤ ϑ\_h (Definition 10.5b.1b.3.2 and
Proposition 10.5b.1b.3.1). (i) and (ii) follow from the throughput
dynamics; (iii) is a separate condition on the feedback the container
receives during and after the reset, which these dynamics do not supply.
∎

\hypertarget{b.1b.5-high-entropy-rewriting-overwriting-and-the-safe-opening-rate}{%
\subsection{10.5b.1b.5 High Entropy: Rewriting, Overwriting and the Safe
Opening
Rate}\label{b.1b.5-high-entropy-rewriting-overwriting-and-the-safe-opening-rate}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.1b.5 (High Entropy: Rewriting,
Overwriting and the Safe Opening Rate).}}

In the high-entropy regime the comfort-aligned majority keeps the
official layer\textquotesingle s self-image intact against a corrupted
operational reality by continuous rewriting, at a resource cost

M\_shield(t) = κ\_sh·(1 − ω(t))·μ\_m(t)·D\_op(t),

κ\_sh \textgreater{} 0 fitted, in resource units per unit time: the
level-driven component of the Catalog\textquotesingle s internal
self-deception maintenance cost M\_decep,int (Definition 10.5b.18.4),
generalizing its κ\_decep·(1 − ω\_meta\^{}up)·D\_op by the meta
layer\textquotesingle s mass, and distinct from the rate-driven
rewriting load M\_rewrite of Proposition 10.5b.18.23g; it enters M\_add
(§7.3). {[}Model-derived.{]}

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.1b.5.1 (The cost of rewriting).} At fixed
meta-layer mass, M\_shield rises as ω falls and as D\_op rises, and
through §7.9 raises resource debt and through §10.3 contracts adaptive
possibility. With μ\_m also changing, M\_shield rises whenever d
log{[}(1 − ω)·μ\_m·D\_op{]}/dt \textgreater{} 0. ∎

\textbf{Definition 10.5b.1b.5.2 (Overwriting).} Sustained rewriting does
not only hide the operational reality; it gradually replaces the
official layer\textquotesingle s own content with the operational
layer\textquotesingle s account. Let O\_w(t) ∈ {[}0,1{]} be the
overwritten share of the official layer\textquotesingle s
original-values content, with

dO\_w/dt = κ\_ow·(1 − ω)·μ\_m·D\_op·(1 − O\_w),

κ\_ow ≥ 0 fitted. Type B total convergence (Definition 10.5b.1.2) is
O\_w ≥ O\_w*, fitted: the official layer no longer holds the original
values, so there is nothing left for a successor to form around
(Definition 10.5b.2.16). ω → 0 drives overwriting but is not itself
overwriting.

\textbf{Definition 10.5b.1b.5.3 (Feedback Flooding).} Let Ā\_o(t) be the
rate at which the official layer can absorb operational reality without
losing coherence, its absorption capacity (fitted, increasing in μ\_o
and d\_A). The container floods when J\_up(t) \textgreater{} Ā\_o(t),
and a flood is a collapse-onset event (§10.5a).

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.1b.5.4 (The rogues must open the valve
slowly).} A high-entropy container avoids flooding exactly while its
truth-aligned mass stays in the safe region T(t) ≤ T\_safe(t) = Ā\_o(t)
/ (Π\_f(t)·κ\_up·D\_op(t)), defined when Π\_f·D\_op \textgreater{} 0;
for a closed container or zero divergence, throughput is zero, T\_safe =
+∞ by convention, and the flooding constraint is inactive. Because each
unit of truth absorbed lowers D\_op, T\_safe rises as the container
improves, so the safe region widens as the opening proceeds. The region
is kept, and flooding avoided, as long as the opening never outpaces it:
dT/dt ≤ dT\_safe/dt whenever T = T\_safe. Opening the valve slowly is
this condition; it describes what a safe opening must satisfy, not a
trajectory the equations generate on their own. ∎ {[}The no-flood
condition is structural.{]}

\textbf{Hypothesis 10.5b.1b.5.5 (The medium-entropy step-down).} along
such a path the official layer begins to move, σ\_o rising while σ\_p
falls, so the container passes through the medium-entropy signature
before reaching low entropy.

\hypertarget{b.1b.6-the-direction-of-a-medium-entropy-flip}{%
\subsection{10.5b.1b.6 The Direction of a Medium-Entropy
Flip}\label{b.1b.6-the-direction-of-a-medium-entropy-flip}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.1b.6 (The Direction of a Medium-Entropy
Flip).}}

A lateral knock on a medium-entropy container acts on its meta layer
during the tipping window (§10.5b.7). Before the knock, the meta layer
divides into three parts: the truth-aligned share ω₀; the loyal
comfort-aligned share ℓ₀ = (1 − ω₀)·Ψ\_loyal, whose closure is
conditional and lifts once the knock arrives (Definition 10.5b.18.12);
and the unconditional comfort-aligned share n₀ = (1 − ω₀)·(1 −
Ψ\_loyal), whose closure persists through it. Measure the knock by its
cumulative impulse 𝒦 = κ\_K·∫ N\_B dt over the window. As the knock
accumulates, loyal members cross to the truth-aligned side, and a share
ζ\_K ≥ 0 of truth-aligned members is shaken loose to the unconditional
side:

dℓ/dK = −ℓ, dω/dK = ℓ − ζ\_K·ω, dn/dK = ζ\_K·ω.

The three shares stay non-negative and sum to 1. Over the window the
knock\textquotesingle s conversion is fast relative to the other terms
of §10.5b.1b.2, so the composition at launch is the exact solution

ω\_L(𝒦) = ω₀·e\^{}\{−ζ\_K·𝒦\} + ℓ₀·(e\^{}\{−ζ\_K·𝒦\} − e\^{}\{−𝒦\}) / (1
− ζ\_K) (ζ\_K ≠ 1), ω\_L(𝒦) = (ω₀ + ℓ₀·𝒦)·e\^{}\{−𝒦\} (ζ\_K = 1),

which lies in {[}0, 1{]} for every 𝒦. The flip direction is

s = +1 (toward the operational layer: reset, B2) if ω\_L(𝒦)
\textgreater{} ω*, s = −1 (toward the official layer: collapse, B1) if
ω\_L(𝒦) \textless{} ω*,

and, at ω\_L = ω* exactly, s is indeterminate (§15.2). The rule is
predictive: ω₀ and Ψ\_loyal are measured before the knock (Ψ\_loyal from
its behavioral proxy, Definition 10.5b.18.12), and 𝒦 from the
knock\textquotesingle s size at its onset, or forecast; nothing in it is
read from the outcome. Directly observed convergence data still take
precedence. Units: 𝒦 is dimensionless, κ\_K converting the impulse
integral into it. ``Exact'' here means exact within the reduced
tipping-window model, in which the knock\textquotesingle s conversion is
the only flow acting on the composition. With no shaking loose (ζ\_K =
0), a large knock (𝒦 → ∞) gives ω\_L → ω₀ + ℓ₀ = ω₀ + (1 − ω₀)·Ψ\_loyal,
which is Definition 10.5b.18.14\textquotesingle s Ψ\_total: the existing
quantity is this rule\textquotesingle s large-knock limit.

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.1b.6.1 (The new rule can select collapse).}
The former rule compared μ\_p\^{}eff with μ\_o and, since μ\_p
\textgreater{} μ\_o in the medium-entropy regime and μ\_p\^{}eff ≥ μ\_p,
always returned +1. Under this rule s = −1 whenever ω\_L \textless{} ω*,
an outcome now possible for a substantial range of pre-knock
compositions, since ω is typically near ½ (Proposition 10.5b.1b.2.1) and
ω* sits just above ½. How often it occurs depends on the distributions
of Ψ\_loyal, ζ\_K and 𝒦, which are to be measured. ∎ {[}Structural.{]}

Mass keeps its role in §10.5b.7: once the direction is set, the selected
route\textquotesingle s momentum, M\_tip(B1) or M\_tip(B2), sets the
strength of the tip, with μ\_p\^{}eff in B2. Only the selected
route\textquotesingle s momentum is computed.

\textbf{10.5b.3 The Confrontation Trigger}

As Π\_f(t) rises, a non-baseline container is increasingly forced to
register its doomed divergence term directly rather than let it pass
unprocessed. Consistent with the no-circularity principle above, the
selection between D\_op and D\_om is made from a continuous structural
variable correlated with the high-/medium-entropy configurations ---
verticality V(t) --- used here to select the confrontation target
without consulting the discrete regime classification itself, which
remains built from σ\_o/σ\_p (§10.5b.1) and is not redefined by this
selector:

\emph{w}\_high(t) = σ(κ\_sel·(V(t) − Θ\_V)), κ\_sel \textgreater{} 0
fitted,

\emph{D}\_target(t) = w\_high(t)·D\_op(t) + (1 − w\_high(t))·𝟙(s(t) =
−1)·D\_om(t) (§10.5b.9a.1's continuously-derived s(t)),

smoothly favoring D\_op as V(t) rises well above Θ\_V (deep high-entropy
territory) and D\_om as V(t) falls well below it while the container is
collapsing (s(t) = −1), with a genuine mixed region near Θ\_V itself
rather than a discrete switch --- the discrete high/medium-entropy
regime label itself remains σ\_o/σ\_p's own classification (§10.5b.1),
computed independently and never consulted to construct D\_target in the
first place. And a transient forcing term, active only in a short window
once Π\_f(t) crosses a confrontation threshold Π\_c while D\_target(t)
remains elevated:

\emph{F}\_conf(t) = ζ · Π\_f(t) · D\_target(t), ζ \textgreater{} 0
fitted, nonzero only near the Π\_c crossing.

This is a genuine self-intensification, not correction --- a brief
amplification of the very behavior producing the divergence, immediately
following the point at which the container is made to confront it,
before any resolution occurs one way or the other. A container whose
Π\_f never crosses Π\_c, or whose D\_target is already low, never enters
a tipping event and remains in bounded oscillation regardless of what
follows below. Note that a slow rise in Π\_f --- one whose rate stays
below the crossing dynamics that trigger F\_conf, rather than merely its
level --- can avoid activating this term altogether; this is the
operative mechanism for the rogue-metamessenger pathway of §10.5b.14.

\textbf{10.5b.4 Convergence Events and Regime-Dependent Valence}

Once a container is in a tipping event, its outcome depends on which
pair of channels subsequently converges toward zero divergence --- and
the same convergence event carries opposite meaning depending on regime,
because which layer is actually reliable flips between regimes:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  D\_pm → 0 (meta converges with operational): in the high-entropy
  regime, the self-monitoring layer validates an actually-chaotic
  operational reality --- internal correction is disabled. In the
  medium-entropy regime, the self-monitoring layer instead recognizes an
  actually-stable operational reality beneath a visibly unstable
  official layer --- genuine reorganization around a real signal.
\item
  D\_om → 0 (meta converges with official): in the high-entropy regime,
  the self-monitoring layer holds to the declared standard against a
  corrupted operational layer; this does not itself prevent a tipping
  event already underway, but determines the container's capacity for
  rapid reorganization once the corrupted operational layer is stripped
  away post-collapse. In the medium-entropy regime, the self-monitoring
  layer instead abandons an actually-accurate operational signal and
  validates an externally corrupted official narrative --- internal
  correction is disabled in the same structural way as the high-entropy
  bad case, despite involving the opposite pair of channels.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{10.5b.5 Driving Force and Convergence Pathways}

The force driving a container through a tipping arc is decomposed in
§8.5's propagation equation. The two regimes are driven by different,
dominant terms in that same equation --- but each regime admits more
than one causal route to its outcome, and the medium-entropy regime
specifically has two genuinely distinct outcomes rather than one,
determined by which channel converges:

\textbf{Flip Condition A (high-entropy, always terminal-locked when
unaided).}

Two causal routes converge on the same outcome. A1 (usual):
self-propelled --- F\_i-dominated, the container's own ongoing internal
churning (fed, per §8.6a and §8.8a, by vented extraction-heat once a
coupled neighbor decouples), with D\_pm → 0 convergence. A2
(occasional): externally triggered --- an external strike combines with
a D\_om → 0 collision event, producing oscillation larger than
self-propulsion alone could generate; the container, careening back
around, finds no counter-oscillating partner to strike against, and the
unopposed momentum forces the same terminal-locked outcome as A1. Both
routes end in the single outcome defined in §10.5b.9 --- this regime
does not have an intrinsic, unaided full-reorganization option under
either route (§10.5b.0, Corollary 10.5b.0.1); §10.5b.11a establishes the
distinct, externally-redirected exception, under which a non-terminal
but costly stripped reorganization becomes reachable.

\textbf{Flip Condition B (medium-entropy, externally driven, two
distinct outcomes).}

Always Σ\_jΛ\_ij-dominated --- driven by simultaneous multi-directional
lateral knocks from neighboring high-entropy containers, which launch
the container higher, and for longer, than any self-generated
oscillation could. Which of two outcomes results depends on which
channel converges during that launch: B1, D\_om → 0 (meta converges with
the visibly chaotic official layer) engages only the smaller fluid mass
--- official and meta, the minority layers in this regime --- producing
a terminal lock. B2, D\_pm → 0 (meta converges with the actually-calm
operational layer) engages the larger fluid mass --- operational and
meta, since operational holds the majority of a medium-entropy
container's mass by the regime's own definition --- and that larger,
coherently-moving mass, combined with the greater launch height shared
by both B routes, completes full reorganization rather than stalling
partway through it.

\textbf{10.5b.7 Fluid-Mass Weighting}

Flip Condition A's two routes both terminate the same way (§10.5b.0), so
the coherence-and-dissipation account below applies to A without
modification. Flip Condition B's two outcomes cannot be explained the
same way --- the medium-entropy regime's own definition requires D\_om
to be elevated, so treating D\_om as ``residual conflict left over''
would produce the contradictory conclusion that the full-reorganization
outcome (B2) is low-coherence, which is wrong. What actually
distinguishes B1 from B2 is not how much divergence is left unresolved
elsewhere, but how much of the container's total representational mass
is carried by whichever pair is converging. Define mass weights μ\_o,
μ\_p, μ\_m for the official, operational, and meta layers respectively
(fitted per container, or adapter-supplied directly), with μ\_p
\textgreater{} μ\_o structurally in the medium-entropy regime. The
effective driving momentum for each B route is weighted by the mass of
its converging pair --- both members, since B1 is official-and-meta
convergence and B2 is operational-and-meta convergence, not a single
layer in either case:

M\_tip(B1) = ∫\emph{\{t\_launch\}\^{}\{t\_resolution\} (μ\_o + μ\_m) ·
N\_B(t) dt, M\_tip(B2) = ∫}\{t\_launch\}\^{}\{t\_resolution\} (μ\_p +
μ\_m) · N\_B(t) dt, where t\_launch is the timepoint Drive\_B(t) becomes
active (the external knock's onset) and t\_resolution is the timepoint
the tipping window closes --- either a confirmed collapse-onset event
(§10.5a) or Drive\_B(t) returning to zero without one, whichever comes
first --- rather than an unbounded or implicit integration window; ``the
tipping window'' used elsewhere in this document refers to exactly this
interval, stated here explicitly so an implementation has a concrete
start and end condition rather than inferring one from context.

where χ\_dir(t) is the directional force modifier of Definition 4.11.2
--- a push landing orthogonal to the container's own established
structural grain contributes disproportionately to tipping momentum
relative to an equal-magnitude push landing along the grain, on top of
and independent of the mass-weighting below. Setting κ\_dir = 0
(Definition 4.11.2) recovers the undirected form exactly.

drawing on the same shared Drive\_B(t) --- the same external launch ---
but scaled by structurally different mass fractions. Because μ\_p
\textgreater{} μ\_o in this regime, B2 carries more momentum than B1 for
the same external launching force --- μ\_m's shared contribution to both
routes does not change this ordering, since it adds identically to each
side. The ordering sets the strength of whichever route is taken, not
which one: the direction is set by Definition 10.5b.1b.6.

\textbf{10.5b.8 Coherence, Dissipation, and Flip Condition As Momentum
Functional}

For Flip Condition A specifically, not all of a tipping event's driving
force survives to complete the transition; some is dissipated as
internal friction, proportional to whatever divergence remains
unresolved outside the converging pair. Define the residual divergence
at a convergence event as the pairwise divergence not involved in the
convergence itself --- for A1/A2's D\_pm → 0 or D\_om → 0 respectively,
the residual is D\_op, consistently elevated in this regime by
definition --- and a coherence factor:

κ(t) = 1 / (1 + D\_residual(t)), κ ∈ (0,1{]}, with M\_tip(A) = ∫ F\_i(t)
· κ(t) dt.

\textbf{10.5b.9 Outcome Thresholds}

Let M̃\_tip = M\_tip / M\_tip\^{}ref, where M\_tip\^{}ref is each route's
own fitted historical reference scale (A and B routes normalized
separately, per §15.3). This reference scale is subject to the same
causal constraint already applied to S, R, and F's own normalization
(§6.8): retrospective estimation of M\_tip\^{}ref from the full
historical record is permitted for forensic, after-the-fact analysis,
but any live implementation must estimate M\_tip\^{}ref using only
information available at or before the current timepoint t. Using a
reference scale informed by data after t would let a live tracker's
classification of an event depend on outcomes the tracker could not yet
have observed at the time of that event --- the same future-information
leak already guarded against elsewhere, applying here without exception.
Two thresholds, M\_lock \textless{} M\_c (both fitted), are applied to
the normalized accumulators, with each route's own M\_tip computed by
its own mechanism (mass-weighted for B1/B2, coherence-weighted for A):
M\_lock to the push accumulator on every route, and M\_c, through the
launch channel of Definition 10.5b.9.2.4, to reorganization-eligible
routes only (Definition 10.5b.9.2). M\_lock itself is not a fixed
constant for the B route: M\_lock\^{}eff(t) = M\_lock · (1 +
κ\_Mlock·Ψ\_total(t)), κ\_Mlock ≥ 0 fitted, where Ψ\_total(t)
(§10.5b.18.14) is the container's own negentropic-aligned population
strength. A container with a strong negentropic core genuinely extends
its own self-arrest window --- more tipping momentum is required before
self-arrest becomes unreachable --- rather than only improving which
outcome an already-unavoidable event resolves to (that effect is
separate, and already covered by μ\_p\^{}eff's existing
loyalty-conditioning, §10.5b.18.13). Setting κ\_Mlock = 0 recovers the
fixed-threshold form exactly.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  M̃\_tip \textless{} M\_lock: no tipping event completes. The container
  self-arrests and returns to bounded oscillation within §6.9's
  equilibrium region.
\item
  M̃\_tip ≥ M\_lock, with no launch or disintegration first:
  terminal-locked outcome per Definition 10.1, on any route. For a route
  that is not reorganization-eligible (A1, A2 and B1, by Proposition
  10.5b.9.2.1 and Corollary 10.5b.9.2.3), momentum above M\_c does not
  reach reorganization: it deepens the lock (Definition 10.5b.9.2.5).
  Requires an external anchor to quiet residual oscillation before
  recovery can begin.
\item
  A completed launch, M̃\_up·M̃\_spin ≥ M\_c² on an eligible route before
  lock or disintegration (Definition 10.5b.9.2.4): full reorganization
  outcome --- reachable only by B2, and structurally unreachable,
  without external redirection (§10.5b.11a), by any A route or by B1.
  Disintegration first gives fracture (§10.5b.9d.3). S(t) and δR(t)
  reset toward zero; d\_A(t) recovers toward d\_A\^{}max. This outcome
  is an instance of the recovery operator U of §10.8: full
  reorganization is what U looks like when reached via the B2 tipping
  pathway specifically, rather than via gradual recovery absent a
  tipping event.
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{b.9.1-launch-geometry-push-lift-spin-and-load}{%
\subsection{10.5b.9.1 Launch Geometry: Push, Lift, Spin and
Load}\label{b.9.1-launch-geometry-push-lift-spin-and-load}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.9.1 (Launch Geometry: Push, Lift, Spin
and Load).}}

A knock does different things to a container depending on how its forces
combine. For a container j under impulses J\_k from containers k in
K\_j(t), applied at points r\_k relative to j\textquotesingle s center
of mass, take four quantities over the tipping window: the total load ΣJ
= Σ\_k ‖J\_k‖ (§8.8a.3b\textquotesingle s L\_B, integrated over the
window); the net push J\_net = ‖Σ\_k J\_k‖ (§8.8a.3b\textquotesingle s
N\_B, integrated); the net lift J\_↑, the upward component of Σ\_k J\_k
(zero if that component points down); and the net spin L\_J = ‖Σ\_k r\_k
× J\_k‖, the angular impulse about the center of mass. Where ΣJ
\textgreater{} 0, define the push, lift and spin shares

p = J\_net / ΣJ, υ = J\_↑ / ΣJ, σ\_s = L\_J / (R\_B·ΣJ),

each in {[}0, 1{]}, with R\_B the container\textquotesingle s structural
scale (its radius, in the model below); υ ≤ p, since lift is a component
of push. Where ΣJ = 0 there is no knock and the shares are undefined
(the no-knock case is Definition 10.5b.9.2.4\textquotesingle s
self-arrest).

\textbf{Physical model.} Each container is a rigid hemispherical bowl, a
thin shell of radius R\_B and mass m\_B, on a level surface; g\_grav is
gravitational acceleration. Its center of mass lies on its axis at
R\_B/2 below its center of curvature, and its moment of inertia about a
horizontal axis through the center of mass is I\_B = (2/3 −
1/4)·m\_B·R\_B² = (5/12)·m\_B·R\_B², so its radius of gyration is k\_B =
√(5/12)·R\_B ≈ 0.65 R\_B. The results below are exact within this model.
The model is a physical analogy; its mapping to containers (impulse and
energy to tipping momentum, spacing to coupling distance) is
model-derived.

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.9.1.1 (When rocking containers touch).} A bowl
rocking by angle θ rolls on its curved base, so its center moves
sideways by R\_B·θ and its leading rim reaches R\_B·(θ + cos θ) from its
starting center. Two bowls with rim gap g at rest, rocking toward each
other, touch when θ + cos θ − 1 = g/(2R). For g/(2R) between about 0.39
and 0.46 the contact angle lies between 30° and 40°; this range is the
result for that spacing, not a general property of rocking containers,
and closer bowls touch at smaller angles. ∎

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.9.1.2 (Rocking alone never tips a container).}
Rolling keeps the center of curvature at height R\_B, so at tilt θ the
center of mass is at height R\_B − (R\_B/2)·cos θ, rising with θ up to
90°: the bowl is self-righting at every angle short of its rim reaching
the surface. Rocking energy E carries it to amplitude θ₀ with E =
m\_B·g\_grav·(R\_B/2)·(1 − cos θ₀). Whether neighbors touch therefore
depends on two independent quantities, how hard they rock (E:
oscillation intensity) and how far apart they are (g/R\_B: coupling
distance). ∎

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.9.1.3 (The 180° flip).} To turn upside down,
the bowl must roll onto its rim and pivot over it. Pivoting on the rim,
its center of mass is at distance (√5/2)·R\_B from the pivot, so it must
rise from R\_B/2 to (√5/2)·R\_B, and the energy needed is

E\_180 = ((√5 − 1)/2) · m\_B·g\_grav·R\_B ≈ 0.62 m\_B·g\_grav·R\_B.

Upside down, resting on its rim, the bowl is again stable, with its
center of mass at R\_B/2. ∎

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.9.1.4 (The 360° launch needs lift and spin
together).} Take v the upward speed of the bowl\textquotesingle s center
of mass at launch, Ω its spin rate about a horizontal axis through the
center of mass, and I\_B = m\_B·k\_B² its moment of inertia about that
axis, k² = (5/12)·R\_B² (as above). To rotate fully while airborne, the
bowl must fit one full turn into its time aloft, 2v/g\_grav, so v·Ω ≥
π·g\_grav. A knock delivering lift J\_↑ and spin L\_J gives v =
J\_↑/m\_B and Ω = L\_J/I\_B, so the condition is

J\_↑ · L\_J ≥ π·g\_grav·m\_B·I\_B = π·g\_grav·m\_B²·k\_B².

Lift and spin enter as a product: neither alone can launch the
container, however large. Counting only the vertical translational and
rotational kinetic energy, ½·m\_B·v² + ½·I\_B·Ω² ≥ √(m\_B·I\_B)·v·Ω,
with equality when the two are equal, so the least kinetic energy
meeting the condition is E\_360 = π·m\_B·g\_grav·k\_B =
π·√(5/12)·m\_B·g\_grav·R\_B ≈ 2.03 m\_B·g\_grav·R\_B, about 3.3 times
E\_180. This is a minimum for this launch geometry only: horizontal
motion, or any other energy the launch must also carry, adds to it. ∎

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.9.1.5 (Pivoting cannot supply the lift).} A
bowl spun up onto its rim and pivoting there at rate Ω leaves the
surface where the centripetal requirement exceeds
gravity\textquotesingle s pull toward the pivot, Ω²·ρ ≥ g\_grav·cos
θ\_c, with ρ = (√5/2)·R\_B and θ\_c the angle of the center of mass from
vertical over the pivot. Its upward speed at liftoff is v = Ω·ρ·sin
θ\_c, so v·Ω = g\_grav·cos θ\_c·sin θ\_c ≤ g\_grav/2, far short of
π·g\_grav. Lift for a full turn must therefore come from the knocks
themselves. ∎

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.9.1.6 (What opposed knocks do).} Two equal
horizontal impulses of size J striking the bowl\textquotesingle s rim
from opposite sides while it is tilted by θ act at a height difference
of 2R·sin θ, since the rim on the side it leans toward is lower and the
other higher. Their pushes cancel and their spins add: p = 0, υ = 0,
σ\_s = sin θ. On a level bowl (θ = 0), push, lift and spin all cancel. A
single impulse of size J at height y above the center of mass gives p =
1, υ = 0 and σ\_s = y/R\_B. ∎

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.9.1.7 (Where lift comes from).} A knocker
leaning toward its target by θ\_K lowers its leading rim, whose lowest
point is at height R\_B·(1 − sin θ\_K). A target leaning away from that
knocker raises its near rim, so the knocker\textquotesingle s rim meets
the target not rim to rim but on the target\textquotesingle s outer
wall, below its rim. That wall is part of a sphere of radius R\_B about
the target\textquotesingle s center of curvature, so a frictionless
impulse there acts along the inward normal, toward that center, which
sits at height R\_B; at a contact point at height z\_c the
impulse\textquotesingle s upward share is

υ\_k = (R\_B − z\_c) / R\_B ≤ sin θ\_K,

the bound because the contact point is on the knocker\textquotesingle s
rim, no lower than its lowest point. The contact point itself is set by
the two bowls\textquotesingle{} positions and tilts, θ\_K and the
target\textquotesingle s tilt θ\_T, and is the point of the
knocker\textquotesingle s rim circle that first meets the
target\textquotesingle s sphere; the bound is reached when it is the
rim\textquotesingle s lowest point. A knocker leaning 30° to 40° can
therefore lift with up to about half to two thirds of its impulse.
Because the impulse passes through the center of curvature, which lies
R\_B/2 above the target\textquotesingle s center of mass along its axis,
it also spins the target, with angular impulse (R\_B/2)·J·cos(θ\_T +
θ\_K) when the contact is at the bound, turning it further in the
direction it already leans. The exception: a target that is itself
careening and lower than the knocker at the moment of contact meets the
knocker\textquotesingle s rim above its own center of curvature, and is
struck from above, with a downward share instead. ∎ {[}Exact within the
bowl model, for frictionless contact on the outer wall; the exact
contact point for given positions and tilts is a computation this
proposition specifies but does not carry out.{]}

A full launch therefore needs knocks from more than one side: a knock
from the side the target leans away from supplies lift (this
proposition), while a knock from the side it leans toward strikes its
lowered rim and supplies most of the spin (Proposition 10.5b.9.1.6).
Lift and spin together, as Proposition 10.5b.9.1.4 requires, come from
lateral knocks from different directions, which is the medium-entropy
launch. A single knock cannot supply both in the amount a full turn
needs; whether a given set of knocks does is computed from their tilts
and sizes by Propositions 10.5b.9.1.4, 10.5b.9.1.6 and 10.5b.9.1.7.

These results give a launch four regimes. Push-dominant (p high, υ and
σ\_s low): the container is driven over its rim into the 180° flip,
terminal lock, if the energy exceeds E\_180. Lift and spin together (υ
and σ\_s both high, J\_↑·L\_J large enough): the only launch that can
carry a full turn, Proposition 10.5b.9.1.4. Spin without lift: the
container turns in place and settles. Neither push, lift nor spin: the
knocks resolve into no rigid-body motion at all. A rigid bowl cannot
break, so the model says nothing directly about fracture; in RICD, load
is taken up by the Catalog\textquotesingle s chaos-pressure law
(Definition 10.5b.9d.1), and what the model shows is that when the
forces cancel, that accumulation is all that remains (§10.5b.9d.3). The
30--40° of Proposition 10.5b.9.1.1 enters as the tilt at impact, which
sets the spin share, sin θ ≈ 0.5 to 0.64 over that range. As an initial
calibration, Propositions 10.5b.9.1.3 and 10.5b.9.1.4 suggest M\_c /
M\_lock ≈ 3.3; this mapping from energy to the momentum thresholds is a
calibration hypothesis, not a consequence of the energy calculation.

Let 𝟙\_host(j,t) = 1 if the tipping event was launched by the decoupling
of a container j was extracting from, and 0 otherwise.

\hypertarget{b.9.2-reorganization-eligibility}{%
\subsection{10.5b.9.2 Reorganization
Eligibility}\label{b.9.2-reorganization-eligibility}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.9.2 (Reorganization Eligibility).}}

For a tipping route r with converging pair π\_r (two of the three
layers), define the pair\textquotesingle s truth share, the share of its
mass carrying the original values:

τ\_r = {[} μ\_\{c\_V\}·𝟙(c\_V ∈ π\_r) + ω\_L·μ\_m·𝟙(m ∈ π\_r) {]} /
Σ\_\{x ∈ π\_r\} μ\_x,

with c\_V the carrier layer (§10.5b.1b.1) and ω\_L the meta
layer\textquotesingle s composition at launch (§10.5b.1b.6). Only the
truth-aligned part of the meta layer counts toward the
pair\textquotesingle s truth share. Route r is reorganization-eligible,
E\_r = 1, only if all of the following hold, and E\_r = 0 otherwise:

(L1) the launch carries lift and spin together, υ ≥ υ₁ and σ\_s ≥ σ\_s1
(Proposition 10.5b.9.1.4);

(L2) the launch carries no downward pull from a departing host, 𝟙\_host
= 0;

(L3) the converging pair is predominantly true, τ\_r \textgreater{} ½.

\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.9.2.1 (B1 cannot reorganize; B2 can).} In the
medium-entropy regime the carrier is the operational layer. (i)
B2\textquotesingle s pair is the operational and meta layers, and B2 is
selected only when ω\_L \textgreater{} ω* \textgreater{} ½
(§10.5b.1b.6), so τ\_B2 = (μ\_p + ω\_L·μ\_m) / (μ\_p + μ\_m)
\textgreater{} ½: (L3) holds. B2 is eligible, and so can reorganize,
when its launch also satisfies (L1) and (L2); a B2 event that does not
is not eligible. (ii) B1\textquotesingle s pair is the official and meta
layers, without the carrier, and B1 is selected only when ω\_L
\textless{} ω*, so τ\_B1 = ω\_L·μ\_m / (μ\_o + μ\_m) \textless{} ω*·μ\_m
/ (μ\_o + μ\_m), which is below ½ provided ε\_ω \textless{} μ\_o /
(2μ\_m). Under that condition (L3) fails and E\_B1 = 0 at any momentum.
∎ {[}Structural.{]}

\textbf{Hypothesis 10.5b.9.2.2 (High-entropy launch geometry).} A Flip
Condition A launch is push-dominant, lacking the lift and spin of (L1),
